INTRODUCTION 


The present volume which deals with the family of Stuteville 
is issued to subscribers to the Record Series for the year 1950. 

The various branches of the family, which took its name 
from Etoutteville-sur-Mer in the arrondissement of Yvetot, dept. 
Seine-Inférieure, present several intricate problems. In the 
first place it is desirable, and not always easy, to distinguish be- 
tween what may be called conveniently the Norman line, whose 
tenure of Valmont, lying about thirteen miles from the place of 
origin, passed in male succession for many generations, and the 
English line from whose senior branch were descended the Wakes 
of Cottingham, Kirkby Moorside and Liddel. Confusion between 
members of the two lines is accentuated by the fact that the 
christian names Robert and Nicholas were used extensively in 
both. Thus, in the second half of the twelfth century a Robert 
was the head of each line ; a Nicholas with a son Nicholas occurs 
in each line at the beginning of the thirteenth ; and as a climax 
of complexity fifty years later a Robert in each line had a wife 
named Joan. It is only by an attempt to use the evidence of the 
descent of land that a distinction between members of the two 
lines can be preserved. 

There is reasonably good proof that the two lines were 
closely related, and that both descended from Robert de Stute- 
ville I, who lost his possessions as a result of the battle of Tinche- 
_braiin 1106. To him had been given a large portion of the Domes- 
day holdings in Yorkshire of Hugh son of Baldric ; and it happens 
that two holdings of Hugh in Hampshire passed to the Norman 
line. An attempt is made in Chapter II to throw further light 
on Hugh son of Baldric, who was certainly of Norman origin ; 
and, although there is an absence of proof to establish his parentage, 
his Domesday holdings in England and the course of their sub- 
sequent history suggest the deduction that both lines of the 
Stuteville family descended from Robert I. 

In order to illustrate the distinction between the two lines 
it has been thought desirable to trace the descent of the Norman 
line down to the time when it lost its English holdings. These 
were acquired in at least three different ways: first, through 
~the possession of lands in Hampshire which had been held by 
Hugh son of Baldric in 1086, and which had passed in all pro- 
bability to Robert de Stuteville I ; secondly, through the marriage 
with Leonia daughter of Edward of Salisbury and Adeliz de 
Rames, which brought not only the honour of Rames in Nor- 
mandy, together with an under-tenancy of the important manor 
of Dedham in Essex, but also a moiety of the barony of 30 knights’ 
fees, mainly in Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire, an inheritance 
from the Domesday tenant-in-chief Ralph son of Hubert ; and 
thirdly, through the marriage with Eleanor de Genoure, which 
brought the extensive castlery of Mitford in Northumberland. 


Vv 


vl STUTEVILLE FEE 


Details of all these holdings are given in §7 of Chapter I. It will 
be seen that although in 1204 the head of the line, Henry, Leonia’s 
son and heir, who married one of earl Hamelin’s daughters, 
adhered to Philip Augustus, he had a temporary restoration of 
his English lands early in the reign of Henry IIi ; and that before 
his death they were confirmed to his son John. It was the latter’s 
son Robert, enjoying the king of England’s favour, who acquired 
the castlery of Mitford by marriage. So, except when the two 
countries were actually at war, the lords of Valmont retained 
their English possessions. There are frequent references to the 
grant of licences to go overseas from Northumberland in the 
period 1282 to 1292, which, we may suppose, were for the purpose 
of visiting Valmont. Although on the outbreak of war in 1294 
Robert adhered to the king of France, he recovered his Euglish 
lands after the peace in 1303. At his death three years later a 
son by his first wife inherited Valmont, and by virtue of a settle- 
ment his English lands passed to his son by his second wife, 
Eleanor de Genoure. Both this son and the latter’s son and 
heir were born overseas; and eventually, though not before 
1337, the English inheritance was finally forfeited. The family 
thus affords an unusual example of the tenure of lands on both 
sides of the Channel to so late a date. 


Of this Norman line there were several younger branches. 
One of these was established at Stratfield in Hampshire, which 
acquired temporarily the name of Stratfield Stuteville, and which, 
passing by marriage to the family of Say, became known in its 
modern form of Stratfield Saye. Another acquired an interest in 
Kimberley in Norfolk by marriage with a daughter of the family 
of Gournay. A third held two of Leonia’s manors as an under- 
tenancy of the eldest branch, and, although of alien status in 
three generations, retained them until their sale in 1332; and a 
younger son of the eldest branch held, also as an under-tenancy, 
the manor of Eckington in Derbyshire which was the “native 
village’ whose tales have been recorded by Sir George Sitwell. 


In Normandy the family descended in the male line until 
the sixteenth century, when the duchy d’ Estouteville was created 
in favour of the heiress and her husband, a member of the royal 
house of France. It had spread into several branches, of which 
a detailed account is given by Pére Anselme in his Histoire 
généalogique ...de France. A history of the family was published 
in Paris about fifty years ago by M. G. de la Morandiére, to 
which a reference is given in the text of the present volume in a 
noc on pi 4h 


To print the texts of charters relating to the English 
possessions of the Norman line is outside our present purpose. 
But several have been used as documentary evidence for state- 
ments in the narrative account. In particular, an important 
collection of original charters is contained in the muniments of 
Eton College. They refer to the hermitage at Stratfield which 


INTRODUCTION Vill 


was the subject of a gift to the abbey of Valmont. A calendar in 
abstract form was published in the ninth report of the Historical 
Manuscripts Commission ; and a complete series of transcripts 
has been made recently by Mr. Noel Blakiston, who has kindly 
allowed me to inspect it. A printed edition, with if possible some 
collotype illustrations, would be of special interest. 


The English line of the Stutevilles, with which the present 
volume is primarily concerned, and its several branches, descended 
from Robert de Stuteville III and some of his younger brothers. 
As the eldest branch died out in the male line before the middle 
of the thirteenth century, the family finds no place in The Complete 
Peerage ; but John Wake, grandson of the heiress Joan de Stute- 
ville, was summonded to Parliament in 1295, and the barony of 
Wake eventually fell into abeyance in 1408 on the death of the 
younger grandson and heir of the Fair Maid of Kent.” 


In view, however, of the baronial status of the family, a 
detailed account of the Stutevilles, of great value by reason of its 
documentary references, was included by Dugdale in his Baronage.8 
He was clearly of the opinion that the Robert who fought at the 
battle of the Standard in 1138 and the Robert who lived to at least 
as late as 1176 were one and the same person, though he gives 
him as Robert II (who was fighting in 1106) instead of Robert ITI. 
Certain amendments to Dugdale’s pedigree must assuredly be made. 
Thus, Sibyl de Valognes was not the second wife of Robert III, 
but the wife of his grandson Robert son of Nicholas I. Nicholas I 
- was undoubtedly the brother and not a younger son of William the 
justice ; and a distinction must be made between the Osmund 
who was the ancestor of the Stutevilles of Weston Colville and 
Burton Agnes, and the Osmund who was the father of William 
de Stuteville of Cowesby and Gressenhall. 


The article on Robert de Stuteville (2.e. Robert de Stuteville 
III) and his son William in The Dictionary of National Biography 
(1898) follows Dugdale in the main, so far as the genealogy is 
concerned. But the author stated, evidently following Sir Harris 
Nicolas,* that ““Dugdale makes one person of the Robert Stute- 
ville who fought at the battle of the Standard and the justiciar, 
but in this he was no doubt in error.”’ This is a crucial matter, 
and the evidence given in the present volume suggests a restora- 
tion of Dugdale’s opinion, with the proviso already mentioned. 
The statement, not derived from Dugdale, that Sir William de 
Skipwith descended from a collateral branch of the family is at 
least open to doubt ;§ nor was Robert de Stuteville III the founder 
of Rosedale priory.® 

1 See pp. 47-8 in Chapter I. 

2 Complete Peerage, \st ed., viii, 36; J. W. Clay, Extinct and Dormant 
Peevages of the Northern Counties, p. 230. 

3 Vol. i, pp. 455-9 ; chart ped. on p. 456. 

* Historic Peerage, p. 457. 

5 See Chapter IV, §2. 

6 See zbid., §19. That the priory was founded by Robert son of Nicholas 
I is equally untrue.

A considerable improvement in several points can be seen 
in the pedigree which Colonel Parker included in his paper on 
‘The Redmans of Yorkshire’ in 1911.1 But he retained the theory 
of the Skipwith descent by making a certain Patrick de Skipwith 
a younger son of Robert de Stuteville II ; and the statement that 
Robert de Stuteville of Cowesby and Gressenhall had in marriage 
the manor of Hegginton |7.e. Eckington], co. Derby, and Dedham, 
co. Essex, is an example of the confusion with a member of the 
Norman line. The evidence, now available, suggests that Burga 
de Stuteville who married William de Vescy was a daughter and 
not a sister of Robert de Stuteville III ; and it is very unlikely 
that Osmund de Stuteville, who married the Gressenhall heiress, 
could have been the latter’s brother. 

But if amendments can be made with some degree of con- 
fidence to earlier accounts of the family there are many details 
which are still obscure. No satisfactory clue is available for the 
parentage of Erneburga wife of Robert de Stuteville II. On the 
one hand, it has been stated that this elusive lady, the second 
half of whose christian name was given to several of her descend- 
ants, was a daughter of Hugh son of Baldric ; and on the other, 
it was suggested by Farrer that she was a daughter of Geoffrey. 
Murdac. Neither of these theories can be accepted. It is to be 
hoped that evidence may be found, perhaps from a Norman 
source, which may provide a satisfactory clue; for the guess 
which is made on p. 3”, having in its favour the not unattractive 
idea that it was through her that the caput of the family’s Norman 
possessions was changed from Etoutteville-sur-Mer to Valmont, 
has not the slightest documentary support. 

Another point which must be left unproved, though the 
evidence makes it reasonably probable, is whether William de 
Stuteville of Cowesby and Gressenhall was the same William who 
through his wife Margery de Say held the honour of. Richard’s 
Castle in the thirteenth century. Again, it is a matter of doubt 
whether the former’s son Robert, whose wife was named Joan, 
was the husband of Joan Talbot rather than the Robert, head 
of the Norman line, whose first wife had the same christian name ; 
the latter alternative has been tentatively adopted (p. 62m). 


Several points of interest arise in the descent of younger 
-branches of the English line. The evidence is practically con- 
clusive that a daughter of John de Stuteville, a younger brother 
of Robert de Stuteville III, whose interests lay mainly in Warwick- 
shire, married Meldred son of Dolfin, the ancestor in the male 
line of the Nevilles of Raby (p. 26). The Pantulf family, closely 
connected with this branch, which has complications of its own 
(p. 27), affords a clue for the reconsideration of the descent of the 
baronial family of Tateshall.2 From the branch which held Weston 
Colville, co. Cambridge, of the honour of Warenne, and Burton 
Agnes of the Brus fee, descends in unbroken succession, through 


1 Yorks. Arch. Journal, xxi, 68. 
2 On this point see Complete Peerage, new ed., xii (i), p. 648. 


LNTRODUCTION 1X 


a series of hereditary steps, the tenure of Burton Agnes (pp. 31-2). 
Although in most of these younger branches the Stutevilles died 
out in the male line before 1300, a branch continued at Cottingham 
to a later date ; and it is to be hoped that evidence will become 
available in the future to prove the actual descent from the main 
line, which at present remains obscure (p. 40). 


§2. 

At the outset of the preparation of this volume a problem 
of some complexity had to be considered. In general terms the 
problem is whether, in cases where a tenant-in-chief (A) returned 
in his carta in 1166 knights’ fees held of him by B, and B was 
himself a tenant-in-chief returning his own carta, charters re- 
lating to the lands composing those knights’ fees should be in- 
cluded in a volume primarily concerned with the honour of A or 
in one primarily concerned with the honour of B.t For the present 
purpose preference has been given to the latter alternative ; and 
therefore charters relating to lands in Yorkshire which formed 
part of the knights’ fees held in 1166 by Robert de Stuteville III 
of Roger de Mowbray have been included in a separate chapter. 
A note of these is given in the introduction to that chapter (p. 
200). The charters include several relating to Welburn and the 
lost vill of Hoveton nearby ; and, in describing the bounds of 
those vills in great detail, they give a large number of place- 
names, some of which can be identified from modern maps, and 
many more of which could perhaps be identified in the light of 
an intimate knowledge of the locality. 


§3. 


Original charters issued by members of the English line of 
the Stutevilles in the twelfth century, or by their tenants, are 
difficult to discover. No charter of Robert de Stuteville III has 
been found to be available. The eight collotype plates, of which 
a list is given at the end of the Table of Contents, include a charter 
of the heiress Joan de Stuteville, issued in the period 1266-74, 
to which an interesting seal was attached, showing her seated on 
a side-saddle with the Stuteville arms on a shield carried in her 
left hand. 

Apart from these, considerable use has been made of Dods- 
worth’s transcripts of the original charters housed in St. Mary’s 
Tower at York after the Dissolution. Dr. Burton’s brief calendar 
of the originals shows that the total which survived, and which 
were then in his possession, was 1868.2 These originals were at 
Burton Constable about the year 1840; but it is feared that

almost all of them are no longer in existence. About 1825 in 
number were transcribed by Dodsworth, though with no attempt 
at arrangement, in his MSS. vii and viii for a ‘Monasticon Boreale’. 
In the present volume the texts of 25 charters are printed from 
his vol. vii; and these bring the total number of texts from his 
vols. vii and viii, printed in the series of Early Yorkshire Charters 
to about 274.2, Several more were transcribed for Farrer for 
other honours which have not yet been undertaken, including 
Mowbray, Percy, Ros, Tickhill and Vescy. 


Another of the Dodsworth volumes which has been of 
special use is his vol. lxii. This contains his copy of the Byland 
Chartulary.2 The original chartulary (Egerton MS. 2823) is defec- 
tive ; for all the folios earlier than f. 74, and several subsequent 
folios are missing. A great part of the earliest portion, consisting 
of abbot Philip’s Foundation History, compiled in 1197, is printed 
in the Monasticon ;4 but the charters interspersed in it were 
omitted. Of these Farrer printed three,> and one or two more 
from Dodsworth’s texts which are now missing from the chartulary. 
Six charters from this volume of Dodsworth are printed in the 
present volume. 


A large number of charters, especially relating to places in. 
the neighbourhood of Kirkby Moorside, has been taken from the 
Rievaulx Chartulary (Cotton MS. Julius D. 1). As Farrer pointed 
out® the texts in the printed edition are often robbed of important 
passages, regarded as of common form ; and the texts now printed 
have been completed from, and compared with the manuscript. 
An important amendment of the date of one of the charters 
given in the printed edition has had to be made (no. 91). 


A fragment of a chartulary of the priory of North Ferriby, 
now in the Bodleian Library, for the reference to which I am 
indebted to the Rev. J. C. Dickinson, contains a charter which 
has a special interest of its own. John de Hessle gave to the

priory land which was confirmed by Robert de Stuteville III 
(no. 18); and one of his charters (note thereto), issued at the 
siege of Acre, had among its witnesses Roger ‘persona de Houden’. 
It is to be hoped that a full transcript of the fragment may be 
made available in print, with a considered note which may throw 
important light on the career of one of the best-known chroniclers 
of the twelfth century. 


For the sources from which the texts of the charters have 
been taken a list is given in the first of the indexes. They include 
a charter (no. 62, plate IV), which Madox printed, though without 
the full list of witnesses, in his Formulare Anglicanum from the 
original in the Augmentations Office. This is numbered 408 in 
his book ; and this number has been preserved in the present 
reference-number of the original now in the Public Record 
Office. 


An appendix at the end of the volume contains some notes 
on the early priors of Newburgh. Notes on the early abbots of 
the two Cistercian houses of Byland and Rievaulx, of whom 
there are several mentions in this volume, will be found in a 
recently issued paper by the present writer in the Yorkshire 
Archaeological Journal. The evidence which is given here on 
pp. 93 and 197 suggests that the usually accepted dates of founda- 
tion of the priories of Keldholme and Rosedale are subject to 
revision. 


My acknowledgements are due to the Controller of Her 
Majesty’s Stationery Office for permission to print the texts of 
four charters from the printed edition of the Curia Regis Rolls, 
now in progress, of several from the printed editions of the Charter 
Rolls, and of passages from volumes in the Rolls Series ; to the 
Lincoln Record Society for permission to print three texts from 
the Registrum Antiquissimum ; and to the Dean and Chapter of 
York, the Dean and Chapter of Durham, and the Librarian of 
Nottingham University for permission to print texts from originals 
or manuscripts in their possession or custody. 


It is a pleasure to record my thanks to the Rev. Chancellor 
Harrison and Miss Brunskill, and to Mr. F. Taylor, Keeper of 
Manuscripts in the John Rylands Library, for their kindness in 
checking several points in Farrer’s transcripts made from manu- 
scripts in their custody; and for help in a large and varied 
number of ways to Sir Frank and Lady Stenton, Miss Kathleen 
Major, Miss Dorothy Whitelock, Professor David Douglas, Pro- 
fessor J. G. Edwards, Mr. Christopher Brooke, Mr. R. W. Hunt, 
Mr. R. H. Whiteing, Sir Edmund Craster, Sir Cyril Flower, and 
Mr. R. E. Latham and Mr: N. J. Williams of the Public Record 
Office. Mr. G. Ellis Flack, Librarian of Nottingham University, 
and Mr. J. Conway Davies have kindly given me special facilities 
for the provision éf photographs of charters at Nottingham and 
Durham respectively.

ADDENDUM 


To the account of Hugh son of Baldric given in Chapter II 
may be added the reference that a Hugh ‘filius Baldici’ (indexed 
as Hugh ‘fils de Baudry’) witnessed a confirmation charter of 
Robert Curthose, duke of Normandy, to the church of Bayeux on 
24 April 1089 (Ant. Cart. Eccl. Baiocensis, Soc. Hist. de Normandie, 
i, 8), “If the witness is the Domesday tenant, as is likely,.che 
reference shows that the antecessor of Robert de Stuteville I in 
England was in the entourage of duke Robert; and it may be that 
on that account his English lands became the subject of forfeiture, 
the possibility of which is noted on p. 72; but it must not be re- 
garded as clear that he was not still in possession of them when 
the Conqueror died.

CHAPTER I. 


THE STUTEVILLE FAMILIES 
<i~< LHE DESCENT -OF TH MAIN ENGLISH VINE 


ROBERT DE STUTEVILLE I is the earliest recorded member 
of the family of Stuteville or Estouteville,| which derived its 
name from Etoutteville-sur-Mer.2~ He witnessed a charter for 
the abbey of St-Evroul not later than 1089.2 In England he 
was granted the main portion of the lands in Yorkshire of which 
Hugh son of Baldric had been tenant-in-chief at the Domesday 
survey,’ together with a holding in Lincoln previously held by 
Hugh son of Baldric,> and probably also holdings in Stratfield 
Saye and Church Oakley, Hampshire, of which Hugh son of 
Baldric had been tenant-in-chief at the Survey. In the account 
of the settlement in 1201 of the claim by his great-grandson 
William de Stuteville against William de Mowbray,’ he is des- 
cribed as Robert Grundebeof. 

He was a benefactor of St. Mary’s abbey, York, making 
gifts of land or tithes in Moreby, par. Stillingfleet ; Ellenthorpe, 
par. Aldborough ; Harton, par. Bossall ; Coxwold ; Hovingham ; 
Kirkby Moorside ; Buttercrambe ; Scrayingham ; and Langton ; 
and, with Robert de Meinil, in Myton-upon-Swale.® He gave 
land in Hunsley, par. Rowley, to Durham priory ;? and to the 
church of Lincoln houses in the city. 

In 1105 ,he supported duke Robert in Normandy, being 
responsible for the duke’s troops and fortresses in the Pays de 
Caux." After his capture at the battle of Tinchebrai in 1106 he 
was condemned to imprisonment for life, and a large portion of 
his English lands was subsequently granted by Henry I to Nigel 
d’Aubigny, from whom they descended to Roger de Mowbray.!” 

1 Although the Latin forms Stutevilla, Stotevilla and Estotevilla are 
used indiscriminately, it is convenient for purposes of distinction to restrict 
the form Estouteville to the Norman line descended from Nicholas d’Estoute- 
ville, lord of Valmont (see §7 below). 

4 Dept. Seine-Iniérieure, arr. Yvetot, cant, Yerville. Although ‘so 
described it lies inland. There are earthworks in the ‘bois aux mottes’ 
which seem to be the site of the original family castle (L. C. Loyd, Anglo- 
Norman Families, Harleian Soc., p. 40). It is not known if Valmont, 
c. 13 miles to the n.w. also in arr. Yvetot, which later became the caput of 
the barony in Normandy, was held by Robert de Stuteville I, or how it 
was acquired, whether by marriage or otherwise. The account of the 
family in Pére Anselme, Histoive généalogique ... de France, viii, 88, gives 
Robert de Stuteville II as lord of Valmont, but without documentary evi- 
dence. 3 Ovdericus Vitalis, ed. Le Prévost, ii, 399. 

* For the details see Chapter II. > Charter dio. 2. 

® Subsequently held by the Norman line; see §7 below. 

7 Charters nos. 42-4. § Charter no: 1 and note. SEOLG.

11 Orderic, iv, 214 ; ‘vir fortis et potens’. 

12 Tbid., pp. 226, 231, 234 ; Chron. Mon. de Hyda in Lib. Mon. de Hyda,

no. 42 below). 


2 STUTEVILLE.FEE 


Besides his son Robert. he had a daughter Emma who 
married Robert, eldest son of Hugh de Grentemaisnil, as the 
second of his three wives ; Robert de Grentemaisnil died 1 June 
1136, and he and Emma were buried in the chapter-house of the 
abbey of St-Evroul.1 In the Liber Vitae of Durham? there are 
commemorated Robert de Stuteuill’, Beatrix his wife, and Robert, 
Graulfus and William their sons. In view of his benefaction to 
Durham priory, it is likely that the entry refers to Robert de 
Stuteville I.2 If so, in addition to his eldest son he had two 
younger sons Graulfus and William by a wife Beatrix. 

Robert de Stuteville I was probably closely related to, and 
possibly the brother of Geoffrey de Stuteville, living towards the 
end of the eleventh century, who held interests in the Isle of 
Axholme and in Hampton-in-Arden, co. Warwick, and Melton 
Mowbray, co. Leicester, and was a benefactor of St. Mary’s abbey, 
York, and Lewes priory.* 


ROBERT DE STUTEVILLE II, son of Robert de Stuteville I.5 
He supported duke Robert against Henry I, and was captured at 
St-Pierre-sur-Dive shortly before the battle of Tinchebrai.6 The 
chronological details relating to his sons show that he must have 
lived for some years subsequently ; but it is unlikely that he ever 
possessed lands in England. He was evidently dead when his 
son Robert recovered part of the inheritance in Yorkshire.’ 

Documentary evidence is available to prove that Robert 
de Stuteville II had a wife named Erneburga, by whom he was 
the father of Robert de Stuteville III. There is no evidence, 
however, to prove her parentage. Dugdale in his Baronage® made 
no suggestion. Banks asserted that she was daughter and heir to 
Hugh son of Baldric whom he described erroneously as ‘a great 
saxon thane’.® This cannot be accepted. On the one hand the 
fact that Robert de Stuteville I held a large proportion of the 
Domesday tenures of Hugh son of Baldric disproves any sug- 
gestion that his son should have become possessed of them by

f. 44V); and again a Gilbert Halsart and Joan de Stuteuilla on f. 23V (p. 19).

marriage with a daughter of Hugh; and on the other hand, 
although Hugh son of Baldric had a daughter who married Guy 
de Craon, the Craon family appear to have inherited none of 
Hugh’s. property except certain holdings in Lincolnshire and 
Hampshire, which were held by Guy of Hugh at the Domesday 
survey after his marriage had taken place,! thus suggesting that

Another suggestion was made by Farrer that Erneburga 
was a daughter of Geoffrey Murdac and sister of Juliana who 
married Thurstan de Montfort ; but that again cannot be sup-

© It will be suggested in the account of the Estoutevilles of 
Valmont in §7 that Nicholas d’Estouteville, the founder of the 
abbey of Valmont, was the eldest son of Robert de Stuteville IT, 
and that, although the latter may have had an earlier wife, 
there is no reason to assume that Nicholas was not his son by 
his wife Erneburga. 

In addition to this son Nicholas (if the above suggestion 
can be accepted) Robert de Stuteville II’s issue was as follows : 

(1) Robert de Stuteville Ill ; see below. 

(2) William de Stuteville. He occurs as brother of Robert 
de Stuteville [IIT] in a claim made by them in 1147 against Roger 
de Mowbray for land in the territory of Coxwold which Roger 
had given to Byland abbey ;* and he is probably the witness of 
that name to a charter of Roger de Mowbray to Newburgh priory, 
1145-c.1160.5 There is reason for supposing that he can be identi- 
fied with the William de Stuteville who with the consent of 
Emma his wife gave land in Murton to Rievaulx abbey.® 

(3) Roger de Stuteville. His place in the pedigree is proved 
by the witness clause in a charter of Henry II to the weavers of 
the city of York, issued at Pickering in 1163, which contains the 
names of Robert de Stuteville [III] and Roger his brother.? He 
was sheriff of Northumberland from Easter 1170 to Easter 1185.8 
He was castellan of Wark on Tweed at the time of the invasion 
of Northumberland in 1173 by the king of Scotland, with whom 
he made a truce, and whom in the following year he repulsed 
from Wark.® In May 1177 he was given the custody of the castle

In 1183 the bishop of Winchester owed a norris hawk in 
Hampshire on behalf of Roger son of John de Neweton, the man 
of Roger de Stuteville, which he promised to the king for right 
of the land of Neweton. At Michaelmas 1185 Roger de Stuteville 
owed certain debts in Northumberland in respect of his office as 
sheriff, including 604. for the cornage of the county, on which 
he paid all but 4s., some of the debts being required in Suffolk ;16 
and at Michaelmas 1186 there is a reference to his having de- 
tained stock belonging to the honour of Wark.” He was still 
owing a debt at Michaelmas 1189, which he paid in the following 
year.18 He probably died shortly afterwards.

THE SPUTEVITLE PAMILIES © 


He also had a daughter who married Robert de Daiville as 
his first wife ;} and probably a daughter Burga, who married 
William Pantulf.? 


ROBERT DE STUTEVILLE III, son of Robert de Stuteville II 
by his wife Erneburga.? It is probable that his birth can be 
placed as c. 1110. In 1138 he was among the northern magnates 
at York who supported archbishop Thurstan against the ravages 
of the Scots.*. In the reign of Stephen he was in possession of 
Cottingham, which had evidently been restored to him as one 
of the tenures of his grandfather Robert de Stuteville I ;> and in 
an account of the miracles of St. John of Beverley wrought in the 
same reign he is described as ‘vir strenuus et militari virtute 
probatissimus’, imprisoning at Cottingham a certain clerk, the 
son of a citizen of Lincoln, threatening him with torture until his 
parents ransomed him, but an escape was successfully made with 
the saint’s assistance ; and a similar story concerns another clerk 
imprisoned in the castle there.6 Probably in the period 1138-41 
he issued a penny at the mint of York.’ 

He witnessed a writ of king Stephen at Lincoln, probably 
in 1141 ;§ and charters of archbishop William, 1144-46,9 of arch- 
bishop Henry, 1150-53,!° and of William count of Aumale, 1150-53." 
As early as 1147 he and his brother William had started the 
disputes with Roger de Mowbray with regard to the tenures 
of Kobert de Stuteville I which Henry I had given te Nigel 
d’Aubigny ; and they were then claiming the land in the territory 
of Coxwold which Roger had given to Byland abbey.” 

_ Early in the reign of Henry IT’8 a compromise was made by 
which he was granted by Roger de Mowbray 10 knights’ fees, 
which included the extensive manor of Kirkby Moorside and its 
appurtenances and land in Warwickshire ;* and in Roger de 
Mowbray’s carta of 1166 8 knights’ fees of the old feoffment and 
one fee of the new were held of him by Robert de Stuteville.™

6 STUTEVILLE FEE 


The evidence relating to the priory of Keldholme suggests that he 
founded that house shortly after he obtained Kirkby Moorside." 

In 1166 Robert de Stuteville III, as a tenant-in-chief, made 
a return of 8 knights’ fees of the old feoffment, and an eighth of 
a knight’s fee of the new ;? and in 1168 he paid 106s. 8d. for the 
aid for the marriage of the king’s daughter, owing 20d. for a new 
feoffment,? these sums precisely corresponding with the amounts 
of his holdings on the basis of a mark per knight’s fee. Again 
in 1172 his assessment for the scutage of Ireland was 8. with 
2s. 6d. for a new feoffment, payments which were completed by 
1176.4 He was given land in Cold Kirby by king Henry II ;° 
and he purchased land in Ingleby Greenhow, a member of the 
Balliol fee.® 

In 1168 he was a supervisor of the works of Bamburgh 
castle.?, Reference is made in 1170 to pleas heard before him and 
Hugh de Moreville in Cumberland and Northumberland.’ He 
was sheriff of Yorkshire from Easter 1170 to Michaelmas 1175.° 
In 1173, with his sons and brothers, he supported the king in the 
rebellion. In the following year he had the custody of the castles 
of Brough and Appleby when they were invaded and taken by 
William the Lion, king of Scotland; and he was one of the leaders 
of the Yorkshire army when William was captured at Alnwick." 
In March 1176-7 he was one of those styled barons of England 
who witnessed the Spanish award made by Henry II.” 

He witnessed charters of Henry II to the burgesses of 
Scarborough at York, 1155-63 ; to the weavers of York at 
Pickering in 1163 ;4 to Rievaulx abbey at Winchester, 1164-70 ;%

abbey at Woodstock, not earlier than 1173; to William de 
Stuteville (his eldest son) in 1175 ;38 to Lenton priory at Nottingham 
in 1176;!® in favour of Bardney abbey at Windsor in the same 
year ;° to the lepers at Jerusalem at Clipston, 1178-84 ;?! and to 
Whitby abbey at Winchester, 1179-80.22 It is probable that the

THE STUTEVILLE FAMILIES 7 


Robert de Stuteville who witnessed numerous charters of Henry II 
in Normandy was the lord of Valmont.! 

He also witnessed a charter of William count of Aumale, 
1165-79 ;? as sheriff, charters of Clement abbot of St. Mary’s, 
York, and Geoffrey de Lascelles to Rievaulx abbey, 1170-75 ;3 
and a charter of his own son William, 1173-85.4 

His benefactions to the abbeys of St. Mary’s, York, Byland, 
Rievaulx and Meaux, and the priories of Durham and Keldholme, 
are’ recorded in charters which are printed in Chapter ITI. It is 
stated in the Rotuls de Dominabus that the daughters of Matthew 
de Neville had been in the custody of Hugh de Creissi for a year 
ending at Christmas [1184], and for the preceding eight years in 
that of Robert de Stuteville ; and the deduction can be made 
that Robert died in 1183.5 

Robert de Stuteville III’s wife was named Helewise,® whose 
parentage is not known. As ‘domina Helewis uxor Roberti de 
Stuteuilla’ with Osmund ‘filius ejus’ she witnessed a charter of 
William de Vescy to the brethren on Farne Island, the first 
witness being Philip de Valognes.? 


Robert de Stuteville III had issue : 

(1) William de Stuteville ; see below. 

(2) Nicholas de Stuteville I; see below. 

(3) Osmund de Stuteville ; see the Stutevilles of Cowesby 
and Gressenhall in $4. 

(4) Eustace de Stuteville, who witnessed with his father as 
‘sheriff and his brother Nicholas a charter of Geoffrey de Lascelles 
to Rievaulx abbey.® He is probably the Eustace who with Robert 
his brother witnessed a charter of William de Stuteville to Keld- 
holme priory, 1190-1201. The details given below under Nicholas 
de Stuteville II] show that he held the manor of Brinklow, co. 
Warwick ; and that he had a son Robert, born and brought up 
overseas, to whom Nicholas de Stuteville II was ordered to 
restore land, apparently in Yorkshire, in 1233. It is likely that 
he was the Eustace against whom Thurstan de Montfort brought 
a claim in 1208 (see Appendix A), and who is mentioned in a 
record of 1213 relating to his tenure among other knights holding 
of the honour of Leicester in Warwickshire.4

WILLIAM DE STUTEVILLE, eldest son and heir. The date 
of his birth was probably c. 1140. 

At Easter 1173 he was granted the custody of the manors 
of Knaresborough and Aldborough, and by a charter of Henry IT, 
probably issued in July 1175, these two manors were granted to 
him and his heirs to hold by the service of 3 knights. In 1174 
he had the custody of Topcliffe castle,? and in the same year, 
with his father, was among the leaders of the Yorkshire army

His name frequently occurs as a witness to royal charters.

charters in the early years of his reign. In a reterence to his 
assessment of 16 marks for scutage in 1199 (at 2 marks per fee) 
Roger de Topcliffe is named as his steward.¥ 


There is conclusive evidence that William de Stuteville 
married Berta, a niece of Ranulf de Glanville. In 1205 or 1206 
Ranulf son of Robert gave 200 marks for having a third of 
William de Stuteville’s land in Bramham (braham)'4 and Leyburn 
(Leburn’),® which William took in marriage with Berta his wife 
who died without an heir, which third fell to Ranulf by hereditary 
right ; and, among other things, for having two writs, one against 
Thomas de Arden’ and the other against Hugh de Auberville for 
the reasonable share which fell to him of the free tenement of 
Ranulf de Glanville.16 Also Thomas de Arden’ gave 60 marks 
and a palfrey for having his reasonable share of the land which 
Ranulf de Glanville gave in marriage to Wiliam de Stuteville 
with Berta his niece, of which Thomas ought to have a third.?!”

William de Stuteville’s lands following the death of Hubert

ie STUTEVILLE FEE 


Ardenn’.! At Michaelmas 1207 in the accounts for Knaresborough 
it is recorded that Ranulf son of Robert and Thomas de Ardenn’ 
had completed their payments, and that a sum was received 
from the share? of Hugh de Alberville in Bramham and Leyburn 
(Letbrunn’).2 The three men mentioned were the representatives 
of the three daughters and heirs of Ranulf de Glanville, the first 
being Ranulf son of Robert son of Ralph, lord of Middleham, 
whose father Robert married Helewise, one of Ranulf de Glan- 
ville’s daughters.* 

The statement that Berta died without an heir makes it 
unlikely that she was the mother of Robert, William de Stuteville’s 
son and heir; for, as there is no mention of a widow surviving 
William or of any arrangements for a widow’s dower, she could 
not have survived William ; and she therefore died earlier than | 
Robert. The latest possible date for William’s marriage to Berta 
is 1190, when Ranulf de Glanville died ; and the earliest date for 
Robert’s birth is 1185, as he was under age in 1205. It is possible 
therefore that Berta was William de Stuteville’s first wife, any 
issue by her which would have enabled him to retain lands given 
in frank-marriage having died young, and that he had a second 
wife whose name is not recorded.® In addition to his son Robert 
he had a bastard daughter.®

ROBERT DE STUTEVILLE IV, son and heir. He occurs with 
his father, other witnesses being the rector and constable and all 
the halimote of Knaresborough, as a witness to a charter relating 
to Ripley ;* and also with his father as a witness to a charter 
relating to Castley.?, On 9 July 1203 king John, then at Rouen, 
confirmed to him all his father’s lands, including Knaresborough 
and Boroughbridge and the forest of Westmorland, and also the 
wardships and farms held by his father ;§ and on the same day 
he granted his wardship and marriage to archbishop Hubert for 
4000 marks, to whom on 4 June he had granted the custody of all 
the lands and castles held by William de Stuteville at his death.® 
Robert de Stuteville died before 5 Aug. 1205, apparently still a 
minor.?°

this appears to be the earliest reference for the possession of 
Liddel by a member of the Stuteville family.” By the death of 
Robert son of his elder brother William he became the latter’s 
‘heir. In 1205, not later than 5 August, he made a fine of 10,000 
marks for having his inheritance of all the lands held by William 
de Stuteville his brother, and of the pledges held by William at 
his death and by the late archbishop of Canterbury, with the 
exception of the castle of Knaresborough and Boroughbridge,

The tenure of the Stuteville inheritance in chief which 
passed to Nicholas is shown by his holding of Cottingham for 6 
fees in 1210-12,4 and of land in Kettleby Thorpe and Bigby, co. 
Lincoln,®> which constituted part of one of the knights’ fees held 
of Robert de Stuteville III in 1166. He also inherited the sub- 
ordinate tenure of the honour of Mowbray which had been granted 
to his father.* In 1206 a plea of common of pasture which he had 
against the abbot of St. Mary’s, York, was respited as long as 
his knights were in the king’s service overseas.’ 


With regard to the lands in Cumberland held by Nicholas, 
of which the caput was Liddel, in 1177 a king’s writ was directed 
to him for a pardon of 10 marks in respect of pleas held in that 
county ;8 and in 1201 he paid 100s. for his land held by cornage 
for the fine of the knights not crossing overseas ; his land held 
by cornage rendering 56s. yearly in 1212. In 1200 he was engaged 
in a plea for a carucate of land in ‘Cresop’ or ‘Gersope’ [Kershope, 
n.e. of Liddel] against Amabel daughter of William de Cardoil, 
also known as Amabel daughter of William ‘medicus’, another 
plaintiff in the following year being Bartholomew de Stuteville,

portion of the barony of Liddel, now a parish extending along

Nicholas de Stuteville I married Gunnora sister and coheir 
of Ralph d’Aubigny,? and widow of Robert de Gant, who died 
in 1191, and by whom she was the mother of Gilbert de Gant.’ 
On 25 April 1197 Nicholas and Gunnora, together with one of 
her sisters and her husband and the widower of her eldest sister, 
were plaintiffs in a final concord by which William d’Aubigny III] 
(their first cousin) recognized their right to the lands of Ralph 
d’Aubigny, namely a fee of 15 knights in Aubourn, co. Lincoln, 
and elsewhere in Lincolnshire and Yorkshire.* Chronological 
considerations show that Gunnora must have been Nicholas de 
Stuteville’s second wife. The name of his first wife, the mother 
of his sons Robert and Nicholas IJ, is not known.é 

The details given below under his son Robert show that 
he was living in 1214. It can therefore be supposed that he was 
the Nicholas de Stuteville who was one of the confederate barons 
who met at Stamford at Easter 1215, and one of those who were 
excommunicated by the pope on 16 Dec. A reference in 1224 
suggests that it was he who was captured at Lincoln on 20 May 
1217 and died in the war waged. between king John and the 
barons ;* and a reference given under Nicholas de Stuteville II 
below shows that he was dead by 30 March 12188

This Robert de Stuteville married Sibyl daughter of Philip 
de Valognes, who in the period 1192-1205 gave to him and Sibyl 
and the heirs of their bodies the vill of Torpenhow, Cumberland.? 
In 1212 Robert de Stuteville was holding this vill of the king by 
cornage, rendering 24s. yearly ; and it was recorded that it had 
been of the barony of Alice de Rumilly,? and that as Reginald 
de Lucy who formerly held the land with Alice’s sister refused 


the homage of Philip de Valognes, antecessor of Robert and his - 


wife, king Henry II took his homage, and its service, therefore, was 
in the king’s hand.4 

In the lifetime of his father he acquired, presumably as a 
gift from him, a knight’s fee in Middleton, wap. Pickering Lythe, 
which, described as Robert son of Nicholas de Stuteville, he was 
holding early in the thirteenth century.® He was a benefactor of 
Rosedale priory, to which he gave the vale of Rosedale and other 
gifts, which were confirmed by Nicholas his father.® 

In 1201 when Nicholas de Stuteville owed a scutage payment 
of 100s. in-Cumberland it was recorded that he held by cornage,

for a knight’s fee in Cumberland. In Trinity term 1212, with 
Nicholas his father and others, he was found not guilty of disseisin 
of a tenement in Cottingham ;® and he died before 27 Nov. 1213,

Sibyl de Valognes, his widow, gave the church of Torpenhow 
to Rosedale priory, which was confirmed by her son Eustace.? 
In 1214 she demanded against Nicholas de Stuteville (her father-

the honour of Rosedale, and a third of the vills of Middleton, 
Cropton, Cottingham, Buttercrambe, Scrayingham, Kirkby

Nicholas, Robert’s father ; Nicholas produced the king’s letters of

Poitou on the king’s service.2 She was living on 26 April 1222, 
though there had been a rumour of her death; and she died 
before 24 June of that year; her manor of Torpenhow, held in 
chief, was committed to the bishop of Carlisle, and subsequently 
to Roger de Quincy, who had Eustace de Stuteville, her son and 
heir, in his wardship.* 


EUSTACE DE STUTEVILLE, son and heir of Robert de Stute- 
ville V. On 21 June 1219 orders were issued to deliver seisin to 
Saer earl of Winchester of the land of Liddel which Nicholas de 
Stuteville [II] had assigned to Eustace son of his brother Robert 
de Stuteville until his full age, the custody of which Wilham 
de Valognes had bequeathed to Saer.> Saer gave the custody to 
his son Roger de Quincy, whose right thereto was confirmed on 
21 July 1220 after Saer’s death.® 

In 1230 Eustace de Stuteville’s tenants in Cropton [par. 
Middleton] and Barton’ owed 5 marks for the tallage;’ and he was 
a benefactor of Rosedale priory,? and of St. Peter’s hospital, 
York? 

In 1234 Eustace de Stuteville came before the king and 
showed that Nicholas de Stuteville, his uncle, in the county 
court of York made over" the manor of Cottingham as his right, 
so that he was in seisin thereof for thirteen weeks, and that after- 
wards he was disseised by the king’s will; and he sought justice 
and such seisin as he had had. On the other hand Hugh Wack

and William de Mastak said that they had been in seisin by 
reason of their wives, daughters and heirs of the said Nicholas, 
without whom they ought not to reply, and that they had been 
in seisin by the king’s will until the king had disseised them ; 
and further that their disseisin was more recent and that they 
knew nothing of the seisin of Eustace. The king recorded that 
he found Eustace seised, and that Eustace had offered 1000lz. 
to preserve his seisin except by judgment, or 50 marks for the 
king to take the land into his hand pending judgment ; and he 
admitted that he had disseised Eustace without judgment. 
Eustace again offered the 1000/., which was accepted! The 
king also said that after the seisin which he gave to Hugh and 
William he took the land into his hand by the counsel of the 
magnates of his court on account of their waste in woods and 
other things. And because the king had first disseised Eustace 
without judgment it was considered that this should first be 
cancelled and that Eustace should have seisin again and answer 
Hugh and William and their wives according to law either by 
an assize of mort d’ancestor or a writ of right.” 

Eustace, who on 25 Jan. 1240-1 was appointed as one of the 
knights in Yorkshire to view the condition of the royal castles,’ 
died shortly before 18 Oct. 1241 ; and on 2 Nov. seisin of his lands 
was ordered to be given to Joan wife of Hugh Wake, who had 
made a fine of 100. for having seisin thereof, Eustace being 
described as her kinsman and the lands falling to her by heredi- 
tary right.* 

The wife of Eustace was named Nichola. On 26 Feb. 1243-4 
the sheriff of Yorkshire was ordered to take into the king’s hand 
the lands which she held in dower of Eustace’s inheritance ; and 
on 3 May 1244 William de Percy made a fine of 100 marks for 
having married her without licence.®

the death of his father he appears to have administered the

Brinklow, which Eustace de Stuteville! had held, as the latter

which show his tenure of interests in Wauldby, Hessle, Cliff 
(nr. Market Weighton) and Skirpenbeck, all in the East Riding, 
and in Thirsk, mention being made of Walter de Saureby as his 
steward and of Benedict his serjeant.* 

In 1220 he witnessed a charter of William de Mowbray in 
favour of Fountains abbey.® In 1221 he was holding land in 
Fadmoor ;* and in the following year he recognized in a final 
concord that the greater part of 8 carucates in Skerne and 2 
carucates and a bovate in Hutton [Cranswick] and Cranswick 
were the right of the abbot and convent of Meaux of the gift of 
Nicholas de Stuteville, his father. In 1225 he brought a case 
against the abbot of St. Mary’s, York, complaining that there 
had been a breach of the agreement made in the time of king 
John® between the then abbot, plaintiff, and Nicholas de Stute- 
ville, Nicholas’s father, whose heir he was, tenant, relating to 
common of wood and pasture in Hutton le Hole, Spaunton and 
Farndale. The case was in progress in 1227, Nicholas complaining 
‘that the abbot had sold and destroyed wood to his damage of 
40l1., a point in dispute being whether a perambulation of bounds 
between the land of the elder Nicholas and the king’s forest 
absorbed the common into the latter or not.° Eventually the 
matter appears to have been settled by a final concord of 15 Oct. 
1233, when facilities were given for the cattle of Nicholas and 
his men in Kirkby Moorside, Fadmoor, Gillamoor and Farndale."

Rutland, which subsequently descended to their daughter Joan.? 
There are conclusive reasons for making it impossible that she 
was of the same generation as Dervorguilla, wife of John de 
Baliol and mother of John de Baliol, king of Scotland ; and it 
can be suggested that she was a sister of Alan of Galloway, son 
of Roland of Galloway by his wife Helen de Moreville, receiving 
Whissendine (held by Alan of Galloway) in frank-marriage, and 
that she was the aunt of Dervorguilla, wife of John de Baliol, 
who was undoubtedly Alan’s daughter.? In 1236 Dervorguilla 
widow of Nicholas de Stuteville made a payment for an agree- 
ment with Hugh Wake and his wife Joan. In 1238 dame Dervor- 
guilla de Stuteville was patron of the church of Bossall by reason 
of her custody of the land and heir of Richard de Bossall ;> and 
in Jan. 1240-1 she called to warrant Eustace de Stuteville, and 
Hugh Wake and his wife Joan, respectively, in claims made by 
Peter de Montfort for a moiety of a third of the manor of Cotting- 
ham and a moiety of a third of the manors of Buttercrambe and 
Scrayingham,® which thirds she was evidently holding in dower. 

Margaret the younger daughter, wife of William de Mastac, 
died shortly before 13 Nov. 1235, when the sheriff of Yorkshire 
was ordered to give seisin of her land to Hugh Wake and Joan 
his wife.’ 


" JOAN DE STUTEVILLE, elder daughter and eventually sole 
heir of Nicholas de Stuteville II, was married to Hugh Wake® 
before 29 May 1229, when Hugh was pardoned for having married 
her without the king’s licence.®

in 1233. On 5 July 1234, as Hugh Wake had shown that by 
reason of the seisin which the king had ordered to be made to 
Eustace de Stuteville of the manor of Cottingham Eustace had 
put himself in the manors of Buttercrambe and Kirkby [ Moorside],

that Joan wife of Hugh Wake became the eventual sole heir to 
all these properties, first by the death of her sister in 1235 and 
then by the death of Eustace in 1241. On 4 Feb. 1236-7 Hugh 
Wake and Joan his wife and Eustace de Stuteville were to be 
summoned to answer for the debts which Nicholas de Stuteville 
had owed to the king, and to receive judgment as to which of 
them ought to answer.? 

Hugh Wake died on crusade in 1241 ;? and on 2 Jan. 1241-2 
the king granted to Joan his widow the custody of all his lands to 
hold until the lawful age of his heirs, together with their marriage, 
and licence to marry at pleasure, for a fine of 10,000 marks, after 
the payment of which she would resume the yearly payment of 
201. made by her husband on account of the debts of Nicholas 
de Stuteville her father.4 Before Michaelmas 1244 she married, 
secondly, Hugh Bigod, Chief Justiciar of England, brother of 
Roger Bigod, earl of Norfolk, by whom she was the mother of 
Roger Bigod who succeeded as earl of Norfolk in 1270.5 Hugh 
Bigod died in 1266 ;® and on 18 July 1267, as Joan de Stuteville, 
she and her heirs were granted a weekly market and yearly fair 
at the manor of Liddel, and free warren in the demesne lands of 
Liddel, Arthuret and other places in Cumberland.’ She died 
shortly before 6 April 1276, her heir being her son Sir Baldwin 
Wake, whom she had enfeoffed of the manor of Kirkby Moorside 
two years previously. A description of we seal is given with 
her confirmation charter to Watton priory.®

89. 
THE STUTEVILLES OF WARWICKSHIRE 


In his notification of a gift to Pipewell abbey of land in 
Long Lawford, co. Warwick, John de Stuteville referred to 
Robert de Stuteville as his lord and brother ;? and in a record 
of his gift he 1s described as lord of Long Lawford, Newbold/-on- 
Avon], and Cosford. There are conclusive reasons for identifying 
his brother Robert as Robert de Stuteville III. First, his gift 
was confirmed by William son of Robert de Stuteville, referring 
to John as his uncle,* and later by Joan Wake in her widowhood 
and by Baldwin Wake, her son and heir,® Joan being great- 
granddaughter and ultimate heir of Robert de Stuteville III. 
And, secondly, in a long list of names in a Liber Vitae of Thorney 
abbey® there occur the names of John de Stuteuilla, Agnes wife 
of John, Robert father of John and his mother ‘Erenburga’.’ 

The interests in Long Lawford and Newbold were held at 
the Domesday survey by Geoffrey de Wirce ;§ and were after- 
wards given to Nigel d’Aubigny, from whom they were inherited 
by Roger de Mowbray.® There is evidence that Robert de Stute- 
ville enfeoffed John his younger brother of land in Long, Lawford,?° 
which conforms with the evidence given above ; and it can be 
deduced that these lands in Warwickshire, held by the Stutevilles 
and their descendants of the honour of Mowbray, formed part of 
the 10 knights’ fees granted to Robert de Stuteville III by Roger 
de Mowbray, as an initial settlement of the disputes between

them, though it is important to notice that no interest in these 
particular lands could have been claimed by Robert de Stuteville 
III, as was the case elsewhere, by reason of any tenure of his 
grandfather Robert de Stuteville I as the successor of Hugh son 
of Baldric.4 

The connexion between Warwickshire and Cottingham in 
Yorkshire is illustrated by two original charters of William 
Pantulf of Breedon-on-the-Hill, co. Leicester. By the first? he 
gave to Langley priory the churches of Somerby and Little Dalby, 
co. Leicester, and land in those places; and by the second? he 
gave in dower to Burga his wife* ‘Tunga’, which can be identified 
as Tonge, a hamlet in Breedon-on-the-Hill.5 The first was wit- 
nessed by Robert de Stuteville, Roger and John de Stuteville ; 
and the second by Robert de Stuteville, Roger de Stuteville, 
John de Stuteville his father and John his brother. The remaining 
witnesses are the same in the two charters, evidently issued at 
the same time ;* they include members of a family taking its name 
from Cottingham, a priest of Rowley [E.R.], and a chaplain of 
Cottingham. 

Another tenure of John de Stuteville the elder is shown in 
the carta of Geoffrey Ridel returned from Northamptonshire in 
1166, where it is recorded that John de Stuteville held 12 caru- 
cates of the new feoffment in Abkettleby, Holwell and Harby 
[co. Leicester], and one carucate in Hemington [co. Northampton].’ 
Geoffrey Ridel had confirmed to John de Stuteville c. 1160 all 
the: tenements which John held of Geoffrey s feé, the charter, 
issued on the day when John did homage to Geoffrey at North- 
ampton, including among the witnesses R. son of Nicholas de

John de Stuteville was probably the man of that name 
who witnessed a Leicestershire charter of Ranulf earl of Chester, 
1139-47,2 and a charter of Simon earl of Northampton to the 
church of Lincoln, 1148-51. The details given above show that 
he had a wife named Agnes and two sons John and Roger ;4 and 
he probably died c. 1184 (see below). 

Roger son of John de Stuteville confirmed tithe in Newbold- 
on-Avon to the priory of Monks Kirby.® In the narrative account 
in the Pipewell Register, cited above, it is stated that Roger de 
Stuteville gave to Roger Pantulf his nephew his manor of Newbold- 
on-Avon, and that from Roger Pantulf came William Pantulf his 
son and heir, who died without issue, leaving as his heirs his two 
sisters Burgia and Emma.® Roger Pantulf made an agreement 
with Pipewell abbey relating to moor and pasture in Long Lawford 
in 1209-10.7. In 1200 he had been defendant against Agnes de 
Stuteville in a plea of land in Warwickshire ;§ and in 1214 Robert 
son of Maldred demanded against Roger Pantulf a moiety of 8 
carucates in [Long] Lawford, of 5 carucates in Newbold [-on- 
Avon], and of 3 carucates in Cosford, as his right which ought 
to descend to him for his share of the sisters of Roger de Stuteville.® 

One further point must be noticed. Reference has been 
made above to William Pantulf of Breedon-on-the-Hill and his 
wife Burga. In 1184 a Burga de Stuteville rendered account of 
20 marks for a recognition of the death of John her brother in 
respect of the land of ‘Ketelebi’ with its appurtenances. As

William Pantulf’s widow, Burga made a small gift of land in 
‘Ketlebi’ to Langley priory.t In 1199 Roger Pantulf was plaintiff 
against Walter de Tattishall and Isolda his wife and Eustachia, 
Isolda’s sister, for a knight’s fee in ‘Ketlebi’ and ‘Holewell’, co 
Leicester.2. It can be supposed that Isolda was the wife of Walter 
son of Robert de Tateshall who held an interest in Breedon-on- 
the-Hill,* and who as Isolda Pantulf, then a widow, confirmed 
gifts to Langley priory in 1217 which had been made by William 
Pantulf her grandfather and Burgia her grandmother.* It has 
been noted above that John de Stuteville held an interest in 
Abkettleby and Holwell, co. Leicester ; and Roger Pantulf, the 
plaintiff in 1199, was one of his grandsons and heirs. The de- 
duction can therefore be made, though as a tentative suggestion, 
that Burga, wife of William Pantulf was sister of John de Stute- 
ville the elder, and that her interest in these places was given to 
her on her marriage with William Pantulf, reservation being made, 
perhaps, for the continuation of the interest of her brother John 
for his life. In that event she can be identified as a daughter of 
Robert de Stuteville II.° Her christian name recalls that of the 
latter’s wife Erneburga ; and it can be noticed that there were 
two other examples of the name Burga or Burgia—namely, 
Burga daughter of Robert de Stuteville ITI who married William 
de Vescy (p. 8 above), and Burgia daughter of Roger Pantulf 
(see above), both of whom were lineal descendants of Erneburga.

fe Slur VILEES OF WESTON COLVILLE 
AND BURTON AGNES 


The details given below will show that an Osmund de 
Stuteville had an interest in Weston Colville, co. Cambridge, 
held of the honour of Warenne, in the reign of Henry II, and that 
his successors there were Roger de Stuteville and Roger’s son 
Anselm. They will also show, in the references to the disputes 
over Anselm’s inheritance, that Anselm had a niece whose husband 
was engaged in a case in 1199. This suggests that the latest 
possible date for Roger’s birth is 1145. He can therefore scarcely 
have been the son of Osmund de Stuteville of Cowesby and 
Gressenhall, who died on crusade c. 1192, and whose widow was

living as the wife of a subsequent husband in 1200 (see §4). 
Moreover Cowesby, held of the main branch of the Stuteville 
family, was inherited by that Osmund’s descendants, who had no 
interest in Weston Colville ; nor had the successors of Osmund 
de Stuteville of Weston Colville any direct tenure of the main 
Stuteville fee. In the account of Osmund de Stuteville of Cowesby 
it will be suggested that he was a younger son of Robert de 
Stuteville III. The combined evidence therefore leads to the 
deduction that there were two Osmunds de Stuteville of different 
generations. Although there is no proof that Roger de Stuteville 
who succeeded Osmund in Weston Colville was his son? the fact 
is very likely ; and it can be suggested that this Osmund was 
a younger brother of Robert de Stuteville III, and so an uncle 


of Osmund de Stuteville of Cowesby. Chronological considerations 


support this suggestion. 

At Michaelmas 1172 Roger de Stuteville paid 37s. 6d. scutage 
for the fee which he held of the fee of Adam de Brus [II]*. This 
represented a holding of 1g knights’ fees; and there seems no 
doubt that it included the manor of Burton Agnes and lands 
belonging thereto. It is possible that Roger acquired this by 
marriage ; but the alternative that he inherited it from his father, 
and that his father was enfeoffed by Brus, must not be over- 
looked,® in which case his father’s death was not later than 1172. 
Mappleton, held of Brus as a sub-tenure of the honour of Holder- 
ness,® was presumably acquired in the same way. 


Roger de Stuteville was succeeded by his son Anselm.’ 
This was presumably earlier than Michaelmas 1194, when William 
Engelram owed 20 marks for having right in the king’s court 
for a knight’s fee in West Heslerton against Anselm (Alelmum)

Anselm de Stuteville was dead in 1199, when the disputes 
over his inheritance, which lasted for several years, had begun. 
In that year Robert de Tateshale was engaged in a case in York-

his wife, and Robert de Cramaville and Isabel his wife.6 In 1202

Another claimant to the inheritance of Anselm de Stuteville 
had originally arisen in the person of a certain Simon de Stuteville, 
who at Michaelmas 1199 rendered account of 5 marks for having 
his right for 9 carucates 7 bovates in Burton [Agnes] against 
Alice de Stuteville, 2 marks similarly for 5 carucates 6 bovates 
in Harpham against Herbert de St. Quintin and Agnes his wife, 
and 4 marks for 9 carucates 2 bovates in Thornholme (Tirmin) 
and Gransmoor (Craucemore) against Robert de Crammaville and 
Isabel his wife.4 His claim was evidently unsuccessful ; and it is 
not unlikely that he was illegitimate.® It is possible, though 
there is no clue available, that he was the ancestor of Henry de | 
Stuteville, whose daughter Agnes was plaintiff in an assize of 
novel disseisin of a messuage and land in Mappleton in 1260, the 
jurors saying that Henry had enfeoffed her on his death-bed, and 
that she should recover seisin.® 

The details given above, together with other evidence, 
provide the following particulars of the five sisters of Anselm de

$4. 
THE STUTEVILLES OF COWESBY AND GRESSENHALL 


Apart from other evidence the fact that the Foliot family, 
heirs of the Stutevilles of Gressenhall, also had an interest in 
Cowesby is a proof that the Stutevilles of Cowesby were the 
same branch of the family as those of Gressenhall. | 

In the comprehensive list of benefactions to Rievaulx 
abbey confirmed by Edward III, there are mentioned a gift 
which Osmund de Stuteville made of facilities in Cowesby, re- 
ferring to a road which the monks made in the time of his father 
at the east of the vill leading up the hill ; and also the confirmation 
which William de Stuteville made of this gift of Osmund his 
brother.2, This shows that Osmund’s father had an interest in 
Cowesby. It can scarcely be supposed that Robert de Stuteville 
II held any such interest ; but it is known that his son Robert III 
had an interest there in view of his controversy with the bishop 
of Durham about the chapel. Robert III had a son named 
Osmund ;4 and it can therefore be suggested that Osmund de 
Stuteville of Cowesby was this younger son of Robert de Stute- 
ville III, his gift to Rievaulx being confirmed by his brother 
William, the latter’s eldest son and heir; and further that he 
was enfeoffed of lands by his father, being the Osmund de Stute- 
ville who held two-thirds of a knight’s fee of Robert de Stuteville 
III in 1166.2 A date c. 1140-45 for his birth would be consistent 
with the fact that he died on crusade at Joppa, probably in August 
1192.6 In 1189-92 he issued a notification that his own controversy 
_ with the bishop of Durham about the chapel of Cowesby had been 
settled.?, He married Isabel the heiress of the family descended 
from Wimar, the Domesday tenant of William de Warenne in 
Gressenhall, Norfolk. With her assent he confirmed to Castle 
Acre priory all the gifts made by Wimar and his successors.® After 
his death and not later than 1194 Isabel his widow married 
William de Huntingfield.

A dispute which arose over her dower from the lands of 
Osmund de Stuteville, her former husband, was in progress in 
1199, when she and William de Huntingfield were plaintiffs 
against William de Stuteville, the case being postponed by the 
king’s writ in virtue of the custody of the counties of Cumberland 
and Northumberland and the castles committed to the latter.? 
The reference identifies the defendant as William de Stuteville, 
son of Robert de Stuteville III, the head of the main English 
line of the family, who as suggested above was Osmund’s elder 
brother ; and it suggests that Osmund’s heir was then under age 
and in the wardship of William. In 1200 the case between William 
de Stuteville and Isabel, described as Isabel de Gressenhall, for 
her dower which she sought in Cowesby, was again respited ; 
and later in the year William de Huntingfield and William de 
Stuteville made an agreement for the dower of Isabel, the former’s 
wife, by which the vill of Cowesby was assigned to her from the 
lands of Osmund, her former husband, with an adjustment should 
it be more or less than a third of his lands.2. In 1202 a final 
concord was levied relating to Isabel’s dower from Osmund de 
Stuteville’s holdings in Brantingham, Riplingham, [?] Newbald, 
Stillingfleet, Newsham [par. Kirby Wiske], Cowesby, Kepwick 
and [East] Ness, by which she quitclaimed all her right therein 
to William de Stuteville, who gave her for her life in the name 
of dower all his demesne of Cowesby and 184 bovates of land 
held in villeinage there, and a third of the service of Robert de 
Newesum who was holding a third of a knight’s fee.® 

There is evidence that Osmund had a son named John, 
perhaps a son by an earlier marriage.t A younger Osmund may 
also have been his son. The son, however, who occurs as the 
successor of Osmund and Isabel was William. 

William de Stuteville occurs as a knight of earl de Warenne 
in 1216 ;§ and on 8 May 1229 he was granted a yearly fair and a 
weekly market at his manor of Gressenhall.? As William de 
Stuteville of Cowesby he was a benefactor of Rievaulx abbey ;8 
and as William son of Osmund de Stuteville he confirmed to

which he held until his death in 1259.8 They were certainly con-

a son named Robert ;7 and William de Stuteville of Richard’s 
Castle had a son who was significantly named Osmund.® 

At the death of William de Stuteville of Richard’s Castle 
shortly before 20 May 1259 his wife’s heir was her son Hugh de 
Mortimer. William de Stuteville of Gressenhall was succeeded

where.!2 He can therefore be identified with the Robert son of

William de Stuteville, who in January 1262-3 with Joan his

PEE STLULEVILLES OF CREAT AY EON AND 
HEMLINGTON 


This branch descended from a Robert de Stuteville who 
died between 1208 and 1219. It is probable that he was Robert, 
‘a younger son of Robert de Stuteville III ;’ but no proof appears 
to be available. At Michaelmas 1206 Robert de Muschans (or 
Muscampis) owed 5 marks for having a writ corvam rege against 
Robert de Stuteville for 6 carucates in [Great] Ayton and 4 
carucates in Hemlington [par. Stainton] as his right and inheri- 
tance. William de Stuteville, eldest son of Robert de Stuteville 
III, held interests in both places at his death in 1203.9 The former 
place was partly a member of the Meinil fee, and the latter a 
member of the honour of Chester.!° 

Robert de Stuteville gave to Guisborough priory a carucate 
of land in Ayton, which Nicholas son of Osbert de Aton held of 
him, with. the latter’s homage and service, namely a pound of

and fair at Ayton! In August 1264 and again in December,

Wilham de Stuteville, described as of Ayton in Cleveland, 
issued a charter to Whitby abbey, quitclaiming all right in the 
advowson of the church of [Great] Ayton and lands belonging 
thereto, and in all the lands which the abbey held in the vill; 
quitclaiming also suit at the court of Ayton and to the mill of 
Ayton therefor; confirming pasture for eight oxen and four 
horses and other easements in the vill; quitclaiming all right 
in certain pieces of land about which he had impleaded the abbot 
and convent by a writ of novel disseisin before Sir Richard de 
Middleton, then king’s justice, assigned to take the case at 
Coxwold ; and granting other facilities for the abbey’s manor in 
Ayton.® The date given in the text of the document is 6 July 
1265 ; but it can be suggested that this is a mistake, possibly for 
1275.7 

It seems clear that William de Stuteville died shortly after- 
wards without an heir, and that the manor of Great Ayton es- 
cheated to Baldwin Wake, who was seised of it in demesne at 
his death, when on 23 March 1281-2 a detailed extent was made, 
showing that he held it of Sir Nicholas de Meinil for half a knight’s 
fee, no mention being made of William de Stuteville among the 
holders of lands ; the extent adds that the lady of Hemlington 
held the manor of Hemlington and 12s. 2d. yearly rent in Ayton 
in dower from the lands of Robert de Stuteville, formerly her 
husband, valued at 21. yearly ; and that the abbot of Whitby 
held the church of [Great] Ayton to his own uses.’

$6. 
A YOUNGER BRANCH OF COTTINGHAM 


A younger line of the Stutevilles was described as of Cotting- 
ham in several references in the fourteenth century. In 1334-35 
Robert de Stuteville granted to Hugh Wake in frank-marriage 
with his daughter Isabel all his lands and tenements in Cottingham 
and Rowley, a witness being Thomas Wake, lord of Liddel ;? and 
on 12 Sept. 13385 a-deed refers to John de Stuteville, rector of 
Houwelle {[Howell, co. Lincoln] and his brothers Nicholas and 
Robert and their sister dame Isabel Wake in connexion with 
lands in Cottingham, Braithwaite and Hull Bank.? In 1307 Robert 
de Stuteville the elder had released to Robert de Stuteville the 
younger all his right in lands in Cottingham and Braithwaite ;4 
and in 1328 a settlement was made of lands in those places and 
in Hull Bank between Robert de Stuteville of Cottingham the 
elder and Margaret his wife and Robert de Stuteville the younger, 
life interests being reserved for Milisent,5 Lora and Isabel, daugh- 
ters of Robert the elder.® 

The earlier descent of this line is obscure; and the only 
clue—which is far from affording any proof—seems to be a deed 
of 1242-43 by which a certain Walter de Hedun released to 
Nicholas son of Robert de Stuteville his right in the service of a 
tenement in Newton, par. Cottingham, formerly held of Sir 
Eustace de Stuteville and Robert his son and afterwards of the 
said Walter.” It is probable that this Sir Eustace was a younger 
brother of William de Stuteville the judge and so an uncle of 
Nicholas de Stuteville II, who in 1233 was ordered to restore 
land which had belonged to Eustace de Stuteville, his uncle, to 
Robert, the latter’s son and heir.6 The deed of 1242-43 suggests 
that this Robert had then been succeeded by Nicholas his son ; 
but no proof is available that Nicholas was the father or ancestor 
of Robert de Stuteville mentioned above, who was possessed of 
land in Cottingham in 1307. A Robert de Stuteville of Cottingham 
Occitan 12797

7. 
THE EARLY GENERATIONS OF THE NORMAN LINE 


The main Norman line of the family, lords of Valmont,! 
descended from Nicholas d’Estouteville, who heads the ensuing 
account. Although documentary evidence is not available, there 
is no reason to doubt his descent from Robert de Stuteville I. 
It is significant that at the Domesday survey Hugh son of Baldric, 
the predecessor of Robert de Stuteville I in a large number of 
holdings in Yorkshire, held tenancies-in-chief in Stratfield—later 
Stratfield Stuteville and subsequently Stratfield Saye—and 
Church Oakley in Hampshire ;? and of these the former was held 
later by Nicholas d’Estouteville of Valmont, and an interest in 
both was held by John d’Estouteville his great-grandson, as is 
shown by the details given below. 


The chronological evidence suggests that Nicholas and 
Robert de Stuteville III were of the same generation, and that 
they were both born in the first twenty years of the twelfth century. 
It is reasonably probable that Nicholas was the eldest son of 
Robert II.2 It has been suggested that he was the son of a first 
wife, and that Erneburga, Robert III’s mother, was a second 


1 Dept. Seine-Inf., arr. Yvetot, cant. Valmont. The castle and abbey, 
well known to visitors to Normandy, incurred no serious damage in the 
recent war. 


2 D.B., i, 48. By a charter c. 1080 William the Conqueror confirmed 
to the abbey of Préaux, dio. Lisieux, the tithe of Stratfield which Hugh 
son of Baldric had given (Cal. Docs. France, no. 318). 


3 Nicholas is given as the eldest son of Robert II, son of Robert I, in 
the account of the Norman lines by Pére Anselme in Histotve généalogique 
... de France, viii, 88 ; this is based almost entirely on French sources and 
no references to their English lands are given. The same descent is given 
by G. de la Morandiére in Histoire de la maison da’ Estouteville en Normandie 
(Paris, 1903), p. 653. This book gives constant references to the English 
lines and English sources, but the distinction between the Norman and 
English lines is far from clear; there are several useful citations from 
‘Archives de Valmont’, though the original texts unfortunately are not 
given. Some of these are given in G. A. de La Roque, Histoire généalogique 
de la maison de Harcourt (Paris, 1662), vol. iv, additions and corrections, 
unnumbered pp. 22-3. My acknowledgements are due, especially for the 
references from the last two works and several others bearing on the early 
descent of the Norman family, to the late Mr. L. C. Loyd with whom I 
was engaged shortly before his death in 1947 in endeavouring to disentangle 
some of the confusions between the Norman and English lines—confusions 
which are made complex by the recurrence of the christian names of Robert 
and Nicholas in branches of both of them. Since this section was compiled 
my attention was drawn to Sir George Sitwell’s Tales of my native village 
(1933), which contains a detailed account of the descent of the lords of 
Valmont and in particular of their connexion with the village of Eckington, 
based both on English and Norman sources, though documentary references 
are not often given ; there is a vivid account of Valmont and its district, 
with a description of the family tombs which existed or still exist in the 
abbey, and an illustration of the castle and abbey from a coloured drawing 
made in 1702 ; an interesting chapter deals with a second John d’Estouteville 
who was rector of a moiety of Eckington from 1312 to 1337.

THE SITUTEVILLE PAMILIES 43 


wife. However that may be, the facts that Nicholas’s only 
apparent inheritance in England was in Hampshire, and that 
neither he nor his descendants appear to have made any claim 
to the bulk of the English possessions of Robert I, which were 
claimed certainly with some success by Robert III and his son 
Wiliam, are not inconsistent with the practice, of which other 
examples could be given, of the elder son inheriting the Norman 
property with a small holding in England, and of the younger 
being allotted an interest in the greater part of the English lands. 

In addition to the lands in Hampshire the Norman line 
acquired important holdings in England through the marriage 
of Robert son and heir of Nicholas with Leonia daughter of 
Edward of Salisbury. These English lands passed by inheritance 
until shortly before the outbreak of the Hundred Years war. 
Their confiscation, during the periods when the two countries 
were actually at war, was of a temporary character; and the 
family affords an unusual example of important lands on both 
sides of the Channel passing by inheritance to so late a date. It 
will be seen that the inheritance acquired by the marriage of 
Robert d’Estouteville to Leonia included the honour of Rames 
in Normandy as well as 15 knights’ fees and other interests in 
England ; and that extensive interests in Northumberland were 
acquired by a marriage of their great-grandson Robert d’Estoute- 
ville of Valmont and Rames.

Nicholas d’Estouteville founded a Benedictine abbey in 1169.1 
His foundation charter, mentioning Juliana his wife and issued 
with the consent of Robert his son and heir, included his gifts. of 
the site of the abbey, land and mills in Valmont and other places 
in the canton of Valmont, together with three churches, and in 
England the church of Stratfield (£stredfled) with the hermitage 
of St. Leonard.? These gifts and gifts made by Juliana his wife 
and his four sons Robert, Nicholas, William and Richard are 
included in a confirmation charter issued to the abbey by king 
Henry II, 1181-83 ;3 and additional gifts made by his sons in a 
subsequent confirmation issued by the king.4 

A clue to the parentage of Juliana his wife is given by the 
references to his holding in the hundred of Norton, Somerset, 
noted above. Between 1121 and 1135 king Henry I gave to 
Jordan d’Auffay a wife named Juliana daughter of Golescalc, 
who had come with queen Adeliza from the territory of Louvain 
to England > and in a return of 1212 it°is stated that the same 
king had given Norton® with the hundred to Jordan d’Auffay in 
marriage with a certain Juliana.” At Michaelmas 1180 the 40s. 
worth of land in the hundred of Norton, which as the preceding 
rolls show had been in the possession of Nicholas d’Estouteville 
from 1158, was in the possession of Richard d’Auffay.® It can 
therefore be deduced that after the death of Jordan d’Auffay 
Juliana daughter of Golescale married Nicholas, who held Norton 
for his life in her right, and that on his death it passed by inheri- 
tance to Richard d’Auffay, her son by her first husband. In the 
first of king Henry II’s confirmations to the abbey of Valmont, 
1181-83, noted above, there were included a gift of Juliana wife 
of Nicholas d’Estouteville of 100s. of land at Norwich ‘de primo 
maritagio suo’, and a confirmation by Richard d’Auffay and 
John his son of a mill at Gouzeville® which had been given by 
Nicholas d’Estouteville and Juliana his wife. 

Nicholas d’Estouteville occurs as a frequent witness to 
charters of king Henry II, some issued at Westminster, 1155-5819 
and some issued in Normandy subsequently." In 1162 he was 
present with the king at Fécamp at the ceremony of the translation

confirming the gift made by Nicholas and Eid’ [sic] his wife. 
Nicholas stated that even if those charters were issued king John 
afterwards presented a clerk when he had the land of Nicholas 
in his custody; and he put forward the charter of king John 
making the gift of the vicarage,? and the charter of J. bishop of 
Norwich confirming this, and another which witnessed that, 
when the land of Nicholas was in the king’s hand because he 
was in Normandy with the king of France, he (the bishop) had 
given the church to John archdeacon of Worcester on the king’s 
presentation and had instituted him, saving the perpetual vicarage. 
Nicholas, therefore, in view of the king’s presentation, claimed 
the same seisin as the king’s ; and as the abbot recognized that 
the king had presented the last rector, though unjustly, it was 
adjudged that Nicholas should have his seisin, and that the 
abbot if he wished should acquire another writ.? 

The combined ‘effect of these reterences ‘shows (that. tne 
elder Nicholas married Eidiva sister of Hugh de Gournay V and 
daughter of Hugh de Gournay IV ;* and there can be no doubt 
that it was through this marriage that he acquired his interest in 
Kimberley and Bedingham.®
