After the restoration to the younger Nicholas this interest 
continued in his descendants during the thirteenth century? 
Thus Nicholas de Stuteville was a party to fines, or mentioned 
as a Claimant, relating to Kimberley and Bedingham in 1234-35, 
1239-40 and 1241-422 John son of Nicholas and Simon de Gren- 
ville, husband of Alice widow of Nicholas, occur there in 1257.3 
At the time of the Barons’ war John de Stuteville possessed the 
manor and advowson of Kimberley* and land in Bedingham, and 
his land in Kimberley was seized, the rent being received by Sir 
William Bardolf.6 John was dead by 3 Oct. 1266, when Denise 
his widow was given maintenance from his land in Bedingham.® 
In 1284 and 1299 an interest in Kimberley was held by Sir 
Nicholas de Stuteville.’ 


(3) William, third son of the tounder of Valmont... In 
addition to his gifts to the abbey of land in Normandy, recorded 
in the confirmation charters of king Henry II, he gave a mark 
of land in ‘Estrafled’, which can be identified as Stratfield, 
Hampshire, the church of which with the hermitage of St. Leonard 
had been given to the abbey by his father. He can therefore be 
identified with the William d’Estouteville who occurs at Michael- 
mas 1176 in connexion with that place; and it can be deduced 
that his interest there was due to a feoffment by his father. It . 
will be seen below that Robert de Say, who married Alice de 
Stuteville, held a knight’s fee there in 1242-43 of John d’Estoute- 
ville of Valmont, the head of the senior line; and that in 1277 a 
later Robert de Say held this of Robert d’Estouteville, John’s 
son.

ROBERT D’ESTOUTEVILLE, eldest son and heir of Nicholas 
d’Estouteville, to whose foundation charter to the abbey of 
Valmont in 1169 he gave his consent. Among his gifts in Nor- 
mandy to that abbey were a mill ‘apud Beverdam de honore 
ramorum’—which can be identified as the honour of Rames— 
and three churches in the canton of Saint-Romain, arr. Le Havre, 
in which Rames is situate. It was through his marriage with 
Leonia that he acquired the honour of Rames in Normandy and 
her large interests in England.

In 1185, after his death, his widow was in the king’s gift, 
being described as ‘de parentela Edwardi de Salesburia ex parte 
patris, et ex parte matris est de progenie Rogeri de Reimes’ ; 
she was then holding the vill called Diham [Dedham, Essex], 
which was her inheritance, worth 24/1. yearly ; she had a son 
and two daughters, and her age was not known by the jurors. 
Her paternity is corroborated by a statement in an assize of 
darrein presentment to the church of Gunby St. Nicholas, co. 
Lincoln, in 1202 that Edward of Salisbury had a daughter Leonia 
whom Robert d’Estouteville married.2 A charter of Robert 
d’Estouteville to the abbey of Saint-Georges-de-Boscherville 
records that at the prayer of his wife Leonia he gave a rentcharge 
of 50s. on his mill on the bank of Biuredan® in exchange for a 
similar rent which Evrard de Salesberiis had given from the 
tenants at Rames.4 This proves that Edward of Salisbury 
possessed an interest in the honour of Rames ; and it 1s important 
to ascertain the name of his wife, Leonia’s mother. 


At Michaelmas 1130 William de Hoctona® rendered account 
of 200i. for the wife of Edward of Salisbury (Sar’) with her land 
for the use of Payn his son, paying 100 marks of silver as an 
instalment ; and Payn de Hoctona rendered account of 200 
marks of silver and 2 marks of gold for the same widow, paying 
5 marks of silver as an instalment. Payn duly married her and 
had issue two daughters, one of whom Maud married Robert 
Grimbald ; andina charter to Biddlesden abbey Maud de Houghton 
mentioned Robert Grimbald her husband, Payn her father, Adeliz 
de Raimes her mother, and William her grandfather.’ It can 
therefore be deduced that Edward of Salisbury married Adeliz 
de Rames, who subsequently married Payn de Houghton; and 
this to some extent conforms with the statement made in 1185 
that Leonia on her mother’s side was ‘de progenie’ of Roger

de Reimes. The relationship of Adeliz to Roger and the identity

In addition to Leonia’s inheritance of the honour of Rames 
in Normandy her holdings in England consisted of three distinct 
portions. These were (a) 15 knights’ fees mainly in Nottingham- 
shire and Derbyshire; (b) the manor of Dedham, Essex ; and (c) an 
interest in the honour of Bourn. 


(a) The 15 knights’ fees represented a share of the barony 
for which Hubert son of Ralph returned his carta of 30 knights’ 
fees in 1166, 20 being of the old feoffment and 10 of the new.? 
This barony was composed of lands held by Ralph son of Hubert 
at the Domesday survey.2 The 15 fees held by Leonia, which 
passed to her descendants the Estoutevilles of Valmont, included 
manors in Barlborough, Duckmanton and Eckington, co. Derby, 
Kirkby in Ashfield and Barton in Fabis, co. Nottingham, and 
land in Gunby St. Nicholas, co. Lincoln, and in Dalby on the 
Wolds, co. Leicester. In all these places Ralph son of Hubert 
had held an interest in 1086. It is significant that at the time 
of the Leicesterhire survey, 1124-29, Edward of Salisbury, Leonia’s 
father, held 9 carucates in Dalby on the Wolds, which was the 
same amount of land held there in 1086 by Ralph son of Hubert. 
Moreover, in the proceedings in the Gunby case of 1202 mentioned 
above it was stated that land there had been recovered in the 
court of Henry II by Robert d’Estouteville who had married 
Leonia daughter of Edward of Salisbury. Edward of Salisbury’s 
mother was named Maud ; for Maud wife of Hasculf de Tany and 
Graaland her son were benefactors of Bermondsey abbey,® and it 
was stated in the Gunby case that Edward of Salisbury was the

1 The complications of the tenures of the Rames family in England, 
with special reference to Dedham, where Leonia’s interest was an under- 
tenancy, are discussed by Round in Geoffrey de Mandeville, pp. 399-404. 
It is not unlikely, although this was not suggested by Round, that Adeliz 
represented an elder line descended from Roger de Rames, the Domesday 
tenant-in-chief ; and that it was through this elder line that the Norman 
property at Rames passed to Adeliz and so to Leonia, a younger line in- 
heriting his lands in England. The basis of such a solution as this was 
adopted by Mr. A. L. Raimes in his paper ‘Reymes of Overstrand’, reprinted 
from Norfolk Archaeology, vol. xxx, which contains detailed accounts of 
some of the English branches of the family, that of Overstrand descending 
in the male line to the nineteenth century. Cf. also his paper ‘The Family 
of Reymes of Wherstead’ in Proc. Suffolk Inst. of Archaeology, vol. xxiii.

At Michaelmas 1187 her son Henry d’Estouteville accounted 
in Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire for 15. for scutage in respect 
of the fee shared between him and Hubert son of Ralph, paying 
this sum in full. It can be deduced that Leonia had made over 
her interest to him; for she confirmed to Welbeck abbey the 
gift of Richard Basset her knight of the vill of Duckmanton, co. 
Derby, which he was holding of her barony, and a subsequent 
confirmation (not later than 1203) was issued by her son Henry, 
who referred to the charter issued by his mother dame Leonia 
de Reynes [sic].4. Her prior interest is also shown by a charter, 
issued apparently as a result of the settlement made in the 
presence of Geoffrey archbishop of York in 1191-94 of a contro- 
versy relating to the church of Annesley, by which she, described 
as Leonia de Ramis, and Henry de Stutivilla, her son and heir, 
confirmed the church to Felley priory.2 At Michaelmas 1202

the commitment to Brian de l’Isle of Kirkby [in Ashfield] which

with his enemies, although Henry’s mother was then holding it.”

In the following year, Leonia, described as widow of Robert 
de Stuteville, gave 200 marks for seisin of all her land of which 
she was seised before she was disseised by the king’s order by 
reason of her son then in Normandy, and for having the manor 
of Barton [in Fabis] of her inheritance ; and seisin was ordered if 
her fine was assured ;} and at Michaelmas she took Henry’s place 
for a sum due for scutage in Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire.? 
At Michaelmas 1205 she accounted for 30 marks for scutage for 
15 knights’ fees in the two counties, Hubert son of Ralph ac- 
counting for the same sum ;3 and in 1212 she and Hubert son 
of Ralph both held 15 knights’ fees there in chief, she holding 5 
carucates in Gunby also in chief.4 


(b) As noted above Leonia was holding the vill of Dedham, 
Essex, of her inheritance in 1185. This was an under-tenancy, 
and it has been suggested above that she held it of a younger 
line of the Rames family. In 1214-15, in a return of the fees of 
Richard de Rames, she held a knight’s fee in Dedham, of which 
the service was due as to one moiety to the heir of Richard de 
Rames and as to the other to the heir of William de Rames.6 At 
Michaelmas 1190 in an entry relating to the scutage due from 
Richard de Rames it was claimed that William son of Robert 
de Stuteville held half a knight’s fee in Dedham.® This suggests 
that Robert and Leonia had a younger son William, enfeoffed 
by his mother of half her holding. In 1202 an assize of mort 
d’ancestor for half a knight’s fee in Dedham brought against 
her by William Grimbald was postponed while her son was in 
the king’s service; and it was again postponed in 1206 while 
her knights were in the king’s service overseas.” It will be seen 
below that Leonia’s interest in Dedham descended to the Estoute- 
villes of Valmont. 


(c) Leonia possessed an interest in the honour of Bourn, 
co. Cambridge. This honour had been held by William Peverel, 
-and on his death in 1147-48 his coheirs were his four sisters.8 
They were Maud, who married Hugh de Dover and died s.p. ; 
Alice, who married Hamo Pecche and had issue; Roaise, who 
married Rollo de Harcourt, by whom she had a daughter Aubreye 
wife of William Trussebut ; and Asceline, who married Geoffrey 
de Walterville and whose eventual heirs were their two daughters.

In 1166 Hugh de Dover returned his caria from Cambridgeshire 
in respect of his wife’s inheritance, apparently amounting to 
5 knights’ fees, on which he paid 5 marks for the knights of the 
honour of Bourn in 1168 for the aid for the marriage of the king’s 
daughter.t At Michaelmas 1194 scutage payments for the king’s 
redemption were made or due from Gilbert Pecche and Leonia 
de Stuteville of two and a half marks each for their shares of the 
honour of Bourn, and Aubreye Trussebut paid 20s. on account of 
33s. 4d.2 These sums represent three equal shares of one and 
two-thirds fees, the total corresponding to the 5 fees held in 1166 
by Hugh de Dover in right of his wife who had died without issue. 
Again, in areturnc. 1210-12 Leonia de Stuteville, Hilaria Trussebut 
and Gilbert Pecche are named as sharing a holding in Bourn of 
the honour of Peverel of Dover.? Although the evidence may 
suggest that Leonia’s interest represented that of the fourth 
Peverel sister, Asceline de Walterville, the origin of such an 
interest 1s obscure. It does not appear to have survived her 
death ; and in 1220 Roger de Torpel, son of one of Asceline’s 
daughters, fined for his relief on his mother’s land, forming an 
inheritance for him of a sixth part of a barony ;° clearly his moiety 
of his grandmother's third part of the honour of Bourn. 


Leonia was living on 8 Jan. 1214-5, when she was engaged 
in a plea of novel disseisin of land in Barlborough, co. Derby.® 
It is probable that she died before 14 April 1216, when the king 
made a presentation to the church of Kirkby in Ashfield, vacant 
.and in his gift by reason of the land which had belonged to her 
being in his hand.? On 6 Jan. 1216-7 the king gave orders for 
Eustace de Es to have 20 librates of land which had belonged to 
her in Barton [in Fabis}] and Bradmore.2 On 22 May 1217 seisin 
of the land of Eckington was ordered to be given to William de 
Cantilupe the younger ;° and on 11 June that of all her lands in 
Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire to William earl de Warenne, 
the manor of Eckington being reserved in Cantilupe’s favour on 
the following day and again on 22 July.!® As her father, Edward 
of Salisbury, was dead at Michaelmas 1130, Leonia lived to an 
advanced age.

With regard to Robert d’Estouteville, Leonia’s husband, he 
can presumably be identified with Robert de Estoteville who 
was among those who did not come or send information for the 
return of knights’ fees in Normandy in 1172.1 In 1180 he and 
his brothers Nicholas and William had extensive holdings in the 
forest of Fécamp ; and he had 200 acres of the king’s gift in the 
forest of Lillebonne, on the western bound of which lay the castle 
of Rames.? In that year he accounted for the farm of Lillebonne 
for five years, and had the custody of the castles of Lions-la- 
Forét and Arques.? After his death, not later than 1185, king 
Henry II gave for his (Robert’s) soul to the abbey of Valmont 
four churches in the district of Lillebonne.t| A large number of 
charters issued by the king in Normandy, none of which can be 
dated later than 1185,° were witnessed by a Robert de Stutevilla 
or Estoutevilla, and the balance of the evidence suggests that he 
can be identified as Robert d’Estouteville of Valmont rather than 
Robert III of the English branch, who was a justice itinerant in 
England and sheriff of Yorkshire. 

Besides Henry his son and successor, and, as suggested 
above, a younger son William, Robert d’Estouteville had a 
daughter to whose husband, Robert de Brettevill, he gave a 
moiety of the land in Gunby in marriage.® 


HENRY D’ESTOUTEVILLE inherited Valmont from Robert 
his father, and the honour of Rames after his mother’s death.’ 
His temporary possession of a considerable portion of his mother’s 
lands in England, which he forfeited as an adherent of Philip 
Augustus in Normandy, has already been mentioned. He was 
among the Norman barons who on 13 Nov. 1205 certified the 
rights which the late kings of England had against the clergy in 
the duchy of Normandy.” Im the list. of .tees. held of Philip 
Augustus in the period 1210-20 he held one of the five and a 
half fees in Etouteville, which lay at Valmont, Thiergeville and 
Angerville ;? and also a fee at Rames.!° By a charter issued in the 
castle of Valmont on 13 Dec. 1228 he gave to the abbey of 
Valmont the tithes of the value of his fairs of Valmont for lights

Maud his wife was a daughter of earl Hamelin by Isabel 
daughter and heir of William de Warenne, third earl of Surrey ; 
she was the widow of Henry count of Eu, who probably died in 
1190-91 ;? and by him she was the mother of Alice, who became 
countess of Eu and lady of Hastings and of the honour of Tickhill.? 
Her own heir at her death was John d’Estouteville, her son by 
her second husband ; and there is some evidence that the Warenne 
holding in Gresham, Norfolk, formed her marriage portion.‘ 
In 1212 she was a benefactor of the abbey of Valmont.® 

It is likely that sometime after the death of his mother 
Leonia in 1215-16 he recovered her inheritance in England, and 
forfeited it again after the renewal of the war with France in 
1224 ; for on 5 Sept. 1226 the sheriff of Nottinghamshire was 
ordered to take into the king’s hand all the lands which Henry 
d’Estouteville had held there of the king ;° and again on 30 March 
1228 all the land which had belonged to him in Barton [in Fabis| 
of the lands of the Normans, and in Bradmore.’ He died not 
later than 1236, and was buried in the abbey of Valmont, where 
his obit was observed on 5 April.8 Besides his sons John and 
Robert, mentioned below, he had a daughter Isabel, who married 
Peétersde- Preus.®

In England he recovered the lands of his inheritance. On 

21 Dec. 1226 (when his father was still living and the two countries 

were at war) the sheriff of Nottinghamshire was ordered to give 

him seisin of his lands which the king had caused to be taken 
into his hand ;! and in 1230 he paid 50d. as a fine for 15 fees in 

Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire. The extent of his English 

lands is shown in several returns. Thus, in 1235-36 in a return of 

the honour of Tickhill®? he was assessed at 9 marks for the aid 
for the marriage of the king’s sister for 44 fees, and a further 

21 marks for 104 fees, making a total of 15 fees ;* his holding in 

Barton in Fabis and Kirkby in Ashfield representing the former, 

and: that in. Eckington representimg the latter”. In 1237 an 

institution to the church of Kirkby in Ashfield was made on 
his presentation. His moiety of the barony of Hubert son of 

Ralph is mentioned in a return of 1242-43 ;7 and in 1244 he held 

Kirkby, Eckington, Barton and Bradmore, and rather more than 

9 fees were held by his tenants.’ In 1242-43 two tenants held of 

him 3 carucates in Gunby, co. Lincoln, his holding in chief being 

of the new feoffment ;? and in 1244 his holding of 4 carucates 
there was included in an inquisition of the serjeanties and lands 
of the Normans and aliens who adhered to the king of France.1° 

In Essex, in 1235-36 the prioress of Campsey paid half a mark’s 

scutage for half a fee in Dedham of his fee 4 and in 1244 he held 

a moiety of that manor.” All these interests had been held by 

his grandmother Leonia de Rames. In Hampshire, in 1235-36 he 

paid 2 marks for the aid for his fee in Stratfield Saye, where in 

1242-43 Robert de Say held a knight’s fee of the old feoffment 

of him ;!8 and in 1242-43 a fifth of a knight’s fee of the old feoff- 

ment was held of him in Church Oakley.4 Certainly the former 
and probably the latter! interest had descended to him from 
his ancestor Nicholas d’Estuteville.

On 17 April 1242 the king gave him respite for the service 
of 3 knights’ fees until the king went to Gascony, so that he 
should do service there ; and the sheriffs of Essex and Notting- 
hamshire were instructed accordingly. But before 12 Feb. 1243-4 
his lands had been taken into the king’s hand, the sheriffs of 
those two counties and Derbyshire being ordered to allow him 
his stocks therein ;? and on the following 29 March the custody 
of his lands was committed to Bernard of Savoy for the use of 
prince Edward.* Bernard had the custody on 5 July 1245 ;* but 
on 10 July he was ordered to deliver seisin to John, to whom the 
king had made a restoration, the lands having originally been 
taken into the king’s hand by reason of the inquisition concerning 
the lands of aliens ;> and on 15 Oct. Bernard was ordered to 
comply with the king’s gift to John of all the corn and hay for 
the past year.® In 1255 he was granted a licence to give a moiety 
of his manor of Dedham, held in chief, to his daughter Alice in 
frank-marriage ;’ and on 31 May of that year, described as a 
knight, he presented his son John to a moiety of the church of 
Eckington.® It is probable that he can be identified with the 
John de Stutevill who on 11 April 1258 had licence to cross the 
seas to defend the cause which the abbot of Marmoutier had 
against him, provided he sent two of his sons in his place to the 
king’s army of Wales.® 

In a record of 1261 it is stated that he died on 15 Aug. 
1258, when his land, held in chief, namely a moiety of the manor 
of Dedham, passed into the king’s hand and was subsequently 
* delivered to Ralph [? recte Robert], his son and heir. On 27 Aug. 
1258 Agnes his widow was given leave to dwell with her family 
in his manor of Kirkby [in Ashfield] until the octave of Michaelmas, 
the term fixed by the king for doing justice to Robert his son 
and heir in respect of the lands of his inheritance." Agnes can 
be identified as the Agnes de Stuteville who in June 1272 made a 
gift to the abbey of Valmont with the consent of her sons Sir 
Robert, Sir Wilham, Sir Stutus (Estoldus) and master John, 
canon of Rouen.“ The evidence given below under the last of 
these sons suggests that master John’s mother was a sister of the 
vicomte de Chateau-Dun. It is recorded in the account of that 
family that Geoffrey IV, vicomte de Chateau-Dun, had a sister 
named Isabel, described as the second wife of Jean sire d’Estoute- 
ville, whose widow she was in 1259.% Although the christian 
names do not correspond the evidence makes it highly probable

In addition to his inherited possessions in Normandy and 
England, Robert d’Estouteville acquired lands in Northumber- 
land as the second husband of Eleanor de Genoure, a kinswoman 
of Queen Eleanor of Provence. After the death in 1272-73 of 
Roger Bertram III of Mitford, Northumberland, Agnes his daughter 
and heir gave Mitford and Felton to Queen Eleanor, in whose 
chamber she had been brought up.2. At a date not later than 
26 May 1275 Queen Eleanor granted to Sir Alexander de Baliol® 
in frank-marriage with Eleanor his wife the castle and vill of 
Mitford with Felton and Molesden, which the queen had of the 
inheritance of Sir Roger Bertram, to hold in chief to them and 
Eleanor’s heirs by Alexander or any other husband.* After 
Alexander’s death in 1278 without issue, and not later than 30 
Oct. 1281, Eleanor married Robert d’Estouteville. On the latter 
date Robert surrendered to the king the manors of Kirkby in Ash- 
field’ and Dedham, Essex, with knights’ fees and advowsons, except 
the homage and service of William, Robert’s brother, and his heirs 
due to Robert for the manor of Eckington ; and the king under- 
took to enfeoff Robert and Eleanor de Baliol his wife and the 
heirs of their bodies with reversion to Robert’s heirs. On 29 
Nov. Robert d’Estouteville and Eleanor de Genoure (Gebunun) 
his wife were duly granted the two manors with that limitation ;? 
and four years later, on 22 Nov. 1285, a similar grant was made 
of the manor of Eckington.® In 1284, having disobeyed the king’s 
summons for the last Welsh expedition, he was pardoned for a 
payment of 100 marks for every knight’s fee held, it being found 
that John his father had held 15 fees of the barony of Hubert 
son of Ralph, and that Robert held 5 fees of the inheritance of 
Eleanor de Genevere his wife, making a total of 20 fees.® 

On several occasions before the outbreak of the war with 
France in 1294 Robert and Eleanor were given letters for the 
appointment of attorneys during their absence overseas—on 
23 April 1282; 2 Jan. 1284-5; 25 Feb. 1285-6; 3 May 1289; 
23 April 1291; and 20 Jan. 1291-2° On the outbreak of war

THE STUTEVILLE FAMILIES 61 


Robert adhered to the king of France. His English lands were 
taken into the king’s hand, who for that reason made four 
presentations to a moiety of the church of Eckington in the 
period 1295 to 1303.1 Eleanor evidently remained in England, 
for on 12 Dec. 1298, described as the king’s kinswoman, she was 
granted the manor of Eckington, taken into the king’s hand as 
her husband Robert was an alien and of the power of the king 
of France, to hold at will for the maintenance of herself and her 
children ; and on 21 Dec. had licence to demise it for two years.” 
On 16 July 1299, following the recent truce, Robert was among 
those pledged to the king of France for English prisoners.® 
His English lands still remained in the king’s hand ;* and on 
18 Aug. the captal de Bouche and Poncius, lord of Chatillon, 
were appointed to all the lands which he held in England before 
the war, to hold for their maintenance in accordance with the 
ordinance for the Gascons.® He evidently returned to England 
after the peace in 1303 and recovered his lands ; for on 20 May 
1304 he made a fine of 60s. for having a writ of attorneys general 
for Eleanor his wife, while staying beyond seas, to last for two 
years ;® and on 29 July of that year it was found that John de 
Longford held the manor of Barlborough, co. Derby, of him by 
the service of 5 knights’ fees and doing suit at his court at 
Eckington.’ 

Robert d’Estouteville died shortly before 14 April 1306 ; 
and at his death he held Mitford castle and two-thirds of the 
forest of Felton in right of Eleanor de Genouere his wife; the 
manors of Eckington, co. Derby, and Kirkby in Ashfield, co. 
Nottingham, each held by the service of a knight’s fee ; the manor 
of Dedham, Essex, jointly with his wife, in chief by the service 
of a knight’s fee ; and a messuage and land in Gresham, Norfolk,® 
held of the heir of John de Warenne for 2 knights’ fees, and 
12 acres held of Roger le Bigod ; his heir was John, his son by 
Eleanor, aged twenty-four or twenty-five, born in remote parts.® 

In the same year Eleanor presented petitions to Parliament 
to have undamaged her third of the manor of Bywell, Northumber-

Thus John, the son of Robert d’Estouteville by his wife 
Eleanor de Genoure, inherited the English lands of his parents. 
But the following evidence makes it clear that Robert’s heir to 
his Norman lands was the son of his son Robert, who can be 
presumed to have been his son by his first wife Joan.® In the 
account of the proceedings in a case in which Robert and Eleanor 
were involved in the period 1284-93, arising from a dispute over 
the seisin of a tenement in Northumberland, which led to blood- 
shed, there is mention of Robert son of Robert de Stuteville and 
his groom.” Again, in 1304 when Robert had his writ of attorneys 
general for his wife on going overseas, a similar writ was issued

for Alice widow of Robert de Stuteville the younger ;! and on

that in 1285 a Robert d’Estouteville married Alix Bertrand de 
Bricquebec, and that in 1311 the heir of Valmont was a younger 
Robert, then under age, the son of dame Alix Bertram.3 

It can be deduced, therefore, that on his death in 1306 
Robert d’Estouteville was succeeded in his Norman possessions 
by his grandson Robert, son and heir of Robert his son by his 
first wife Joan. From his grandson descended the later Estoute- 
villes of Valmont. . In the early part of the fifteenth century 
their representative was Louis d’Estouteville, the defender of 
Le Mont St. Michel against the English, and the husband of the 
heiress of the Paynels of Hambye;* his brother was the well- 
known Cardinal. In the following century Adrienne, daughter 
and heir of Jean d’Estouteville, married Francois de Bourbon ; 
and in 1534 the duchy d’Estouteville was created in their favour.

THE STUTEVILLE FAMILIES 65 


ROBERT D’ESTOUTEVILLE, son and heir, was still in the 
wardship of Thomas Wake in March 1334-5... But when he 
attained full age the lands of aliens were taken into the king’s 
hand in view of the approaching war. On 25 July 1337 the king 
granted the manor of Dedham to Robert de Ufford, lately created 
earl of Suffolk ; and on 25 Sept. the manors of Kirkby in Ashfield 
and Eckington, lately held by John d’Estouteville, by birth of 
the parts of Normandy, to Hugh de Audley, lately created earl 
of Gloucester, Margaret his wife and the heirs male of his body.’ 
On 1 March 1339-40 the reversion of those two manors, escheats 
by the forfeiture of Robert son and heir of John d’Estouteville, 
was granted to John Darcy ‘le cousyn’.3 


Thus closed the interest of the younger branch of the 
Estoutevilles of Valmont in the inheritance of their ancestress 
Leonia daughter of Edward of Salisbury, which had descended 
to them from the twelfth century. 


APPENDIX A. 
GEOFFREY MURDAC AND THE CLAIMS OF THURSTAN DE MONTFORT 


In 1115-18 Geoffrey Murdac held 1 carucate 6 bovates in 
Kettleby Thorpe and Bigby, co. Lincoln.4 This holding evidently 
represented almost the entire tenancy-in-chief in 1086 of Hugh 
son of Baldric of 10 bovates in Kettleby Thorpe with a berewick 
of 54 bovates in Bigby.® If this holding of Hugh son of Baldric 
passed with other holdings to Robert de Stuteville I, it can be 
suggested that after the latter’s forfeiture in 1106 Geoffrey 
Murdac acquired the holding as a tenancy-in-chief. 

Geoffrey Murdac was the first witness to archbishop 
Thurstan’s charter to the men of Beverley, 1115-28 ;° and in 
1128 witnessed the charter of king David and archbishop Thurstan’s 
declaration relating to the consecration of the bishop of St. 
Andrews.” In 1130 he rendered account of 15 marks of silver 
imposed by the justices at Blyth,® which indicates his tenure of 
lands in Yorkshire. He was the donor of 14 carucates in Sutton 
by Stamford Bridge to St. Mary’s abbey,® a gift which was sub- 
sequently confirmed by Robert de Stuteville ITI.

The following proceedings show that he had a daughter 
Juliana who married Thurstan de Montfort! In Michaelmas

claimed a moiety of the vill of Langton [E.R.] with its appur- 
tenances against Eustace de Vescy as his right and inheritance 
by descent from Geoffrey Murdac, who was seised thereof, to 
Juliana his (Geoffrey's) daughter, who had held that land as 
her reasonable share which fell to her, and from her to Robert 
her son, and from Robert to Henry his brother, father of Thurstan 
the plaintiff, to whom it ought to descend, as he said ; the case 
was postponed. Thurstan the younger was also engaged in a 
plea of land with Nicholas de Stuteville in Yorkshire.2 In Easter 
term 1210 Nicholas de Stuteville gave the king 300 marks and 
5 palfreys for the maintenance of his father’s charter which he 
had relating to certain lands claimed by Thurstan de Munford 
against him, and for having peace accordingly unless the king’s 
court should consider that he ought to answer ;4 and in Michaelmas 
term a day was-given them to hear judgment on 1 Dec. The 
judgment is not recorded. The scope of Thurstan’s claims is 
shown by an entry on the Pipe Roll for the year ending at 
Michaelmas 1208, when he owed 50 marks for having two writs, 
one against Eustace de Stuteville to restore a moiety of a knight’s 
fee in Little Weighton (Witton) and [North] Cave ;§ and the 
other against Nicholas de Stuteville to restore a moiety of the 
vills of Cottingham, Buttercrambe, Scrayingham and Langton, 
and a moiety of the service of 8 knights in Hessle (Hese) and other 
vills.’ 

These details suggest that Thurstan’s claim against Nicholas 
was for a moiety of the lands held in chief by Robert de Stuteville 
III in 1166.8 The basis of his claim was evidently the same as 
that of his claim against Eustace de Vescy for a moiety of the 
manor of Langton,’ that is to say by descent from Juliana daughter 
of Geoffrey Murdac. The course of his statement in the Langton 
case suggests that Geoffrey had another daughter besides Juliana ; 
and Farrer has supposed that Erneburga, wife of Robert de 
Stuteville II and mother of Robert de Stuteville III, was her 
sister. To this, however, there is a serious objection ; for of the

APPENDIX A 67 


places mentioned in Thurstan’s claim against Nicholas de Stute- 
ville certainly Buttercrambe, Scrayingham and Langton had been 
held by Robert de Stuteville I before his forfeiture in 1106 ;! and 
therefore Robert de Stuteville III’s interest, which was sub- 
sequently inherited by Nicholas his younger son, must have been 
derived from Robert de Stuteville I, his grandfather, and not as 
an inheritance from his mother Erneburga. 

It is significant that Thurstan de Montfort, Juliana’s husband, 
held an interest in Great Ayton where he gave a rent from his 
mills to Guisborough priory. But this interest passed to the 
Stutevilles as an under-tenancy of the Meinil fee; and Farrer 
has stated that there is no evidence that any of the lands in 
Yorkshire held by Thurstan descended in his line.? The balance 
of evidence on this point suggests that Thurstan lost his interest 
in at least some part of the inheritance of his wife Juliana when 
Robert de Stuteville III recovered a portion of the possessions 
of his grandfather Robert de Stuteville I, and that Thurstan’s 
remaining interest did not survive his death. The claims made 
by his grandson were unsuccessful, though the pedigree which 
he put forward appears to be precisely correct. 

The Montfort family did not, however, abandon their 
claims ; and in Jan. 1240-1 fines were levied by which Peter de 
Montfort, claiming a moiety of the manors of Cottingham, 
Buttercrambe and Scrayingham, received sums of 100. and 
100 marks from the Stuteville representatives in return for his 
quitclaims.°® 

Taken as a whole the evidence suggests that after Robert 
de Stuteville I’s forfeiture in 1106 a considerable portion of his 
lands was acquired by Geoffrey Murdac, just as another con- 
siderable portion was acquired by Nigel d’Aubigny ; but that, 
although the latter’s portion was inherited by his Mowbray 
descendants as a tenancy in chief (not without prolonged disputes), 
Geoffrey Murdac’s portion was lost, either by him or his heirs, 
when a restoration was made to Robert de Stuteville ITT.

CHAPTER II. 
THE LANDS OF THE STUTEVILLE FEE 


No member of the Stuteville family appears among the 
tenants-in-chief, nor with any certainty among their under- 
tenants, in the Domesday survey. MSBut shortly afterwards a 
considerable number of holdings had passed to Robert de Stute- 
ville I. Of his holdings in Yorkshire a large proportion had been 
held by Hugh son of Baldric in 1086. In his introduction to the 
Yorkshire Domesday Farrer collected the available details which 
concern Hugh son of Baldric, and showed that he was of Norman 
origin.t Described as Hugh son of Baldric, sheriff of York, he 
gave the tithe of Stratfield, Hampshire, and Shaw, Berkshire, to 
the abbey of Préaux, dio. Lisieux, his gift being confirmed by 
William I, c. 1080 ;? and he is mentioned in charters of Gerold 
‘miles Christi’ to the abbey of St. Amand, Rouen, in one as a 
witness and in the other as apparently possessing an interest 
in one of Gerold’s benefactions.4 The name Baldric was not | 
uncommon in Normandy in the eleventh century ; and there are 
several mentions of the name in Orderic. There was, for instance, 
a Baldric Teutonicus who came with Wigerius his brother to 
Normandy to serve Richard II, duke of Normandy from 996 to 
1026.5 Orderic records that he had six sons, the eldest being 
Nicholas de Baschevilla [Bacqueville-en-Caux],@ and another 
Baldric de Balgenzeio [Bocquencé].’ A Nicholas de Bascavilla is 
named in a charter to St. Amand, Rouen, in company with 
William duke of Normandy, not later than 1066.8 In the period 
1066-87 a certain Baldric quitclaimed to that house the service 
of two knights which the nuns owed for 40 days yearly for the 
fee of Bacqueville (Bascheville), until he or his heirs rendered 
3011. which he owed to them for his sister Elizabeth, made a nun 
there ;§ and Orderic records that a Baldric, who possessed the

priory of Bacqueville in 1133, and in the following year he gave the church

honour of Bocquencé with Wiger his brother, gave his sister 
Elizabeth to Fulk de Bonneval in marriage, and that Fulk gave 
the church of St-Nicholas [-des-Laitiers],) which Baldric’s father 
had built, to the abbey of St-Evroul in the time of abbot Robert, 
1059-61.2, There are, too, references to a Baldric son of Nicholas, 
who in 1085 witnessed the determination of a suit between 
Gulbert d’Auffay and the abbey of Fécamp in the presence of 
king William,’ and in 1087 left the duke’s service and went to 
Spain, his lands being forfeited and subsequently restored.4 

It seems, therefore, that we have three generations—Baldric 
Teutonicus, his son Nicholas de Bacqueville, and the latter’s son® 
Baldric, whose approximate years of birth may have been c. 975, 
c. 1000, c. 1025. It is tempting to suggest that Hugh son of Baldric 
was a younger son of this younger Baldric ; but for this there are 
chronological difficulties, seeing that Hugh was sheriff of York- 
shire as early as 1069 and was old enough to have two married 
daughters in 1086. At the same time it is not impossible to 
suppose, in view of the St. Amand evidence, that he belonged to 
that family. Moreover, it can be noticed, for what the evidence 
may be worth, that Bacqueville hes about 74 and 9 miles res- 
pectively from Le Bourg-Dun and Arques, mentioned in the charter 
of Gerold ‘miles Christi’; and that at Gonneville-les-Hameaux, 
cant. Totes (much the same distance from Bacqueville) a gift of 
land which had been held by a Walter son of Baldric was made to 
the priory of Longueville Having regard to the association of 
these names Walter and Baldric, it is curious to find that in 
Yorkshire a certain Walter son of Baldric de Cotingham witnessed 
a charter to Rievaulx abbey ;§ and that a Baldric de Cothigham 


of Bacqueville to the abbey of Tiron by a charter which was witnessed and 
assented to by his three brothers of whom one was named Baldric (Cal. 
Docs. France, nos. 1008-9)—a fact which suggests that his mother may 
have been of the ‘Baldric’ family.

(who may have been his father) witnessed a charter which was 
also witnessed by several members of the Stuteville family.1 As 
Cottingham was one of the principal holdings of Hugh son of 
Baldric which passed to the Stutevilles a coincidence of this kind 
must not be altogether disregarded. 


§2. 


In England Hugh son of Baldric was an important tenant- 
in-chief in Yorkshire, and to a smaller extent in Lincolnshire ; he 
also held two manors in Nottinghamshire, single holdings in 
Wiltshire and Berkshire,? and interests in four holdings in 
Hampshire. The latter included Church Oakley and Stratfield, 
which after being known as Stratfield Stuteville became Stratfield 
Saye. It is of special importance for our present purpose to 
notice that these places were held by the Estoutevilles of Valmont. 
It can be deduced that Nicholas d’Estouteville their ancestor 
was of the same family as Robert de Stuteville I, and that the 
latter was probably the successor of Hugh son of Baldric in 
these places as he was in so large a portion of Hugh’s holdings in 
Yorkshire.’ 

Farrer has given evidence on which he based his suggestion 
that Hugh son of Baldric died shortly after the completion of the 
Survey and whilst the Summary was being compiled.4 Although 
a forfeiture must not be ruled out, it seems clear that he was 
not in possession of his holdings after the Conqueror’s death. 
The Survey shows that he had then two sons-in-law, one, named 
Walter, who was his tenant at Marden, Wiltshire,® and the other, 
Guy [de Craon], who held of him a manor of 3 bovates in Sloothby, 
and a manor of a carucate in Claxby St. Andrew, with soke 
amounting to 8 carucates 1 bovate in six places named, all in 
the wapentake of Calcewath, co. Lincoln. That makes a total of 
94 carucates ; and it is recorded in the Lindsey survey that Alan 
de Craon held as a tenant-in-chief 10 carucates in that wapentake.? 
Later evidence shows that places in Lincolnshire held by the 
Craon family in chief correspond with those held by Guy de 
Craon of Hugh son of Baldric in 1086.8 But there is no evidence 
that Alan de Craon held any other of Hugh’s possessions in 
Lincolnshire ;? and certainly none of Hugh’s holdings in Yorkshire

LANDS OF THE STUTEVILLE FEE 73 


passed to the Craon family. Moreover, in South Warnborough, 
Hampshire, where Alan de Craon made a gift to Frieston priory, 
co. Lincoln,? the interest in 1086 had been held of Hugh by Guy 
[de Craon] with Hugh’s daughter.? It can be deduced, therefore, 
that none of Hugh’s holdings passed to his issue except those 
which, in his lifetime, had formed the maritagium of one or 
other of his two daughters. 


The details relating to Hugh’s tenancy-in-chief in Lincoln- 
shire show that it consisted mainly of 20 small manors, which, 
with the berewicks and sokeland attached, had a total of about 
30 carucates. The descent of two of these manors, Kettleby 
Thorpe and Bigby, which were later held by the Stutevilles in 
chief, has been discussed in Appendix A above. Hugh also had 
a holding in the city of Lincoln which certainly passed to Robert 
de Stuteville 14 


In Yorkshire Hugh son of Baldric held about 50 manors 
with many berewicks and sokeland, assessed at approximately 
410 carucates. The greater part of these holdings passed, pre- 
sumably by royal grant, to Robert de Stuteville I; and after his 
forfeiture in 1106 they were taken into the king’s hand. Many 
of them,‘ including Cottingham and apparently Scrayingham and 
Buttercrambe, were restored to Robert de Stuteville III in the 
reign of Stephen ; and at an earlier date, in the reign of Henry I, 
a substantial portion of the remainder was granted to Nigel 
d’Aubigny, from whom it was inherited by his son Roger de 
Mowbray, forming part of the honour of Mowbray. These Mowbray 
lands in Yorkshire included (1) those which were acquired by 
Robert de Stuteville III, early in the reign of Henry II, as an 
under-tenancy of Roger de Mowbray, forming part of the initial 
compromise of the claims brought by Robert for all the possessions 
of his grandfather which had passed to the honour of Mowbray ; 
and (ii) those in which the descendants of Robert de Stuteville I 
never recovered any interest, either as a tenancy-in-chief or as 
an under-tenancy.

of king Henry II, shows that he had acquired these 12 carucates

Nottingham and Northampton, which subsequently formed part 
of the honour of Mowbray ; but this is by no means conclusive.? 
§3. 

At a date subsequent to 1166 several additions were made 
to the tenancy-in-chief then held by Robert de Stuteville III. 
He himself obtained from the king, perhaps at an earlier date, 
land in Cold Kirby. His son William received several grants 
from Henry II, including the important manors of Knaresborough 
and Aldborough ; but William’s interest did not survive the death 
of his son Robert while still a minor, and a detailed examination 
of their components is outside the scope of the present volume.® 
He also acquired an interest in Bramham and Clifford, which passed 
to the Wakes with his Stuteville inheritance. At some date 
about 1174 Nicholas de Stuteville I, William’s younger brother, 
was granted the honour of Liddel in Cumberland, accompanied 
by lands in Cleveland, with which it had earlier been associated, 
known as the honour of Rosedale. These two honours became 
incorporated with the other Stuteville tenancies-in-chief under 
Nicholas I, as heir to his nephew Robert, and with them descended 
to the Wakes of Liddel. The final settlement of the Mowbray 
dispute in 1201 increased the original under-tenancy of 10 knights’ 
fees by a further 9 knights’ fees and land in Brinklow, co. Warwick.’ 
In the thirteenth century onwards the returns of fees and various 
inquisitions after death give a detailed, though by no means a 
comprehensive or consistent picture of the Stuteville possessions 
which passed to the Wakes; and an examination is necessary 
before any general deductions can be suggested.

Except for some references to the honour of Liddel' and to 
the holding in [Kettleby] Thorpe and Bigby, co. Lincoln,? the 
returns entered in the Book of Fees throw no appreciable light 
on the tenancies-in-chief of the main English branch of the 
Stuteville family ;3 for there are no Yorkshire returns comparable 
to the detailed returns of 1212 and 1242-43 for Lincolnshire and 
other counties.4 But several references relate to the Stuteville 
under-tenancy of the honour of Mowbray. There are lists for 
several counties of the fees held of Nigel de Mowbray, who was in 
possession of the honour from 1224 to 1230.2 In Yorkshire 
Nicholas de Stuteville [II] held of him 3 fees in Erton’, Aton’ 
and elsewhere,* and 2 carucates in Glapham’ where 12 carucates 
made a knight’s fee ; in the return for Warwickshire and Leicester- 
shire Nicholas held 74 fees and 4/,; part in Thrussington and 
Sysonby ; in Buckinghamshire $ fee in Burston ;8 and in Sussex 
2 fees, the place or places being left blank. A few years later, in 
1242-43, among the fees held of Roger de Mowbray, Nigel’s 
successor, in Yorkshire, Joan Wake is given as holding 3 fees ; 
but their locality is not recorded.® Additional light is thrown by 
other returns. Thus, in 1235-36 Roger de Welton ‘pro feodo de 
Mubray de parvo feodo de Mortuna’ [Mortain] was assessed on 
one fee in Staverton, one in Welton and one in Grimscote, all 
co. Northampton ;!° and in 1242-43, among Roger de Mowbray’s 
tenancy-in-chief in that county, Roger de Whelton held these 3 
- fees in the same places of Joan Wake.™ Again, in 1235-36, Thomas 
de Wappebury held a knight’s fee in Wappenbury and another in 
Newbold [Revel], co. Warwick ; and also 4 fee of Mowbray in 
Thrussington, where Gilbert de Houby also held a fee, and Hugh 
de Pereres held a fee in Sysonby and Welby, co. Leicester.!2 There 
is also a record of a holding in 1242-43 by Adam de Welton of 
the honour of Mortain in part of Burston, co. Buckingham 
(Bridelesthon’).8

78 STUTEVILLE ‘FRE 


After the death of Joan de Stuteville inquisitions were held 
in Cumberland and Yorkshire, following the writ of 6 April 1276.4 
She held the barony and forest of Liddel and demesne lands in 
Arthuret and other places named, where several tenants held at 
money rents, and the advowson of Easton, all being held of the 
king in chief by the service of 56s. cornage at the king’s exchequer 
at Carlisle and suit at Cumberland county court; and of the 
same tenure Cropton and Middleton, co. York, which Nicholas 
Wake held of her by service unknown. In Yorkshire she held the 
manor of Kirkby Moorside, of which an extent was given, of the 
heir of Mowbray by the service of 10 knights, of which manor 
she had enfeoffed her son Baldwin two years previously by charter. 
She also held in chief the manor of Cottingham, of which an 
extent was given, with Buttercrambe, Scrayingham and Langton, 
by the service of 3 knights; and of these Baldwin Wake held 
Buttercrambe and Scrayingham, and John de Vescy the manor of 
Langton, so that she only held Cottingham in demesne at her 
death. She also held the advowsons of Cottingham, Rowley, Etton, 
Scrayingham and Middleton, and there are details of her bondmen 
and beasts in Cottingham, Hull [Bank], Newland, Wolfreton [in 
Kirk Ella], and Newton [in Cottingham]. But there is no record 
of the knights’ fees held of her, and there are no returns for 
Warwickshire or other counties. 


Information in greater detail is given in the several extents 
made in March 1281-2 after the death of Baldwin Wake.? These 
include his inherited Wake possessions, principally the manor of 
Bourne, co. Lincoln, with a large number of knights’ fees spread 
over several counties, which are outside the scope of the present 
inquiry. Of the Stuteville lands which he inherited from his 
mother, he held the manor of Liddel, of which a full extent was 
given, tenants in a large number of places holding their lands by 
cornage amounting to 56s. yearly, and a knight’s fee being held 
in Drigg in Copeland. He held the manor of [Great] Ayton in 
Cleveland of Sir Nicholas de Meinil in chief for half a knight’s 
fee, with lands there and in Thornaby and Hemlington, where, 
in the latter place, the widow of Robert de Stuteville was holding 
the manor in dower.? The following knights’ fees are given as 
pertaining to the manor of Cottingham, where 8 carucates were 
_ held in demesne :—

To these are added the manor of Brinklow, co. Warwick, 
held by Gilbert de Witteleburi for a sore sparrowhawk or 2s. 
yearly ; and the following knights’ fees, which certainly did not 
pertain to Kirkby Moorside, but relate to the tenure-in-chief of 
the honour of Rosedale :' Cropton and Middleton, 1 k.f. held 
by Nicholas Wake ; Wrelton, Aislaby and Middleton, $ k.f. held 
by Roger de Wrelton and his parceners ; Wrelton, 4 k.f. held by 
Bastace de Peérte. 

Several details relating to the Stuteville lands are given in 
the inquisitions held after the death of Roger de Mowbray 
following the writ of 21 Nov. 1297.2, They include extents of such 
manors as Hovingham and Thirsk which had been held by Robert 
de Stuteville I in chief, but in which his descendants never 
recovered any interest. On the other hand they show that the 
Stuteville under-tenancy, then held by John son of Baldwin 
Wake, included 10 knights’ fees in Kirkby Moorside, Gillamoor 
and Fadmoor ; 44 fees in Wappenbury, co. Warwick ; one fee in 
Little Dalby, Welby and Sysonby ; and another in Hoby, all co. 
Leicester. Other holdings include 5 carucates in Slingsby for 
+ k.f., and 4 carucates in Muscoates for 4 k.f.3 

The inquisitions held after the death of Thomas Wake of 
Liddel on 30 May 1349? give very full details of his holdings, and 
for many of these which can be identified as his Stuteville inheri- 
tance a continuity from earlier times is remarkable. They include 
the castle and manor of Liddel at the original cornage rent of 
56s.; the manors of Cottingham and [Little] Weighton held in 
chief by the service of a barony and finding a mounted esquire to 
carry the king’s hauberk in the Welsh war for forty days if there 
should be war in Wales ; the manor of Buttercrambe with Scraying- 
ham in chief for a knight’s fee ; the manor of Langton in chief 
for a knight’s fee ; a list of knights’ fees in Yorkshire which include 
several holdings showing their continuity from Robert de Stute- 
ville’s carta of 1166 and the fees given in Baldwin Wake’s in- 
quisition ;> the manors of Cropton and Middleton in chief for 
half a knight’s fee ;* the manor of [Great] Ayton, partly in chief

Lastly, the inquisitions held after the death of John de 
Mowbray on 4 Oct. 13618 record certain holdings which can be 
identified as forming part of the Stuteville under-tenancy. Thus 
half a knight’s fee in South Ferriby, co. Lincoln, a member of the 
manor of Epworth, was held by the heir of the earl of Kent; a 
knight’s fee in Little Dalby, Welby and Sysonby, another in Hoby, 
co. Leicester, and another in Thrussington and Ratcliffe on the 
- Wreak, were held by Thomas Wake, who also held 2 knights’ 
fees in Newbold on Avon, Cosford and Long Lawford, and 4 
knights’ fees in Wappenbury, Princethorpe and Eathorpe, co. 
Warwick, and also 3 knights’ fees in Welton, Staverton and 
Grimscote, co. Northampton. The total for these is 124 knights’ 
fees.4 

Many details bearing on the Wake tenancies-in-chief and 
their under-tenancies of the honour of Mowbray in Yorkshire can 
be found in the 1284-85 returns known as Kirkby’s Inquest and 
the later Yorkshire inquisitions of 1302-1303, 1346 and 1428, 
all of which are printed in Feudal Atds, vol. vi. Some of them, 
which are often useful in identifying the location of the knights’ 
fees recorded in the 1166 carta of Robert de Stuteville III, will 
be cited in the accounts of the various sections in Chapter IV of 
the present volume. In certain respects, however, especially when 
the tenancy-in-chief is the point at issue, they must be used with 
care ; and examples of statements which are in conflict with other

In 1284-85 there is a section for the Stuteville fee in the 
wapentake of Harthill, giving certain details for the tenancy-in- 
chief held by the heir of Cottingham, 7.e. John son of Baldwin 
Wake. The places mentioned are Cottingham itself,? North Cave, 
Hotham, Brantingham, Riplingham, Drewton, Hunsley, [Little] 
Weighton, Willerby, Etton, Hessle, and Swanland. Except for the 
knight’s fee in Etton and a quarter of a knight’s fee in Hessle, the 
details recorded are confined to a carucage basis ; and amounts of 
land forming under-tenancies, with in many cases the names of 
the tenants, are included. There is also a record of the knight’s 
fee constituting the honour of Rosedale, held by John son of 
Baldwin Wake, in the king’s wardship, which consisted of 5 car. 
in Cropton, 2 car. in Wrelton, 2 car. in Aislaby, 3 car. 3 bov. in 
Middleton, and-2 car. in Cawthorn, a total of 14 car. 3 bov., a 
large number of under-tenancies being specified.4 There are also 
some details, by no means complete, relating to the manor of 
Kirkby Moorside and its appurtenances; these show that the 
heirs of Wake held of Roger de Mowbray 4 car. in Kirkby Moor-

+ k.f. ; 4 car. in Gillamoor for 4 k.f. ; and 5 car. 7 bov. in Fadmoor

(a) those held in chief by Robert de Stuteville III, consisting

With regard to (c) only 8 fees of the old feoffment and one of 
the new are given as Robert de Stuteville III’s tenancy in Roger 
de Mowbray’s carta of 1166; but there is clear evidence that the 
number held was 10 In 1190, when William de Stuteville owed 
4l1. for the scutage of Wales, being a sum at 10s. per fee on 8 fees 
held in chief, he also accounted for 100s. for the knights of Nigel 
de Mowbray ‘quos habet in custodia’.2~ The manor of Kirkby 
Moorside, which formed the caput of this under-tenancy, was 
held by the service of 10 knights’ fees. Their composition cannot 
be given in complete detail ; but there is good evidence to show 
that, in addition to Kirkby Moorside and its appurtenances in 
Yorkshire held for 14 or 1} fees, and also temporarily the manor 
of Kilburn,’ the fee in Newbold on Avon, Cosford and Long Law- 
“fond! Co. Warwick, where an interest was granted by Robert de 
Stuteville III to John his younger brother, can be included.4 The 
balance seems to have been spread over places in the counties of 
Leicester, Northampton, Sussex and perhaps Buckingham ; and 
possibly included a knight’s fee in Hessle, Yorkshire, and South 
Ferriby, co. Lincoin.® 

With regard to (d) 44 out of the 9 knights’ fees were held by 
Richard de Wappenbury in 1201; and of these the greater part 
of his holding lay in cos. Warwick and Leicester, including 
Wappenbury itself, Newbold Revel and Thrussington ; the location 
of the other 44 fees, held by Eustace de Vescy in 1201, has not 
been determined.®

CHAPTER III. 
CHARTERS OF THE STUTEVILLE FAMILY 


The first two documents in the present chapter refer to 
gifts made by Robert de Stuteville I before his forfeiture in 1106, 
and the third to the churches of three of his manors. Of those 
manors Kirkby Moorside was the only one in which Robert de 
Stuteville IIT recovered an interest, his interest being a subordinate 
tenure of the honour of Mowbray. The next section (nos. 4 to 61) 
comprises charters issued by or in favour of Robert de Stuteville 
III, who died in 1183, and his descendants.! The lands concerned 
include those which he recovered, either as tenures in chief or as 
subordinate tenures of the honour of Mowbray, such as the manor 
of Kirkby Moorside and its dependencies. It also includes certain 
charters relating to lands which his son William de Stuteville 
acquired independently. Later sections include charters issued by 
members of the Stuteville families of Warwickshire (nos. 62-3) 
and of Burton Agnes (nos. 64-6). Some charters issued by the 
Stutevilles of Cowesby will be printed in Chapter IV, §4. 


1. Record of the gift by Robert de Stuteville [I] to Stephen abbot 
of St. Mary’s York, of 3 carucates in Moreby [par. Stillingfleet]. 
[c. 1089-1106] 


Chartulary of St. Mary’s York (John Rylands Library, Latin MS. 
220), f. 160. 


In primis habetur carta in predicto xxvj° folio? per quam 
R[obertus] de Stuteuilla concessit tres carucatas terre in Moreby 
Stephano abbati et eyus monachis ad ecclesiam sancte Marie in 
elimosina quiete et libere ab omnibus rebus, etc. 


Other benefactions of the grantor to St. Mary’s abbey are included in 
the confirmation charter of king Henry II, 1156-57. These were a carucate 
of land in Ellenthorpe, par. Aldborough ; 12 carucates in Harton, par. Bossall; 
and tithes of his demesne of Coxwold, Hovingham, Kirkby [Moorside], 
Buttercrambe, Scrayingham, and Langton [E.R.], with a bovate in each 
vill, 2 bovates being the amount in Coxwold ; also with Robert de Meinil 
8 carucates in Myton [-upon-Swale].4 His gift of the vill of Harton was 
confirmed to the abbey by his grandson Robert de Stuteville III (no. 4). 
Robert de Stuteville I also gave land in Hunsley, par. Rowley, to Durham 
priory which was confirmed by king Henry I at Christmas 1100.4 These 
benefactions give a proof of his tenure in the places named before he forfeited 
his lands in 1106. His interest in Moreby was evidently due to his succession

In the next charter Sutton is described as ‘juxta Pontem Belli’; and 
in a charter of Stephen de Meinil I as Sutton ‘juxta Pontem de Bello in illa 
parte Deruenti fluminis que est versus civitatem Eboraci’; the place being 
identified by Farrer as a lost hamlet in Gate Helmsley.> The 3 carucates 
are represented by the moieties of 14 carucates each, one of which was 
given to the abbey by Robert de Meinil and the other subsequently by 
Geoffrey Murdac (no. 70 below and note). Although there is no direct proof, 
it is likely that Robert de Stuteville I held an interest in at least part of 
Sutton by Stamford Bridge, and that he was given this, together with the 
vill of Harton, by reason of their proximity to his manor of Buttercrambe. 
In 1086 the king held a manor of 12 carucates in Harton and two holdings 
of 14 carucates each in Sutton.°®

he brought against Nicholas de Stuteville, the grantor’s son, in Michaelmas 
term 1207, claiming common of wood and pasture in respect of Hutton 
{le Hole] and Spaunton, belonging to his free tenement in those vills as the 
right of his church, of which abbot Savaric had been seised in the time of 
Henry II. Nicholas said that he had nothing in demesne in the vills, which 
he had confirmed to the abbot as far as the river Dove, but that the claim 
was beyond the appurtenances of the vills, namely in Farndale, and of this 
he put himself on the great assize. <A licence for a concord was accepted ; 
and the fine was accordingly levied on 20 Oct. 1207, by which Nicholas 
granted the common of wood and pasture in pure and perpetual alms with all 
things contained in his father’s charter.’ 

Some earlier charters relating to Hutton le Hole are given in Chapter 
V, including a charter of Hugh son of Baldric and Roger de Mowbray’s 
confirmation to St. Mary’s of the vill within specified bounds (nos. 133, 
136). For the gift of tithes made by Robert de Stuteville I, confirmed 
in the last clause, see the note to charter no. 1. 

The absence of witnesses makes it impossible to assign any approximate 
date for this charter, but it was evidently issued not earlier than the feoff- 
ment of the manor of Kirkby Moorside made to the grantor by Roger de 
Mowbray, and therefore not earlier than the accession of Henry II.8

Some notes on Skipwith are given in Chapter LV, §2. King William I 
apparently included the church in his gifts to William, bishop of Durham, 
who certainly gave it to Durham priory.! It was confirmed to the priory 
by archbishop Thurstan, c. 1121-1128,? and again, with 2 bovates of land, by 
Henry I! in 1158.3. It is probable that Richard Murdac was the man of that 
name who occurs in the middle of the twelfth century, and was the pre- 
decessor of Robert Murdac, a tenant of Robert de Stuteville III in 1166 ; 
see Chapter LV, §2. The charter may have been issued soon after Robert de 
Stuteville III recovered a portion of his grandfather’s holdings.

Several charters relating to the lost vill of Hoveton and to Welburn, 
with some notes on the boundaries here described, are printed in Chapter 
V. The present charter was evidently issued after Robert de Stuteville IIL 
was granted the manor of Kirkby Moorside by Roger de Mowbray ; and 
it was presumably later than the gifts of Hoveton and Welburn which 
Roger himself had made in the period 1154-57 (nos. 125, 151). 

Robert dean of Helmsley, evidently the rector of Helmsley and rural 
dean of Ryedale, witnessed charters with Jeremy archdeacon of Cleveland, 
who held that office from c. 1170 to 1189 ;4 and, as Robert de Helmsley, 
dean of Ryedale, he witnessed a charter c. 1181-1189.° Mag. Geoffrey de 
Duua (for whom see the note to no. 12) witnessed a charter in the period 
1165-75, and another dated 1166, which was also witnessed by Richard 
Cruer and Reginald de Cap[etot],”7 both of whom witnessed the present 
charter. It seems safe, therefore, to assign c. 1160 as its earliest date,® 
especially if the fifth witness was the grantor’s younger son Nicholas.

animalia sua et oves et porcos cum vaccaria sua ibidem et terra culta

It is usually stated that the priory of Keldholme was founded by 
Robert de Stuteville in the reign of Henry 1.3 The only evidences for this 
early date appear to be a statement made by the prioress at the Quo Waranto 
proceedings of 1278-81 that Henry the king, son of the Conqueror, issued 
a charter to the nuns which she produced ;* and, secondly, a statement 
that there was a prioress named Sibyl living in that reign.» With regard 
to the first, any corroboration is lacking ; and it is significant that king John 
in his confirmation (no. 13) refers to a charter of his own father, Henry II, 
and not to any charter issued by Henry I, and gives as the principal donor 
Robert de Stuteville, with the consent of his son William, which clearly 
identifies him as Robert de Stuteville III. With regard to the prioress 
Sibyl the evidence given is that she issued a charter witnessed by Stephen 
de Meinil. But there were three Stephens of that name whose periods 
respectively were early in the twelfth century, late in that century, and 
1224-69.© It so happens that a Sibyl prioress of Keldholme issued a charter 
in the last of these periods, which actually was witnessed by a Sir Stephen 
de Meyniil;’7 and it would be a strange coincidence if this was not the 
charter in question. 

The evidence taken as a whole suggests that the prioress was mistaken 
in supposing that the king’s charter that she produced, which may well 
have been a charter of Henry II confirming gifts made by Robert de Stute- 
_ville III, was one issued by Henry I] ;§ and it can be put forward as an 
alternative hypothesis that the priory was founded by Robert de Stuteville 
III, and that he issued the present charter shortly after the foundation, at 
a date subsequent to his acquisition of the manor of Kirkby Moorside 
after the accession of Henry II. The latest date for the foundation can be 
taken as 1166, for in that year Geoffrey ‘magister de Duva’ witnessed a

The site of the priory lies on the Dove less than a mile to the east of 
Kirkby Moorside. Yauenwyth seems to be identical with Revenwich’ and 
Evenewit which occur in king John’s charters (no. 13 and note), and can 
so be identified with Ravenswyke which lies less than a mile to the north of 
Keldholme. Farndale lies up the Dove to the north of Gillamoor, and 
Bransdale about 3 miles west of Farndale. The privileges granted to the 
priory, therefore, extended over a wide area. Other charters issued or 
relating to it are printed at nos. 26, 51-2, 113, 144.

William de Stuteville and everything within the bounds ‘a rogo calcis! que 
est juxta Duuam? per fossatum ipsarum sanctimonialium usque ad divisam

est inter Kirkebi Moresheved’ et Sivelington’ [Sinnington] a Chatwait? 
per vallem usque ad divisam de Parva Edeston’ et deinde usque in fluvium 
de Duua et ab illo loco fluvii de Duua per ipsum fluvium usque ad predictum 
rogum’.* Among the other gifts thereby confirmed were several in ‘Engelby’, 
2 carucates there being given by William son of Columba de Engelby.> The 
place is Ingleby Greenhow, where in the thirteenth century the prioress of 
Keldholme was holding 2 carucates (in Englebt juxta Grenhou).© It lies to 
the north of Bransdale ; and at the Domesday survey 7 carucates there 
were soke of the manor of Stokesley, which became a member of the Balliol 
fee.? In 1166 Robert de Stuteville III purchased 2 carucates there for 14 
marks, to hold for a sixth part of a knight’s fee ;* he thus became the superior 
lord of the Ingleby family, his confirmation of their gift of the 2 carucates 
being included in the first confirmation of king John.

Thornton, from whose mill a rent of a mark was given, may be the Thornton, 
wap. Harthill, where the Vescy family had an interest ;? but this cannot 
be more than a suggestion.

heirs of the knight’s fee in Sutton [under Whitestone Cliffe], 
Marderby and Laysthorpe; to hold of him as freely as he held

The knight’s fee of the old feoffment which is the subject of this and 
the next charter was held of the grantor by Geoffrey Fossard in 1166 ; and 
an account of it is given in Chapter IV, §6. The date of Geoffrey’s death 
and of the succession of Adam is not known; but the next charter shows 
that it was not later than 1183, when Robert de Stuteville died.

17. Notification by Robert the dean and the chapter of York of an 
exchange between Roger de Mowbray, Robert de Stuteville [IIT], 
and Robert de Daiville and Juliana his wife, daughter of Thurstan 
de Montfort, by which, whereas Robert de Daiville was holding 
a moiety of Langford [co. Nottingham] of Roger de Mowbray 
in fee and the other moiety of Langford with the vill of Kilburn 
in frank-marriage of Robert de Stuteville, whose sister he had 
accepted as his wife, Roger de Mowbray gave to Robert de 
Stuteville the former moiety of Langford in exchange for Kilburn ; 
and Robert de Daiville, holding Kilburn of Roger de Mowbray, 
gave it to Juliana for her dower, who quitclaimed her dower 
rights in a moiety of Langford. [1154-70] 

Reg. Mag. Album (D. and C. York), i, 73. 


Robertus decanus et capitulum ecclesie sancti Petri Ebor[acensis] 
omnibus sancte matris ecclesie filiis salutem. Sciant omnes tam presentes 
quam futuri quod in presentia nostra hoc modo factum est escambium

acceperat. Dedit autem Rogerus de Moulbrai Roberto de Stuteuill’ 
illam medietatem Landeford quam Robertus de Daiuille de eo in

98 STUTEVILLE FEE 


suus ei antea dederat in dotem, hujus escambii conventionem imper- 
petuum firmiter observandum affidarunt in manu Roberti decani 
Ebor[acensis] Rogerus de Moulbrai et Robertus de Stuteuille de se et 
heredibus suis; affidavit quoque in manu ejusdem Roberti decani 
predicta Juliana uxor Roberti de Daiuille quod nuncquam nec per 
se nec per alterum quipiam reclamabit nec nomine dotis aliquid exiget 
in prefata villa Landeford. Si quis autem horum ab hoc escambio sive 
conventione recedere voluerit nos in eum s[um]mam anathematis ex 
precepto domini archiepiscopi faciemus. Testibus hiis, Roberto 
decano Ebor[acensi], Roberto magistro scolarum, Simone de Sig[illo], 
Thoma de Rain[euille], Geroldo filio Serlonis. 


It can be deduced that before the issue of this agreement Robert de 
Stuteville III had become possessed of an interest both in Kilburn and in a 
moiety of Langford. Kilburn was held by Hugh son of Baldric in 1086, and 
passed to Nigel d’Aubigny with other lands of Robert de Stuteville I, and 
so to Roger de Mowbray.! It can be supposed, therefore, that it formed, 
like the manor of Kirkby Moorside with several berewicks, a portion of the 
under-tenancy granted by Roger de Mowbray to Robert de Stuteville III. 
With regard to Langford a manor there, of 2 carucates 3} bovates, was 
held in chief by Geoffrey de Wirce in 1086.2 This, with other land held 
by Geoffrey elsewhere, also became a member of the honour of Mowbray ; 
and the terms of the present agreement suggest that an interest in the 
manor, probably a moiety, had formed another portion of the under- 
tenancy mentioned above. In the agreement of 1201, settling the Stute- 
ville-Mowbray disputes (no. 43 below), Langford was then included among 
the places in which William de Stuteville quitclaimed all right against 
William de Mowbray, thus setting aside part of the present agreement. 

Robert de Daiville was succeeded by his son John in the period 1185-91 
(no. 137). Juliana his wife, mentioned in the present charter, was living in 
1202.3 In 1206 John de Daiville (de Auvill’) gave 40s. for a writ that William 
de Mowbray should restore Langford to him, namely a knight’s fee, which 
Robert de Daiville his father had given in marriage to Roger de Caily with 
Joan his daughter, who was dead and whose son had died without an heir, 
so that the fee ought to revert to him, the said John, as son and heir of 
Robert.‘ 

The extreme limits of date for the present agreement are 1154, being 
the earliest date for the feoffment made to Robert de Stuteville III by 
Roger de Mowbray, and 1177 when Robert, master of the schools of York, 

was drowned.> The last three witnesses were all canons of York. Gerold 
son of Serlo and Thomas de Raineville witnessed as canons, 1149-53 ;° the 
former and Simon de Sigillo, 1154-60 ;7 and all three witnessed as canons 
a charter of Robert II the dean and the chapter, 1158-62.8 As Simon de 
Sigillo had apparently ceased to be a canon by 1170 ,° that can be taken 
as the latest limit.

18. Confirmation by Robert de Stuteville [III] to the Temple of the 
Lord [at Jerusalem, 7.e. the priory of North Ferriby] of the gift 
made by John de Hessle of all his land and that of his men 
in [?] Hessle, and [?] woodland of the same breadth extending 
to the Humber. [c. 1164-1183] 

Bodleian Library MS. Add. C. 51, fragment of a North Ferriby 
Chartulary, f. 4V. 

Omnibus fidelibus visuris et audituris litteras has Robertus de 
Stuteuile salutem. Sciatis me concessisse et hac presenti carta 
mea confirmasse donacionem illam quam Johannes de Hesell’ dedit 
et concessit templo Domini et fratribus ibidem Deo servientibus, 
videlicet totam terram ejus et hominum suorum in Holflet Wria et 
grouos ejusdem latitudinis usque in Humbre fluvium in liberam et 
perpetuam elemosinam sicut carta predicti Johannis attestatur, pro 
salute anime mee et uxoris mee et patris et matris mee et omnium 
antecessorum meorum. Hiis testibus, domino Adam et domino Galfrido 
de [blank] et fratre Warino monachis, Rogero de Stuteuill’, Roberto 
Murdak, etc. 


The section, headed Hesell’, includes several charters of John de Hesell’ 
to the brethren of the house at North Ferriby ; his charter making the gift 
confirmed by Robert de Stuteville was witnessed by Simon, Osbert, and 
Philip Morinus, canons of Beverley ;! another was witnessed by Roger 
‘persona de Houden’ and others then present ‘cum domino Johanne de 
Hesell’ at the siege of Acre, handing the charter ‘in manum domini Petribricii 
qui tunc temporis erat prior ejusdem templi dominici’. John’s gifts were 
confirmed by his son Robert, who issued several other charters making 
gifts in Hessle and South Ferriby (ff. 6-8Y). Some notes on the family are 
given in Chapter LV, §17. The priory of North Ferriby was a cell of the 
Austin Canons of the order of the Temple of the Lord at Jerusalem.? 

Robert Murdac, the last witness, held a knight’s fee of the grantor in 
1166 (Chapter IV, §2).

Murton. [ante 1158]

100 STUTEVILLE FEE 


omni terreno servitio in perpetuum et per omnia communem pasturam

meam carta sua confirmante. His testibus, Alueredo presbitero, Lam-

Stulta villa. 


Murton, par. Hawnby, wap. Birdforth, held at the Survey by Robert 
Malet, became a member of the honour of Mowbray.* The family of Malebisse 
held an under-tenancy ; and the vill was given by Hugh Malebisse to Byland 
abbey, with the confirmation of Roger de Mowbray.? William his brother, 
who it can be supposed also had an interest there,# married Emma, an 
illegitimate daughter of William de Percy.* Farrer supposed that after 
the death of William Malebisse in 1176 she married a William de Stuteville;§ 
and on that assumption the grantor of the present charter was dealing with 
land of her first husband which she held in dower. 

Any theory of that kind must, however be discarded. It must be 
supposed that the present charter was issued not later than 1157, for in 
the general confirmation charter of Henry II to the abbey issued not later 
than that year there is the clause ‘ex dono Willelmi de Stutevilla quandam 
partem terre in Morthona sicut idem Willelmus illis dedit’.? Such a date 
makes it impossible for this William to have married the widow of William 
Malebisse ; and further it makes it reasonably impossible that the grantor 
was William the eldest son of Robert de Stuteville III. It is therefore likely 
that he was William, the latter’s brother, who with him claimed lands held 
by Roger de Mowbray in 1147 (see p. 5 above). 

The fact that William’s lord is given as Roger de Mowbray introduces 
the suggestion that the compromise had already been made by which Roger 
granted the 10 knights’ fees to Robert de Stuteville ; but the deduction is 
not altogether safe as the land granted by this charter may have been 
held in Emma’s right. No acceptable suggestion can be offered, however, 


for her identity.

There is no doubt that this and the ensuing charters were issued by 
William the eldest son of Robert de Stuteville IIL. The present charter 
was included in charters relating to the Brus fee, apparently in error.! 
The original gift of the church to the priory, made by Ivo de Charcheni or 
Karkeni with the consent of John [de Hessle] his son, and John de Hessle’s 
charter relating thereto, are printed at nos. 101-2; and cf. no. 18. 

The latest date for the present charter is 1186, by which year Bernard 
had become prior of Newburgh; and, if it can be assumed that William 
de Vescy was the head of that family, who died in the year ending at 
Michaelmas 1183,? that would give an earlier limit. This would show that 
the charter was issued in the lifetime of the grantor’s father. The occurrence 
of master Roger Arundel as a witness suggests 1175 as the earliest limit.3 


21. Agreement in the presence of William de Stuteville between 
Roald prior of Guisborough and Richard Duket, clerk, by which 
the latter should hold the church of Hessle, rendering 5 marks 
yearly to the priory in the name of pension, 5 marks for the 
good of peace, and the sum of 20 marks within four years which 
the priory had given to the pope for the corn of the church. 

[c. 1190-1203] 
Guisborough Chartulary, Cotton MS. Cleopatra D. ii, f. 347. Pd. 
in Guisborough Chartulary, 11, no. 1067.

salutem. Noveritis quod in presencia mea ita convenit inter Roald[um]

omnibus pertinenciis reddendo inde singulis annis ipsis priori et

de Hesel, quod bladum idem Ricardus habuit. Et in hujus rei testi- 
monium hoc scriptum feci sigillo meo signatum. 


Roald occurs as prior in 1194-98 and 1199 ;* and it is unlikely that he

102 STUTEVILLE FEE 


Interea Willielmus de Stutevylla dominus de Cotyngham quia 
prepotens erat molendinum quod pater suus nobis dederat nobis 
abstulit et cartam patris sui quam inde habuimus tum minis tum 
promissis fallacibus extorsit. Sed tamen dedit nobis in escambium 
prefati molendini sedem alterius molendini prope pristinum molen- 
dinum ad bladum nostrum proprtum molendum. Coegit etiam nos 
dare medietatem expensarum ad faciendum magnum fossatum quod 
ad heremitorium suum exit in Hullum. In cujus capite australi juxta 
aquam vivam quam tunc ibidem descendere fecerat de Cotyngham 
unum molendinum ad opus nostrum fecimus et ipse juxta illud aliud 
faciebat ad opus suum; aquam illam et piscariam ejus inter ipsum et 
nos partiendo. Et ipse nihilominus sibi faciebat molendina super 
aquam vivam quam nobis auferebat. Sed et illud vetus croftum duarum 
acrarum et iv. perticatarum quod pater suus dudum nobis cum 
prefato molendino pristino [concessit] et quod ipse Willielmus cum 
prefato molendino nobis pariter abstulit nobis iterum reconcessit in 
escambium pro duobus croftis que in ipso territorio prius habebamus, 
perticatam pro perticata. 


This is entered in the time of Thomas, the third abbot (1182-97). An 
earlier entry in the time of Philip, the second abbot (1160-82) records that 
William de Stuteville and Benedict de Sculcotes, making ditches for draining 
Cottingham marsh, seized the greater part of the common pasture of Myton 
and neighbouring vills.1 That proves that William de Stuteville was dealing 
with Cottingham affairs in his father’s lifetime ; but it is unlikely that his 
actions recorded here took place before his father’s death. A record of the 
gifts made by his father is given at no. 9 above. After William’s death there 
was a further dispute about the mill with his brother Nicholas, who gave 
easements and fishing rights with certain reservations and issued confirma- 
tions for a sum of 30 marks.?

one bovate in Fadmoor given by Robert de Maltby and Emma his wife, 
and the same amounts in the same places given by Jordan de Boltby and 
Sibyl his wife. The 2 bovates in Fadmoor, of William de Stuteville’s fee, 
were those confirmed by the present charter.. The charter of Robert de 
Maltby and Emma his wife, issued on the same occasion, is printed at no. 

, T18. 

An account of the family of Jordan de Boltby, probably a younger 
brother of Adam de Boltby, who held a knight’s fee of Robert de Stuteville 
III in 1166, is given in Chapter IV, §7. Jordan de Boltby and Robert 
de Maltby evidently married two sisters in whose right they held land in 
Nunnington and Fadmoor.

This is followed by a gift by the grantee to Bridlington priory, for the 
soul of his lord William de Stuteville, of 10 bovates in Cattal, 12 tofts and 
a third of one ‘ovenam’ and a moiety of the mill with the service from 3 
further bovates. 

The Domesday survey shows that land in Cattal was in the soke of 
Aldborough ;? and it was evidently that portion in which William de 
Stuteville acquired an interest in virtue of the grant made to him of Knares- 
borough and Aldborough.$ 

The first witness, Anselm de Stuteville of Weston Colville and Burton 
Agnes, was dead in 1199.4 The grantee witnessed charters nos. 25-6 in the 
period 1183-1201, and other witnesses suggest the earlier limit assigned.
