108 STUTEVILLE FEE 


nobis dedit in escambium pro prefata terra de Kirkeby.! Hiis testibus, 
domino H[ugone] Murdac, Willelmo de Ruelaie, Benedicto de 
Sculecotes, Henrico de Knaresburg’, Hugone capellano, Radulfo de 
Bosco, Radulfo de Carenci, Gervasio de Traili, et multis aliis. 


Kirkby Ouseburn, which was in the soke of Knaresborough, is now 
represented by Kirkby Hall in Little Ouseburn, and the church by that of 
Little Ouseburn. In the section on the Crown Demesne, dealing with Knares- 
borough and Aldborough, Farrer printed a charter of William de Stuteville, 
giving to Ralph son of Siward de Kirkby Ouseburn and to Emeline his wife, 
daughter of Avice de Chambord, 9 acres of land in Kirkby Ouseburn.? This 
was taken from the Fountains Chartulary, where there is a section of over 
forty charters relating to Kirkby Ouseburn, many of which illustrate William 
de Stuteville’s interest there. The present charter and the next refer to the 
advowson ; no. 33 shows the gift made by William to Fountains abbey of 
all his land there ; and no. 49 is a confirmation made by his brother Nicholas. 

The advowson was acquired by Fountains. In Jan. 1209-10 Robert prior 
of St. Andrew’s quitclaimed to Alan de Kirkby-his right in 12 bovates in 
Kirkby Ouseburn (evidently the 14 carucates of the present charter) and 
to John abbot of Fountains his right in 100 acres of land and the advowson ; 
the prior receiving 100s.4 A quitclaim of the advowson to Fountains, for a 
yearly payment of 20s., was also made by Nun Monkton priory,> to which 
house William de Arches and Juetta his wife had confirmed the gift of the 
church of Kirkby Ouseburn not later than the period 1147-53. But on 
1 March 1217-8 Fountains abbey gave the patronage of the church to 
archbishop Gray, who in 1221-22 annexed it to the precentorship of York.’ 

The details of the Domesday survey show that portions of Barthorpe, 
where William de Stuteville gave this land in exchange to St. Andrew’s 
priory, were held by the count of Mortain and Odo Arbalistarius, and it is 
not mentioned among the lands of Hugh son of Baldric. It may be that 
the land here mentioned had become attached to the Stuteville manor of 
Scrayingham, from which it is only 2} miles distant ; or it may actually 
have formed part of Hugh son of Baldric’s 12 carucates in that place at the 
Survey. 

The earliest date for the present charter is that of the foundation of 
St. Andrew’s priory by Hugh Murdac.® - Prior M., not apparently recorded, 
was probably the first prior ; in 1208 the prior was named Bartholomew, 
to whom a certain Juliana widow of Reiner quitclaimed her right in 2 
bovates in Barthorpe.?

An agreement made on 30 Aug. 1190 records the grant of these 14 
carucates by William de Kyrkeby to Hugh Murdac.! There seems to be no 
record of the dispute mentioned in the present charter, the date of which 
is probably of the same period as no. 31. Adam had succeeded William as 
rector of Cottingham ; the latter occurs as rector in the period 1190-1201 
(no. 26), and in other charters as William de Cottingham and William the 
clerk of Cottingham. For the first two witnesses see the note to no. 26.

34. Presentation by William de Stuteville of the monks of Bardney 
to the churches of [Great] Hale and Heckington [co. Lincoln], 
in confirmation of the charter of Gilbert de Gant before he 
became earl of Lincoln, when Gilbert’s barony and heir, namely 
Gilbert son of Robert de Gant, was in his custody by the authority 
of king Richard I. [1192-1202] 


Bardney Chartulary, Cotton MS. Vespasian E. xx, f. 72 (old f. 67). 


Universis Christi fidelibus ad quos presens carta pervenerit 
Willelmus de Stuteuile salutem. Noverit universitas vestra quod 
audito jure monachorum de Bard[eneia] et cognito super ecclesiis de 
Hale et Hekingt[ona] et rationibus eorum intellectis et plene cognitis 
tam per vivas voces et testimonium baronum de feodo quam per 
cartam Gilberti de Gant eis datam antequam comitatum Lincfolnie] 
fuerit adeptus ipsos ad predictas ecclesias absque omni retencione 
presentavi integre cum omnibus pertinenciis inperpetuum habendas 
sicut predictus Gilbertus de Gaunt eis plenius et melius concessit et 
dedit. Hanc autem presentacionem feci et sigillo meo confirmavi cum 
esset baronia Gilberti de Gaunt et heres ejusdem baronie scilicet 
Gilbertus de Gaunt filius Roberti de Gaunt in custodia mea auctoritate 
et precepto domini regis Ricardi. Test[ibus], magistro Willelmo sub- 
decano Linc’, magistro Stephano archidiacono de Bukingh[amia], etc. 


The elder Gilbert de Gant was created earl of Lincoln c. 1147-48, and 
died in 1156.1. Robert de Gant, his brother, died in 1191, leaving his son 
Gilbert the younger under age. At Michaelmas 1192 William de Stuteville 
rendered account in Lincolnshire of 100 marks for having the custody of 
Robert’s land with his heir. These two churches, described as the gift of 
earl Gilbert his uncle, were subsequently included in the comprehensive 
charter of confirmation to Bardney issued by Gilbert the younger.4 The 
limits of date for the present charter make it uncertain whether the first 
witness was William de Blois, subdean of Lincoln, 1192-97 or William de 
Bramfeid, subdean 1198-1205.5 


On 27 Jan. 1201-2 Gilbert de Gant the younger confirmed to William 
de Stuteville land in Barton-upon-Humber and Hunmanby, which William 
had purchased of Gilbert’s fee; William relinquishing his purchases in 
Gilbert’s fee in Swaledale. This explains the reference to Healaugh in 
Swaledale confirmed to William by king John in 1200 (no. 36).

weekly market in the vill of Cottingham. 
At Rouen. 24 Aug. 1199.

nostri sicut carta ejusdem regis Ricardi rationabiliter testatur. 
[witness and dating clauses). 


Farrer has given the early history of the manor of Bramham, a member 
of the Fossard fee, in an annotation to a charter of Robert Fossard to Nostell 
priory, c. 1126-1129; and suggested that its acquisition by William de 
Stuteville dates from the time, c. 1175, when William Fossard II was in 
difficulties.! It is probable that Ranulf de Glanville then acquired an interest 
which he gave to William de Stuteville, who married his niece.2 The Paynel 
interest had arisen through the marriage of Agnes Fossard to Alexander 
Paynel, father of William Paynel of Hooton Pagnell. 


William de Stuteville’s tenure of Bramham is illustrated by certain 
charters which are printed among those relating to the Fossard fee : 


(i) his confirmation of land granted in Bramham (E.Y.C., ii, no. 1030). 
(ii) his gift to Nostell priory of land there (zb7d., no. 1031). 
(iii) his agreement in 1192 with the priory of St. Clement, York, 
relating to Monkhaid in Bramham (zbid., no. 1039). 


His principal interest in Bramham appears to have passed eventually 
to Baldwin Wake, who held 2 knights’ fees in Bramham and Clifford of 
Peter de Mauley II (successor of the Fossards) at the latter’s death in 1279.4 
There was land in Oglethorpe which at the Domesday survey was soke of 
the manor of Bramham.5 


Some notes on Clifford, also a manor of the Fossard fee, will be given 
in Chapter IV, §16 in connexion with the holding there of half a knight’s 
fee (part of the 2 knights’ fees mentioned above), which by 1282 had become 
an appurtenance of the Stuteville manor of Buttercrambe, and was then 
held by the successor of Adam son of Robert de Birdsall. 


The confirmation of Milburn was followed on 4 March 1200-1 by the 
grant by king John to William de Stuteville and his heirs of all the king’s 
forest of Milburn within specified bounds.* The vill of Milburn passed to 
William’s brother Nicholas, who gave it to Robert de Vipont, by whom the 
grange of Milburn was given to Shap abbey.’ As a moiety of Long Marton 
was held in 1282 by Roger de Clifford, who married a coheiress of Robert 
de Vipont,® it is probable that Long Marton had been acquired from 
Nicholas de Stuteville similarly. 


Healaugh in Swaledale was a member of the Gant fee there, held of the 
honour of Richmond.? Robert Rosel, presumably an under-tenant, witnessed 
a charter of Robert de Gant relating to land in Swaledale, 1185-91.19 William 
de Stuteville relinquished his purchases there in Jan. 1201-2 (note to no. 34).

The Mowbray lands here mentioned are included in no. 43 (where they 
are given in modern form in the heading), except the following : Bonby, 
Saxby, Great Limber and Stallingborough, all co. Lincoln. The origin of 
any Mowbray interest, which was not a tenure in chief, in these places is 
obscure. In 1115-18 Robert, the bishop’s nvepos, held 3 car. in Great Limber, 
7 car. in Bonby and one car. in Stallingborough.! A comparison with the 
Domesday holdings shows that Hugh son of Baldric had held lands there, 
his tenure in Stallingborough also being one carucate.* This suggests that 
Robert de Stuteville [ may have succeeded Hugh in those places, as he 
had succeeded him in a large proportion of Hugh’s holdings in Yorkshire ; 
and that any claim made by William de Stuteville was based on that fact.

Willelmus de Stutevilla iterum suscitavit litem de baronia illa in 
curia Johannis regis Anglie. Cumque super hoc diu certatum esset, 
tandem consilio regni, et voluntate regis, pax et finalis concordia facta 
est inter predictos Willelmum de Stutevilla et Willelmum de Mubrai 
in hunc modum: quod Willelmus de Stutevilla renunciavit calumnize 
suze quam habuit adversus Willelmum de Mubrai super baronia sua, et 
Willelmus de Mubrai dedit Willelmo de Stutevilla pro homagio suo, 
et pro renunciatione juris sui, in augmentum, feoda novem militum, 
et duodecim libratus! reddituum. Et sic remissis omnibus querelis 
hinc et inde, facti sunt amici coram Johanne rege Angliz, secundo 
anno regni sui, apud Lue in Lindissia, villam Lincolniensis episcopi, 
prima Dominica Septuagesime. 

This account shows that the claims originally made by Robert de 
Stuteville III against Roger de Mowbray, which had started in the reign 
of Stephen (p. 5 above), had been settled temporarily after Henry IIL 
had succeeded ; and that the compromise had included Kirkby Moorside in 
the feoffment of 10 knights’ fees then made by Roger. That feoffment 
was therefore made between 1154 and 1166, when in Roger’s carta it was 
stated that 8 knights’ fees of the old feoffment and one of the new (totalling 
9, and not 10 as given in this account) were held of him by Robert de 
Stuteville.4 Kirkby Moorside descended to Joan de Stuteville, wife of 
Hugh Wake, and at her death the manor was held of the honour of Mowbray 
by the service of 10 knights. For the additional gift of 9 knights’ fees and 
12 librates of land see no. 44.

There is a reference to the case in the previous year when Wilham 
de Stuteville had put in his place William de Perci or Nigel de Plumton’.or 
Jordan de Rifford’; the places named correspond (Welleburn’ being so 
spelt), except that Laxinton’ is not included ; Rolland son of Uctred is 
so described. 

There is clear evidence that Robert de Stuteville 1 had possessed an 
interest in Coxwold and Hovingham (note to no. 1), and it can be assumed 
in Thirsk also. These places were held by Hugh son of Baldric at the Survey. 
The descendants. of Kobert de Stuteville 1 mever recovered Tis iverest 
therein, but on the other hand Robert de Stuteville ILI acquired an interest 
in Welburn (no. 10), when he was enfeoffed of Kirkby Moorside ; and it 
may have been included in the list of quitclaims to preserve the interest 
of Rievaulx abbey there.? 

There is no precise evidence that any interest in Axholme, Crick, 
Langford, Monks Kirby, Hampton-in-Arden or Melton Mowbray, held by 
Geoffrey de Wirce at the Survey, or in Kirkby Malzeard, then held by 
Gospatric, or in Burton-in-Lonsdale, where 4 carucates had then been terra 
vegis, had been held by Robert de Stuteville I. It is, however, possible that 
in view of these quitclaims the Stutevilles had some basis for claiming an 
inherited interest, and that the gifts of these places to Nigel d’Aubigny, 
who acquired a large proportion of the holdings of Geoffrey de Wirce, and 
from whom they descended to the Mowbrays,* were not made until after

the forfeiture of Robert de Stuteville in 1106. It is perhaps significant that 
among the benefactions to Lewes priory, confirmed by king Stephen, 
were the church of Melton [Mowbray] and the tithes of Axholme and 
Hampton-in-Arden of the gift of Geoffrey de Stuteville.t Again, some basis 
for an inherited interest by William de Stuteville in Burton-in-Lonsdale is 
suggested by his claim against Roland de Galloway and his wife. That lady 
was Elena or Hilaria, daughter of Richard and sister and heir of William 
de Moreville ; and her husband Roland lord of Galloway held the Moreville 
inheritance in her right. In 1166 Richard de Moreville had held 5 knights’ 
fees of Roger de Mowbray.? 

It is curious to find Langford mentioned in the list of quitclaims in 
view of the Stuteville interest there as shown by no. 17 above.

Vescy fees recorded were 2 knights’ fees in Sherburn (E.R.) and Langton ;° 
but these were appurtenant to the manor of Buttercrambe, and did not 
form part of the 44 fees in question.

Johannes Dei gratia etc. Sciatis nos concessisse et presenti carta 
confirmasse venerabili patri nostro H. Cant[uariensi] archiepiscopo pro 
quatuor millia marc[arum] custodiam Roberti filii et heredis Willelmi 
de Stutevill’ cum tota terra sua in Anglia et Normannia et cum castris 
tenementis et feodifirmis wardis firmis et vadiis suis quorum termini 
non sunt finiti. Concessimus etiam eidem archiepiscopo maritagium 
ipsius Roberti ita tamen quod ipsum non maritabit nec militem faciet 
nisi per voluntatem nostram et assensum nostrum. Concessimus 
-quoque ei de assensu predicti Roberti omnia predicta tenenda usque 
ad quatuor annos a festo sancti Johannis Baptiste anno regni nostri 
quinto et amplius quicquid de ipso Roberto contingat donec pecuniam 
suam vel rationabilem custum suum inde perceperit. Per hunc itaque 
finem concessimus eidem archiepiscopo omnes carrucas in predictis 
tenementis inventas et debet istam pecuniam solvere a festo sancti 
Johannis Baptiste anno regni nostri quinto infra duos annos scilicet in 
predicto festo sancti Johannis Baptiste mille marcas et infra eundem 
annum mille marcas in sequenti vero anno duo milia marc{arum]. Si 
vero de ipso archiepiscopo interim humaniter contigerit ille vel illi qui- 
bus hanc custodiam assignaverit omnia predicta tenebunt per predictum 
finem eodem modo et exinde omnia predicta ad prefatum Robertum per 
eundem finem integre revertentur vel antea si ipse Robertus prius inde 
gratum archiepiscopi fecerit. Quare volumus et firmiter precipimus 
quod ipse archiepiscopus vel illi quibus eandem custodiam assignaverit 
omnia predicta teneant bene et in pace [efc.] libera et quieta ab 
omnibus demandis debitis et exactionibus que ad nos pertinent preter 
predicta quatuor milia marc{arum] usque ad predictum terminum 
salvo nobis debito servicio et eo salvo quod predictus archiepiscopus 
non maritabit ipsum Robertum nec militem faciet nisi per voluntatem 
nostram et assensum nostrum sicut predictum est.

On 4 June 1203 the king had announced his grant to the archbishop 
of the custody of all the lands and castles which had belonged to William 
de Stuteville at his death and of the wardship of the heir.t. In April 1204 
Peter des Roches had the king’s letters of presentation to the church of 
[North] Cave which the archbishop who had the custody of the land of 
William de Stuteville had granted to the king. The archbishop still had 
the custody for three quarters of the year ending at Michaelmas 1205,° and 
died on 13 July of that year. William de Stuteville’s son Robert was dead 
by 5 Aug. (no. 48). 


48, Notification by king John that Nicholas de Stuteville [I] had 
made a fine of 10,000 marks for having his inheritance of all the 
lands of which his brother William de Stuteville was seised at 
his death, except the castle of Knaresborough and Borough- 
bridge to be retained in the king’s hand until the sum should be 
paid; which sum included 2100 marks owed by William at his 
death. Nicholas was to have the pledges held by William at his 
death, and afterwards by the archbishop of Canterbury at his 
death, for the remainder of their term. 

At Lambeth. <5. Aug. [1205].

50. Quitclaim by Nicholas de Stuteville [I] to Kirkstall abbey of 
all his right in Collingham and Bardsey. [c. 1205] 


Pd. in Kirkstall Coucher Book, no. 316. 


Omnibus hanc cartam visuris vel audituris Nicholaus de Estute- 
villa salutem. Sciatis quod ego pro amore Dei et salute anime mee 
concessi et hac mea carta quietum clamavi de me et de omnibus here- 
dibus meis in perpetuum Deo et sancte Marie et monachis de Kirk[estal] 
totum jus et totum clamum quod ego habui vel habere potui in 
Colingham et in Berdeseye et in earum pertinenciis, ita quod ego et 
heredes mei nunquam clamum vel calumpniam movebimus versus 
predictos monachos de predictis tenementis. Et hanc quietam claman- 
tiam per hanc cartam nostram eis confirmavimus. ‘Testes. 


The interest in these places acquired by William de Stuteville is shown 
in no. 40. Although it is clear that this interest was not confirmed to his 
brother Nicholas, the monks may have requested the issue of a quitclaim 
to guard against any contingent rights. Collingham and Bardsey were 
granted to Kirkstall by king John on 3 May 1205 (note to no. 40), and this 
quitclaim was probably issued by Nicholas soon after he succeeded his 
nephew Robert in that year.

manor of Liddel.? For the gift of the church to Rosedale priory see the note

The inspeximus and confirmation of 26 Feb. 1327-8 records notices of 
twenty-four other charters to the priory. They include the gift by Robert 
de Stuteville of the vale of Rossedale from the bounds of Peter de Bruys 
in length towards the south as far as Anchon [vecte Anchou] and in breadth 
from the highroad lying by ‘Anchon’ as far as Synene [vecte Syuene], and 
the meadow of Baggethwait and all the bark of all his wood as far as Cropton; 
a gift and confirmation by Eustace de Stuteville of all the place of Baggeth- 
wait except his forge, and all the meadow of Baggthwait from Smalesikes 
to ‘Synene’ and from Smalesikes to the nuns’ land towards the north, with 
common of pasture with him and his men from Bagethwait to Amoth ; 
another by the same cancelling the exception of his forge ; a confirmation 
by Nicholas de Stuteville [I] of the vale of Rossedale, etc. in accordance 
with the charter of Robert his son ; a confirmation by John son and heir 
of Sir Baldwin Wake ; and in full detail one by Thomas Wake, lord of Lidel, 
son and heir of John Wake ; also the gift of Sibyl de Valoniis in her widow- 
hood of the church of Torpenhow and the confirmation thereof by Eustace 
son of Robert de Stuteville. | 

Of the places mentioned Rosedale is in par. Middleton, wap. Pickering 
Lythe ; and the river Seven flows south down the vale. Anchou is represented 
by Hancow House in Hartoft, about a mile east of Rosedale Abbey ; and

land in Great Ayton and Hemlington ; see Chapter I, §5.

de Bathonia occurs as sheriff of Yorkshire from 1242! and Elias de Cumbe

This is the first charter in a considerable section of the Chartulary 
relating to Kelk.2 The whole manor and vill of Little Kelk became a pos- 
session of the priory,® and Brundone or Brundonne is named as land therein.’ 

The grantor was the son of Roger de Stuteville of Weston Colville, co. 

- Cambridge, and Burton Agnes, whom he succeeded between 1172 and 1194 ; 
he was dead in 1199.8 There were three successive members of the Talun 
family named Robert, benefactors of Bridlington priory, the second of 
whom gave mills and land in Kelk in the lifetime of Roger de Stuteville.® 

The Stuteville interest in Little Kelk was probably quite small, perhaps 
an outlying portion of the holding in Burton Agnes, from which it is 2 miles 
distant.

CHAPTER IV. 
CHARTERS RELATING TO THE KNIGHTS’ FEES 


The sections inthe present chapter, together with the 
accompanying charters, relate primarily to the knights’ fees 
pertaining to that portion of the possessions of his grandfather 
which was recovered as a tenure-in-chief by Robert de Stuteville 
III in the reign of Stephen. The first document (no. 67) 1s his carta 
of 1166. It shows that he held in his own hand two fees of the 
old feoffment chargeable to the demesne, which was represented 
mainly by those parts of the manors of Cottingham and Butter- 
crambe and their appurtenances which were not sub-infeudated, 
and probably also by the manor of Langton. These have been 
numbered as §1. The knights’ fees then held of him, which are 
numbered §2 to §9, consisted of six fees of the old feoffment and 
an eighth of a fee of the new. Subsequent sections, §10 to §18, 
deal with some of the further sub-infeudations which were made 
at a later date, and were recorded as pertaining to the manors 
of Buttercrambe and Cottingham in 1282.1. There then follows 
a section dealing with the honours of Liddel and Rosedale, held 
in chief by Nicholas de Stuteville I and his descendants.

of the old and new feoffment. [1166]

the Exchequer, p. 429, from Liber Rubeus de Scaccario, f. 119V.

§2. THE MURDAC FEE 


Robert Murdac held a knight’s fee of Robert de Stuteville 
III in 1166. The evidence suggests that this consisted of the 
Domesday holdings of Hugh son of Baldric in Skipwith (which 
may have included land in Menthorpe, par. Hemingbrough), 
together with a small portion of Little Weighton, par. Rowley, 
and in Kettleby Thorpe and Bigby, wap. Yarborough, co. Lincoln. 


t Por*the full list of these seé Chapter Ul. 
2 Comin, Lib. R. 3 Croere, 1b. * Bolteby, ib. 


136 STUTEVILLE FEE 


The predecessor of Robert Murdac appears to have been 
Richard Murdac, who joined with Robert de Stuteville III in a 
confirmation to Durham priory of the church of Skipwith (no. 7). 
A charter of archbishop Henry in favour of Watton priory, 1150- 
53, was witnessed by Robert de Stuteville with Hugh Murdac and 
Richard his brother.! Richard Murdac and Richard Cruer, another 
of Robert de Stuteville’s tenants, were among the witnesses to a 
charter of archbishop Henry in favour of Nun Monkton priory, 
1147-532 Richard Murdac witnessed a confirmation of the same 
archbishop to Fountains abbey,® and was the first witness to a 
charter of Ralph de Neville to Bridlington priory. 


The names of Richard and Robert Murdac suggest that of 
Geoffrey Murdac who lived earlier in the twelfth century. There 
is definite evidence that the latter held an interest in Kettleby 
Thorpe and Bigby (see Appendix A above); and it is not unlikely 
that, being in possession of some of the forfeited lands of Robert 
de Stuteville I, he made a feoffment of this knight’s fee to a 
member of a younger branch of his family,® and that this passed 
to the overlordship of Robert de Stuteville III on his restoration.® 


Nothing seems to be known of the heirs of Robert Murdac. 
In 1212 Nicholas de Stuteville was the tenant-in-chief of 1 caru- 
cate 6 bovates in Kettleby Thorpe and Bigby, which approximately 
corresponds to the Domesday holding of Hugh son of Baldric of 
154 bovates in those places, and precisely to the holding of Geoffrey 
Murdac in 1115-18;7 and the under-tenants of Nicholas were 
William de Scoteny and Geoffrey de Thorpe by the service of a 
third of a knight’s fee.® In 1238-41 Walter de Killingholme, 
William de Thorpe and Helena de Scoteny held a third of a knight’s 
fee in Kettleby Thorpe ;? and in 1242-43 Walter de Killingholme 
and William de Thorpe each held an eighth of a knight’s fee 
there of Joan Wake, who held in chief. In 1282, after Baldwin 
Wake’s death, it was found that a knight’s fee in Skipwith, [Little) 
Weighton, and Thorp in Lindsey |2.e. Kettleby Thorpe], pertaining 
to the manor of Cottingham, was held by John de Skipwith

(Shtpwik) and his parceners.1 This gives the composition of the 
fee which can evidently be identified as that held by Robert 
Murdac in 1166. 


With regard to Skipwith a manor of 3 carucates was held 
there by Hugh son of Baldric in 1086, 5 carucates being entered 
as his holding in the Summary.? The bishop of Durham acquired 
land there and in Menthorpe, par. Hemingbrough, a place which 
is not mentioned in the Survey. In Feb. 1199-1200 the bishop 
enfeoffed Richard d’Avranches of 2 carucates in Skipwith and 
5 carucates elsewhere. In 1280 John d’Avranches and John de 
Thorpe occur as tenants in Skipwith in an inquisition relating to 
their bounds. In 1284-85, of the 3 carucates in Skipwith, 2 
carucates were held by John d’Avranches of the bishop of Durham’s 
fee and the remaining carucate by John de Thorpe of the heirs of 
Baldwin Wake ; and in Menthorpe, of the 4 carucates, 2 carucates 
were held of the bishop of Durham’s fee and the others by John 
de Thorpe of Skipwith of the same heirs.® The total holding of 
the Wake, formerly Stuteville, fee in the two places was therefore 
3 carucates ; and in 1346 that was the amount held of the Wake 
fee in Skipwith and Menthorpe by John de Skipwith.§ 


With regard to Little Weighton it has been noted above 
that in 1282 land in that place was associated with land in Skip- 
with and Kettleby Thorpe in the composition of the knight’s fee. 
This, however, must have been only a small portion of Little 
Weighton, as the greater part of it was included in another holding 
(see §3). 

From these details it can be deduced that the knight’s fee 
held by Robert Murdac in 1166 passed to a family which was 
known by the names of Thorpe or Skipwith. William son of 
Osbert de Skipwith held interests in Skipwith and Menthorpe 
early in the thirteenth century (no. 68). In 1258 there is a refer- 
ence to a gift of land in Menthorpe which had been made by Richard 
de Thorpe brother of William [de] Skipwith.?, The name Skipwith 
prevailed. Sir William Skipwith, the distinguished judge of the 
middle of the fourteenth century, was so known.’ In 1375 William 
son of Sir William de Skipwith held interests in Menthorpe ;? and 
an inquisition of 1417-8 found that Sir Thomas Skipwith had 
held the manor of Skipwith with appurtenances in Menthorpe.!° 
Accounts of the family name as its ancestor Patrick de Stuteville, 
a younger brother of Robert de Stuteville III, who was given the 
manor of Skipwith ; but no documentary evidence for the descent

in the confirmation charters of Henry II, 1156-57,! and Richard I ;? and in

assigned, conform with the period of his activities noted in Appendix A 
above. 


$3. THE CAMIN PEF 


The knight’s fee held of Robert de Stuteville III by Maud 
daughter of Hugh Camin in 1166 lay in Little Weighton, par. 
Rowley, and North Cave. 

At the Domesday survey the sole tenant-in-chief in Little 
Weighton was Hugh son of Baldric, who held a manor of 5 caru- 
cates there with a berewick of 2 bovates in North Cave.* There 
were four feudal tenements in North Cave at the Survey, which 
Farrer has described in an annotation to a charter of the Fossard 
fee, with a note on their devolution ; pointing out that in addition 
to the berewick, to which the church of North Cave was appur- 
tenant, 3 carucates in North Cave passed from the Malet fee to 
the Stuteville tenancy-in-chief.®> Later details show that in 1302-03 
6 carucates in Little Weighton and 24 carucates in North Cave 
were held of the Stuteville fee. It seems clear that a small portion 
in Little Weighton became attached to the knight’s fee held by 
Robert Murdac in 1166 (see §2); and it is recorded that in Little 
Weighton 8 carucates made a knight’s fee.’ 

It can be deduced from the terms of the claim made in 1208 
by Thurstan de Montfort for a moiety of a knight’s fee in Little 
Weighton and North Cave (Appendix A above),’ based on his 
descent from his grandmother Juliana, a daughter of Geoffrey 
Murdac, that Geoffrey Murdac held an interest in those places ; 
and it can be suggested that he acquired it, as he acquired the 
interest in Kettleby Thorpe and Bigby (see §2), with other holdings 
held in chief by Robert de Stuteville I, after the latter’s forfeiture 
in 1106. And as this knight’s fee, held by Maud Camin in 1166, 
was of the old feoffment, and so created before 1135, its creation in 
favour of Hugh Camin her father or his predecessor may have been 
due to Geoffrey Murdac, unless it had been created before 1106.

Hugh Camin witnessed a charter of count Alan of Britanny 
to Durham priory, 1136-45, which included a gift of half Hunsley!— 
a place which had a close connexion with Little Weighton,? and 
where Robert de Stuteville I had made a gift to the priory (note 
to charter no. 1). He also witnessed a charter of Roger de Mowbray 
(no. 165). His wife’s name was Anor who occurs as holding a life 
interest (presumably in dower) in land in Myton. This Camin 
interest in Myton, held of the Mortemer tenancy-in-chief,* passed 
to Robert son of John de Meaux in marriage with Maud Camin, 
daughter of Hugh and Anor.6 It can be supposed that her in- 
herited interest in the knight’s fee held of Robert de Stuteville 
III passed to her husband in the same way ; and, as in 1166 she 
and not her husband was named as the tenant, it can be assumed 
that either the marriage had not taken place or else that her 
husband was dead ; and of these the former is more likely. Maud 
daughter of Hugh Camin also held a quarter of a knight’s fee in 
1166 of Everard de Ros,® which can be located in Leppington.’? 


Described as Maud Camin she was living on 19 May 1196, 
when her son John de Meaux (Mehus) made an agreement with her 
relating to her dower from the land of Robert his father.8 The 
tenure, in whole or part, held by her of Robert de Stuteville, 
passed to her descendants.? In 1282, after Baldwin Wake’s death, 
it was found that John de Meaux (Meus) held three-quarters of a 
knight’s fee in Little Weighton and North Cave pertaining to the 
manor of Cottingham ;° and Godfrey de Meaux, who died shortly 
before 3 Feb. 1310-1, held of Thomas Wake by knight-service a 
capital messuage and 34 bovates in Little Weighton, and a toft 
and the advowson of the church in North Cave." There appears 
to be no clue to the reduction of the holding from a knight’s fee 
to three-quarters, except that in 1284-85 the entry for Little 
Weighton, a member of Cottingham, clearly defective as there is 
no mention of the Meaux holding, gives a tenure by John de

§4. THE COWESBY FEE 


The two-thirds of a knight’s fee held by Osmund de Stute- 
ville of Robert de Stuteville III in 1166 lay in Cowesby, wap. 
Birdforth, Kepwick, par. Over Silton, East Ness, par. Hovingham, 
and Newsham, par. Kirby Wiske, all in the North Riding ; and 
later evidence shows that his holding was subsequently increased 
to 2 knights’ fees by grants of land in Brantingham, Riplingham, 
par. Rowley, Stillingfleet, and possibly Newbald, all in the wapen- 
take of Harthill in the East Riding? 

Details relating to the descent of the fee have been given in 
the account of the Stutevilles of Cowesby and Gressenhall in 
Chapter I, where it is suggested that Osmund de Stuteville was 
a younger son of Robert de Stuteville III. The fee passed to 
William son of Osmund de Stuteville, and subsequently to William’s 
grandson Jordan Foliot, whose holding of 2 knights’ fees of Baldwin 
Wake in 1282 lay in Cowesby, East Ness, Stillingfleet, Riplingham 
and Brantingham. The intervening tenure by Robert son of 
William de Stuteville, who died s.p., is shown by charter no. 75. 

The returns of 1284-85 show that of the 4 carucates in 
Cowesby 24 carucates were held by or of Jordan Foliot, who held 
of the heirs of Baldwin Wake ;3 in East Ness 3 carucates were 
held for a quarter of a knight’s fee of Jordan Foliot, who held of 
the same heirs ;# of the 4 carucates in Newsham one carucate was 
held by the abbot of Fountains and 3 carucates of Jordan Foliot 
who held of the same heirs ;° in Stillingfleet, where 3 out of the 
5 carucates formed part of the Wake z.e. Stuteville fee, one caru- 
cate was held of Jordan Foliot who held of [the heirs of] Baldwin 
Wake ;§ and in Brantingham and Riplingham 1? and 2} carucates 
respectively were held of the Stuteville tenancy-in-chief, though 
no under-tenant in those places is mentioned.’ These returns have 
a confusing character in that with regard to Cowesby, East Ness 
and Newsham the tenure of the heirs of Baldwin Wake is stated 
to be a sub-tenure of Roger de Mowbray ; and although that was 
true of Kirkby Moorside and its members, which had been held 
by Robert de Stuteville III of Roger de Mowbray I in 1166, it is 
difficult to suppose that it was true of lands which were held in 
chief by Robert de Stuteville III and held of him by knight- 
service when he returned his carta in that year. The same point 
arises in connexion with Boltby and the lands which constituted 
the Boltby fee (see §7). It is significant that no holding by knight- 
service in Cowesby or East Ness, nor also in Boltby, occurs in the

extensive list of knights’ fees held in chief by Roger de Mowbray 
III and recorded in the inquisitions after his death in 1297," 


(1) COWESBY 


71. Notification by Osmund de Stuteville that the controversy 
between Hugh bishop of Durham and himself relating to the 
chapel of Cowesby had been the subject of a settlement by which 
he quitclaimed all right therein, recognizing that it belonged to 
the mother church of Leake and that the chaplain should be 
appointed by the bishop and his successors, saving to Osmund, 
his heirs and men, the maintenance of service in the chapel. 

[1189-92]

The next charter in the MS. records a further gift of the same grantor 
of all his part of Traneker’ and all his land between Traneker’ and the water 
of Wiske belonging to his half carucate of land in Neusom, and notes his gift 
of an acre of the marsh below Magnhill ‘ut in carta precedenti.’ 

The MS. from which these charters are taken is a register drawn up 
in 1509 ; the fourth volume of the fifteenth century chartulary in five vol- 
umes, which would have provided witnesses, is missing.?

half a carucate of land in Newsham, where ten carucates made 
a knight’s fee. [late 12th cent.] 


Fountains Register, B.M. Add. MS. 18276, f. 155. Abst. in

Omnibus etc. Nisandus filius Ode* de Neusom salutem. Sciatis

Ensuing charters in the same MS. record confirmations by Robert son 
of Nisand de Neusom of his father’s gifts. 

The grantor’s father can be identified as the Odo de Neusum who 
witnessed Roger de Mowbray’s gift to Fountains abbey of Aldburgh near 
Masham in the middle of the twelfth century.> Robert, Eustace and Enisant 
de Neusum witnessed a charter to Fountains giving land in Kirby Wiske 
c. 1174-1181. The latter was the grantor of the present charter ; and he

with members of his family witnessed several other charters to Fountains 
relating to Kirby Wiske.! Enisant or Nisand de Newsham was dead by 
1202 when Robert de Newesum (his son) held a third of a knight’s fee and 
owed service to William de Stuteville.2 Robert was succeeded by his 
son Ranulf before 1234, when the latter made an agreement with the abbot 
of Fountains relating to pasture in Newsham.? Ranulf had a son Robert, 
whose son, a younger Ranulf, succeeded.* This younger Ranulf is men- 
tioned in no. 75 below, of date 1270. 


74. Confirmation by William son of Osmund de Stuteville to 
Fountains abbey of half a carucate of land in Newsham which 
Nisand [de Newsham] had given, and of a part of the turbary 
in Traneker which Eustace [de Newsham] had given. 

[early 13th cent.] 
Fountains Register, B.M. Add. MS. 18276, f. 155. Abst. in 
Fountains Chartulary, p. 773. 

Omnibus etc. Willelmus filius Osmundi de Stuteuilla salutem. 
Sciatis me concessisse et confirmasse Deo et monachis [de Fontibus] 
dimidiam carucatam terre in Neusom quam habent ex dono Nisandi 
in perpetuam elemosinam quietam ab omnibus serviciis salvo mihi et 
heredibus meis forinseco servicio. Concessi etiam eisdem monachis et 
confirmavi quandam partem turbarie in Traneker’ quam Eustachius 
eis dedit. 

This is followed by the gift of Eustace de Neusom and Andrew de 
Kyrkbywisk and Juliana his wife, Eustace’s daughter, of all their part in 
the marsh of Traneker’, with facilities in detail on the same lines as those 
granted in no. 72 above ; and then by an agreement for warranty made by 

_- Eustace in 1201. It is probable that the present charter was issued soon after 

the grantor came of age, which is suggested as being in the period 1200-1202.

de Stuteville, to Fountains abbey of all right in 7 bovates of 
land in Newsham which Ranulf de Newsham had held of him 
and of which Ranulf had enfeoffed the monks, and in other 
specified interests there. 1270.

Fountains Chartulary, p. 780.

John de Snape, mentioned in this charter, was a party to a fine with

bovates and 3 acres of land in Newsham at a yearly rent of 10s.1_ In 1271

865. ° THEVORVUER FEE 


The third of a knight’s fee held by Richard Cruere of Robert 
de Stuteville III in 1166 consisted of land in Cawton, par. Gilling 
in Ryedale, and possibly of a tenure in Cold Kirby. 

At the Domesday survey the count of Mortain held 3 caru- 
cates in Cawton, and Hugh son of Baldric held two manors of 4 
carucates, Gerard being named as his tenant.? The former holding 
passed to the Paynel fee; and in 1284-85 3 carucates in Cawton 
were held of John Paynel by Roger de Blancmusters for a quarter 
of a knight’s fee, Hugh son of Baldric’s holding being then repre- 
sented by 3 carucates which were held of the heirs of [Baldwin] 
Wake. by John Cruer for a quarter of a knight's fee* in 1167 
the tenants of Cawton had been Richard Criuere and Gerneg{an];° | 
and it is probable that the latter was the predecessor of the 
Blancmusters family in the Paynel portion of the vill. 

Richard” Crier, “the “tenant “of the “Stoteville pertion. of 
Cawton, witnessed a charter of archbishop Henry Murdac, 1147- 
53,6 a charter to the Templars, c. 1155-1177,? and a charter giving 
land in Ingleby Greenhow to Robert de Stuteville III dated 1166.8 
He held land in Cold Kirby, wap. Birdforth, which at a date 
later than 1154 became a member of the Stuteville fee held in 
chief,® where he gave 6 carucates to the Templars, and made an 
agreement with Roger abbot of Byland (no. 76). He also gave 
the Templars an acre of land in Cawton, which in 1185 was held

by his son Aumundus at a rent of 12d.) It seems probable that 
the Richard Cruer who witnessed a charter relating to Gilling in 
Ryedale in the time of Robert abbot of St. Mary’s,? and therefore 
not earlier than 1184, was of a younger generation, and that he 
was the grantor of no. 78 below. 

It is likely that Aumundus son of Richard Cruer can be 
identified with the Osmund Croer who witnessed a charter of 
Geoffrey de Lascelles to Rievaulx abbey, 1170-76 ;3 and with the 
grantee of no. 77 below. As Osmund Cruer, knight, he witnessed 
a charter relating to Gilling in Ryedale c. 1181-1189.4 Alan Cruer, 
who gave land in Snainton in Pickering Lythe to Yeddingham 
priory, c. 1190-1200,5 was presumably a member of the same 
family. In 1218-19 among the sureties of Thomas Cruer, appealed 
of rape, were Richard and Robért Cruer.* In Dec. 1226 Richard 
Cruer quitclaimed a toft in Cawton to’ Luke de Cawton ; and in the 
following month he was one of three claimants to 2 bovates in 
Muscoates, par. Kirkdale.? In 1232 Emma Cruer claimed against 
William Cruer 3 carucates in Cawton as her right; and in 1234, 
described as daughter of Robert Cruer, she granted this land 
with a small exception to William and his heirs to hold of her 
and her heirs. In 1252 the abbot of Rievaulx brought a plea 
against William Cruer (Cer) to hold to an agreement touching 
8 acres of meadow in Cawton ;® and in 1262 William Cruer was a 
juror in an inquisition for the tenure of land in the parish of Mid- 
dleton in Pickering Lythe2® In 1268 William Cruer and Ellen 
his wife granted land in Cawthorn, par. Middleton, to hold of 
them and Ellen’s heirs.“ 

There seems to be no proof of the parentage of John Cruer 
who, as noted above, held the 3 carucates in Cawton of the heirs 
of Baldwin Wake in 1284-85; and who is named as one of the 
two joint lords of the vill in 1316. <A connexion with Cawton 
was maintained by members of the Cruer family for a long period ; 
and six generations are given in the maternal ancestry of George 
Rosse of Cawton at the Visitation of 1584.13 

It is confusing to find that in the inquisition held in 1282 
after the death of Baldwin Wake it is stated that the third of a 
knight’s fee in Cawton was held, not by John Cruer, but by

148 STUTEVILLE FEE 


William de Lascelles! Again at the death of Thomas Wake of 
Liddel in 1349 tenements in Cawton were held by William de 
Lascelles for a third of a knight’s fee ;? and in 1416 after the death 
of Alice countess of Kent a third of a knight’s fee in Cawton is 
described as that which William de Lascelles lately held, another 
holding there for an eighth of a knight’s fee being that which 
William ‘Crowell’ lately held. As is shown by the charter below 
(no. 79) a William de Lascelles held an interest in Cawton. In 
1212 Thomas de Lascelles was accused of making disseisins in 
Cawton ;# and in 1218-19 his connexion with Cawton was men- 
tioned in a case.® 

A difficulty of another kind hes in the discrepancy of re- 
corded statements as to the tenancy-in-chief of which the Stute- 
ville portion of Cawton was held. As in 1166 it was land held by 
knight-service of Robert de Stuteville III, and represented one 
of the feoffments specified in his carta, it would naturally be 
supposed that he was the tenant-in-chief ; and, indeed, at the 
death of Baldwin Wake it was duly entered among the knights’ 
fees appurtenant to the manor of Buttercrambe which he held 
in chief. On the other hand in the returns of 1284-85 the 3 caru- 
cates in Cawton held by John Cruer of the heirs of [Baldwin] 
Wake are given as held by the latter of Roger de Mowbray ;6 
after the death of Roger de Mowbray in 1297 it was recorded in 
1301 that 6 carucates in Cawton were held of him by Joan Wake 
for half a knight’s fee ;? and at the death of Thomas Wake of 
Liddel in 1349 the tenancy in Cawton was apparently given as a 
member of the manor of Kirkby Moorside, which he held of 
John de Mowbray. But Cawton hes considerably nearer to 
Kirkby Moorside than to Buttercrambe, and it may have been 
administered from the former for reasons of convenience. It is 
not unlikely that in those circumstances the jurors may have 
supposed that the tenancy-in-chief was the same as that of Kirkby 
Moorside, and that the Stuteville portion of Cawton was therefore 
a member of the honour of Mowbray ; and the balance of evidence 
suggests that this was incorrect.

The second witness gives rise to a difficulty, for Robert Butevilain 
did not become dean of York until sometime between 13 Dec. 1157 and 
6 May 1158, having witnessed several charters of archbishop Henry as 
archdeacon.? It can be suggested, therefore, that decano is an error in the 
MS. for archidiacono. As Hugh du Puiset, the treasurer, witnessed in the 

' presence of archbishop Henry the earliest date is 1150.4 

The charter shows that Richard Cruer held an interest in Cold Kirby 
before Henry II gave it to Robert de Stuteville III. For Robert d’Aunay, 
the hermit of Hood who joined abbot Gerold’s congregation, see no. 115.

§6. THE FOSSARD FEE 


The knight’s fee held by Geoffrey Fossard of Robert de 
Stuteville III in 1166 consisted of 10 carucates in Sutton under 
Whitestone Cliffe and Marderby, both par. Felixkirk, and Lays- 
thorpe, par. Stonegrave. 


At the Domesday survey Hugh son of Baldric held manors 
of 5 carucates in Sutton funder Whitestone Cliffe], 3 carucates in 
Marderby, and 2 carucates in Laysthorpe, Girard his man being 
mentioned in the first two places.2. The returns of 1284-85 show 
that among the lands which constituted the liberty of Byland, 4 
carucates in Sutton, 4 carucates in Marderby, and 2 carucates in 
Laysthorpe were held of the abbot of Byland, who held of the 
heirs of. Baldwin Wake.? The knight’s fee had been given in pure 
and perpetual alms to Byland abbey by Thomas Fossard (no. 88); 
and there is no mention of it in the extensive list of knights’ fees 
held of Baldwin Wake at his death.4 


Farrer has given an account of the main line of the Fossard 
family descending from Nigel Fossard, the tenant of the count of 
Mortain at the Domesday survey.® The fact that Nigel Fossard 
held 6 carucates in South Otterington of the bishop of Durham® 
shows that Geoffrey Fossard, who subsequently held land there of 
the Brus fee,? was a member of the same family ; more especially 
as Geoffrey Fossard held a knight’s fee of William Fossard in 
1166 ;8 and Farrer suggested that Geoffrey was.a younger son of 
Robert son of Nigel Fossard, and so a younger brother of William 
Fossard.9 


The charters of Robert de Stuteville III giving the knight’s 
fee to Geoffrey Fossard and confirming it to Adam Fossard have 
beén- printed in Chapter III, nos. 15, 16. . At Michaelmas 1163 
Geoffrey Fossard accounted for 40s. in Yorkshire. He enfeoffed 
Geoffrey his younger son of his tenure in South Otterington ; and 
this was confirmed to the younger Geoffrey by Adam de Brus II 
not later than 1195." His wife’s name was Maud (no. 80), and his 
heir was Adam, presumably his elder son, who succeeded before 
1183 (no. 16), and was amerced in Yorkshire in 1185, 1188 and 
1193.42 He claimed an interest in Hood, which lies about a mile

to the east of Sutton under Whitestone Cliffe ; his dispute with 
Newburgh priory as to that place was settled in the presence of 
Hugh bishop of Durham, not later than 1183 (no. 121); and his 
gift of Hood to the priory was confirmed by William de Stuteville, 
1183-99 (no. 24). Agreements to which Adam Fossard was a 
party, being described as lord of all the ‘vavasaria’ of Sutton, and 
lord of Marderby, are printed below (nos. 81-2). He also held 
land in Bagby and Balk of the honour of Mowbray.! He married 
Hawise,a niece of Robert de Stuteville III (no. 16); and was 
succeeded by 1209 by his son Robert, who was living in 1217, 
when he returned to allegiance,? and as a knight in Yorkshire in 
1220.3 Robert Fossard gave the manor of Marderby to Byland 
abbey, his gift being confirmed by Nicholas de Stuteville II (no. 
60); and he was a benefactor of the Hospitallers (no. 83), and also 
of Newburgh priory.4— Joan, Robert’s widow, was a party to fines 
relating to her dower in Sutton in Jan. 1226-7 and 1227 ;> and 
she married William Arundel as her second husband, with whom 
in 1240 she was a party to a fine with the abbot of Byland relating 
to land in Sutton.® 

Robert Fossard was succeeded by his brother Thomas.? In 
1235 the latter gave to William, archdeacon of Durham, 2 bovates 

and a mill in Sutton, saving the rights in the mill of Joan, Robert 
Fossard’s widow. In Jan. 1236-7 he confirmed to the abbot of 
Byland 18 bovates, 189 acres of land, 14 tofts and pasture for 
260 sheep, 60 pigs, 20 cows, a bull, 20 oxen and 4 horses in Sutton 
and Marderby, with the fishery of Gormir, which had been given 
by Robert Fossard his brother, adding pasture for 60 sheep in 
Sutton ;°° and not later than 1238, described as son of Adam 
Fossard, he gave to Byland abbey the knight’s fee which is the 
subject of the present note (no. 88). 

The younger branch of the family, namely the Fossards of 
South Otterington, a member of the Brus fee, is outside the scope 
of the present note. But it can be recorded, as is shown by certain 
pleadings in 1231, that they held their land in South Otterington, 
as also some land in Sutton, as under-tenants of the elder branch, 
namely the Fossards of Sutton ;© and this conforms with the 
terms of the charter of Geoffrey Fossard to Geoffrey his younger 
son cited above. Their tenure of land in Sutton is shown in a fine 
of 14 Jan. 1240-1 between the abbot of Bvland and Robert Fossard 
(of South Otterington).™

80. Gift by Geoffrey Fossard and Maud his wife to Hugh the clerk 
of the hospital and his heirs of specified land [in Sutton] rendering 
3s. yearly. [ante 1183] 
MS. Dodsworth vii, f. 215, from the original formerly in St. Mary’s 
Tower, York. 

Notum sit omnibus sancte matris ecclesie filiis tam presentibus 
quam futuris quod ego Gaufridus Fossard et Matildis sponsa mea et 
heredes nostri concessimus et donavimus et hac nostra carta confirm- 
avimus Hugoni clerico hospitalis et heredibus suis terram illam quam 
Lambertus de nobis tenuit, que videlicet est inter [fontem]! de Kalde- 
kelde et divisas factas inter nos et Adam de Boltebi. Hanc terram 
concessimus eis totam et integram usque ad croftum Helsi cum tofto 
eidem terre et cum Caluecrofto ; tenendam de nobis et heredibus nostris 
in feudo et hereditate liberam et quietam ab omni seculari servitio; 
reddendo annuatim tres solidos, scilicet xviij denarios ad festum sancti 
Martini et xviij denarios ad Pentecosten. De hac donacione testes sunt : 
milites de wapentac et nominatim isti, Fulco Painel, Richerius de 
Wassand, Tomas Mansel, Radulfus de Hiserbec, Gaufridus filius 
Ernaldi de Kiluintun’, Stefanus de Kiluintune, Hugo filius Arturi 
de Torentun, Iuo Peuenesel, Richerius de Cattune, Robertus Wigot, 
et alii plures. 


The reference to the bounds between the grantors’ land and that of 
Adam de Boltby suggests that the land granted in this charter lay in Sutton, 
which lies a mile south of Thirlby, a member of Adam’s fee (see §7). The 
first witness was Fulk Paynel I of Drax and Hambye, which he held for 
many years till his death in 1182-83.2, Ralph de Ysebec and Hugh son of 
Arthur de ‘Torton’ occur in 1185.3 The grantor was succeeded by Adam 
Fossard not later than 1183.4 


81. Record of an agreement between Arnold lord of Upsall and 
Adam Fossard lord of Sutton [under Whitestone Cliffe], with 
a description of the bounds of Middlemore which included the 
wood called Killingwith towards Marderby, where Arnold 
had no common and where pasture and common were used 
by the men of Sutton and Marderby. Disputes which had arisen 
between Arnold and Adam relating to common in the wood, 
and the felling of trees in Middlemore, were settled whereby 
Arnold should marry Juliana, Adam’s sister, and have for his 
men of Upsall common in the wood, without, however, the right 
to fell trees. . [c. 1183-1203] 


Byland Chartulary, Egerton MS. 2823, f. 67V (old f. 174¥).

Memorandum quod tempore regis Henrici secundi filii Matildis 
imperatricis erant superstites Arnaldus dominus de Upsall’ et Adam 
Fossard dominus totius vavasarie de Sutton’ juxta Sanctum Felicem, 
ambo milites quorum solum et pastura simul conterminant. Et fuerunt 
divise inter solum predictorum Arnaldi et Ade a mora que vocatur 
Mideimore ut patet per siketum in presenti et per quandam antiquam 
fossam per medium Burtonholme usque ad Fossarddyk et inde ascend- 
endo per ductum qui vocatur Holbek usque ad campum de Kyrkeby 
Knoll’, infra quas divisas quidem boscus includitur qui dicitur Kill- 
yngwith versus villam de Martherby, in quo bosco nec dictus Arnaldus 
nec homines sui ullam communam aliquo tempore habebant nisi per 
eschapium et ipso Adam Fossard hoc ignorante, set ipsa pastura et 
communa homines de Sutton’ et Martherby pacifice utebantur in 
dicto bosco et ultra predictas divisas usque ad ductum qui vocatur 
Theuesdalebek; et sepe inter Arnaldum et Adam discordia magna 
emanavit tam pro predicta communa de Killyngwith quam pro amput- 
acione arborum crescencium in Middelmore que tunc erant speciosa et 
pulcherrima. Tandem mediantibus amicis et consanguineis anborum 
finali pace concordati sunt : ita quod dictus Arnaldus acciperet sororem 
ipsius Ade Julianam nomine in uxorem, et quod de Middelmore fieret 
composicio pacis pro se et suis imperpetuum, et quod ipse Adam super 
hoc in augmentum dicti matrimonii daret et concederet eidem Arnaldo 
et heredibus suis et eorum hominibus de Upsale tantum communam in 
predicto bosco de Killyngwith, sic tamen quod non liceret eidem 
Arnaldo heredibus vel eorum hominibus inibi arbores succidere vel 
asportare sine licencia et voluntate predicti Ade et heredum suorum; 
que omnia amicabiliter et in pace sunt completa. Isti Adam et Arn- 
aldus usque ad finem alterius in magna pace et amore pariter vixerunt. 
Et dictus Adam Fossard fecit partem manerii de Sutton’ et magne 
aule de Matherby ut patet per antiquum registrum de meremio pred- 
icte more de Middelmore ac de Killingwith, et ut patet per predictum 
registrum vocabatur Middelmore quia solum et pasturam de Martherby 
et de Upsall’ simul conterminat. 


Upsall hes a mile west of Kirby Knowle, which is 2 miles north of 
Marderby. The agreements recorded in this memorandum and the next 
were probably not made until William de Stuteville, the first witness to 
the next, had succeeded his father in 1183 ; and the first sentence suggests 
a date not later than 1189 ; in any case 1203, when William de Stuteville 
died, is the latest limit. At a substantially later date the agreements were 
included in the confirmation charter of Nicholas de Stuteville II (no. 60). 
For Arnold de Upsall see §12. 


82. Agreement between Adam Fossard, lord of Marderby, and 
Arnold de Upsall, lord of Upsall, regarding the felling and re- 
moval of trees in Middlemore which lay undivided between their 
land and pasture. It was agreed that timber should be used 
by both for building, but none should be sold or given away 
except by mutual consent. [c. 1183-1203] 


MS. Dodsworth cxviiu, f. 81¥, apparently from the original. Chartu- 
lary of Byland, Egerton MS. 2823, f. 68 (old f. 175).

83. Gift by Robert son of Adam Fossard to the hospital of St. John 
of Jerusalem of the whole of ‘Batinum’ and other specified land 

in Felixkirk. [c. 1210] 

Pd. in Curia Regis Rolls, vi, 109, from Curia Regis Roll 68, m. 12 ; 
and in Mon. Ang., vi, 840, from the same. 

Omnibus sancte matris ecclesie filiis has literas visuris vel audi- 
turis Robertus Fossard’ filtus Ade Fossard salutem in Domino. Noverit 
universitas vestra me divine caritatis intuitu pro salute anime mee et 
predecessorum et successorum meorum dedisse et concessisse et pre- 
senti carta confirmasse Deo et sancte Marie et sancto Johanni Baptiste 
et beatis pauperibus sancte domus hospitalis Jerosolim et fratribus ejus- 
dem domus totum Batinum tam in bosco quam in plano tam in longum 
quam in latum quantum ad me et heredes meos. pertinuit excepta 
cultura Simonis Blundi et totam terram in longum et latum a Kalde- 
keld’ per cilium montis sicut vetus fossatum se extendit usque ad 
eandem viam que venit a monte versus ecclesiam sancti Felicis usque 
ad curtilagium Gaufridi Codi et ita per curtilagium Wilekini* de 
Monte usque ad curtilagium uxoris Roberti Heremite et ita per curti- 
lagium Roberti presbiteri sicut fossatum suum extendit descendendo 
usque ad cheminum et ita per eundem cheminum sicut cheminus se 
extendit suptus Rugemunt usque ad Kaldekeldesic et ita ascendendo 
usque ad Kaldechelde; totas prenominatas terras dedi Deo et sancte 
Marie et sancto Johanni Baptiste et beatis pauperibus sancte domus 
prefate hospitalis Jerosolim et fratribus ejusdem domus in liberam puram 
et perpetuam elemosinam sine aliquo retenemento et absque omni 
seculari servicio vel exactione mihi vel heredibus meis pertinente. 
Ego vero et heredes mei warantizabimus prefato hospitali Jerosolim 
et fratribus ejusdem domus totas prenominatas terras inperpetuum 
contra omnes homines in omnibus. His testibus, etc. 


This is entered on the roll for Michaelmas term, 1210.

84. Release by Robert Fossard to Robert de Kirby of the rent of 
3s., being part of the rent of 10s. paid to him by the Templars 
and their men of [Cold] Kirby for the common pasture of Sutton 
[under Whitestone Cliffe] in accordance with the agreement 
made in the court of king John; reserving a rent of 6d. at 
Christmas. [1216-24] 

Byland Chartulary, Egerton MS. 2823, f. 49 (old f. 154). 


Sciant presentes et futuri quod ego Robertus Fossard relaxavi et 
imperpetuum quietum clamavi a me et heredibus meis Roberto de 
Kereby et heredibus suis redditum trium solidorum quos mihi annuatim 
reddere solebat de illis decem solidis quos Templarii et homines sui de 
Kereby mihi annuatim reddunt pro communi pastura de Sutton’ 
que est inter magnam viam que tendit ad Clyueland et cilium clivi in 
lato et in longo sicut continetur in cyrographo facto in curia regis 
Johannis inter Templarios et homines de Kereby petentes et me 
tenentem, ita quod ego Robertus Fossard predictus et heredes mei 
decetero nichil exigemus de predicto Roberto de Kereby et heredibus 
suis occasione predicte concordie nisi sex denarios argenti quos mihi 
et heredibus meis annuatim reddere debent ad Natale Domini. Et 
sciendum quod predictus Robertus de Kereby dedit mihi pro ista 
clamacione quieta et relaxacione decem marcas argenti. His testibus, 
W[Pillelmo] abbate de Ryeualle, etc. 


The chirograph to which. reference is made in this charter and the 
next was drawn up in the king’s presence in Nov. 1209; it describes the 
pasture ‘que est inter magnam viam que tendit ad Cliveland’ et ciliam clivi 
in lato et in longo a divisis factis inter ipsum Robertum et Adam de Bolteby 
usque ad divisas de Killebrun’, which the master of the Templars claimed as 
belonging to his manor of [Cold] Kirby, and Robert claimed as belonging 
to his manor of Sutton ; Robert, whose right was recognized, granted the 
pasture to the master and brethren for the service of 10s. yearly, of which 
one of the Templars specified by name was to pay 3s.1 

If, as seems likely, the present release was made after the death of 
king John, the first witness must be William, abbot of Rievaulx from 1216 
to 1223-24.2, Robert Fossard himself was dead by 1227 (see above).

rent of 7s. which they and their men of [Cold] Kirby paid him 

for the pasture of Sutton [under Whitestone Cliffe], reserving a 

rent of 6d. at Whitsuntide; the terms of the agreement made in 

the king’s court to be maintained. [1209-26] 
Byland Chartulary, Egerton MS. 2823, f. 49 (old f. 154).

The last witness, if he can be identified as the Robert who died before 
1218-19 (see §17), puts back the latest date from 1226. In that case the 
first witness would be Eustace son of Robert de Stuteville III rather than 
Eustace son of Robert V, who was under age in 1219 (p. 17 above). The 
second witness may be William son of Osbert de Skipwith, living early in 
the thirteenth century (no. 68). 


87. Confirmation by Robert Fossard to Henry Fossard his brother, 
and his heirs, of a toft and croft in Sutton [under Whitestone

Omnibus videntibus vel audituris hanc cartam Robertus Fossart 
salutem. Sciatis me concessisse et presenti carta confirmasse Henrico 
Fossart fratri meo pro homagio et servitio suo toftum et croftum in 
Sutton que Walterus filius Carl tenuit cum omnibus pertinentiis et 
aisiamentis que ad tantam terram pertine[n]t in villa et extra; habendum 
et tenendum sibi et heredibus suis de me et heredibus meis in feudo 
et hereditate libere et quiete solute! et honorifice ab omni servitio 
consuetudine et exactione; reddendo mihi et heredibus meis unum 
denarium per annum pro omni servitio ad Natale Domini. Ego autem 
et heredes mei predicto Henrico et heredibus suis predictum toftum 
et croftum cum omnibus pertinentiis et aisiamentis warantizabimus in 
perpetuum. Huis testibus, Willelmo Britone, Johanne de Mundeuile, 
Stephano de Manil, Hugone de Upsale, Roberto Fossart, Roberto de 
Auforde, Petro de Richemund, W. Pinzun, et aliis.

stone Cliffe] to Byland abbey of a knight’s fee in Sutton, 
Marderby, Laysthorpe, Hood and Felixkirk. [1237-38] 


Byland Chartulary, Egerton MS. 2823, f. 105 (old f. 211). 


Sciant omnes tam presentes quam futuri quod ego Thomas 
Fossard filius Ade Fossard de Suttona dedi et concessi et hac presenti

homines imperpetuum. Hiis testibus, Rogero de Stapilt{ona] tunc 
vicecomite Eboracensi, Jordano Hayrun, Adam de Hiltona, Olyuero 
de Buscy, etc. 


The first witness was deputy sheriff of Yorkshire in 1236 and until 
Michaelmas 1238. The gift was presumably made later than Jan. 1236-7, 
when the grantor confirmed to the abbot land and pasture in Sutton and 
Marderby, adding a gift of additional pasture in Sutton (see above). The 
knight’s fee, described as in the same places as in the charter, was the 
subject of a fine dated 5 June 1239 between Hugh Wake and Joan his

160 STUTEVILLE FEE 


wife (as confirmors) and the abbot, the fee to be held of them in frankalmoin 
at a yearly rent of 60s. and by doing the service of a knight’s fee in scutage 
only, other feudal services being expressly excepted.! It was the subject of 
an additional fine, between the abbot and Thomas Fossard in July 1240.7 


e7.. THE BOLIBY Fre 


The knight’s fee held by Adam de Boltebi of Robert de 
Stuteville III in 1166 consisted of 10 carucates in Boltby, Ravens- 
thorpe? and Thirlby, all in par. Felixkirk, and Borrowby, par. 
Leake. 

At the Domesday survey Hugh son of Baldric held 8 caru- 
cates in the first three places ; and additional land in Ravensthorpe 
and land in Borrowby formed part of the king’s manor of North- 
allerton.4 The returns of 1284-85 show that 7 carucates in Boltby, 
Ravensthorpe and Borrowby and 3 carucates in Thirlby were heid 
of the heirs of Baldwin Wake.® 

The family of Boltby was descended from Odo de Boltby 
who gave to Rievaulx abbey land and privileges in Hesketh near 
Boltby, 1142-45 (no. 89), and who was a benefactor of the Hos- 
pitaliers’ preceptory of Mount. St...John, Felaxiark.* It is not 
improbable that he was the ‘Gwido’ de Boltby who harassed the 
monks of Byland when they were preparing their new site for 
their move from Old Byland in 1147.7 His wife’s name was Juetta 
or Judit (nos. 89, 90), and he had three sons Adam, John and 
Jordan (no. 89). The latter can probably be identified with Jordan 
de Boltby, who with Sibyl his wife gave land in Nunnington and 
Fadmoor to Keldholme priory (no. 26 and note).

CHARTERS RELATING TO THE KNIGHTS’ FEES 163 


Adam de Boltby, the tenant of the fee in 1166, succeeded 
before 1160, by which date he gave additional land to Rievaulx 
abbey (no. 90). He confirmed his father’s charter thereto in 1178 
(nos. 91-2). He witnessed a charter to Fountains abbey, also 
witnessed by Robert de Stuteville, sheriff of Yorkshire, 1170-75, 
and a charter of William de Stuteville, 1175-85.2 He was old 
enough to have a nephew Savaric who witnessed a charter to 
Rievaulx abbey, 1170-76.2 He was the predecessor, perhaps the 
father or more likely the grandfather, of a younger Adam de 
Boltby who at Michaelmas 1203 rendered account of 20 marks 
for the fine of his land of Ravensthorpe and Thirlby, namely of a 
knight’s fee of the fee of William de Stuteville.* 


On 7 Nov. 1208, following an assize of mort d’ancestor 
Robert son of Adam quitclaimed to Adam de Boltby his right in 
a carucate of land in Thirlby for 3 marks of silver.* In Nov. 1209, 
following a dispute between the master of the Templars and 
Adam de Boltby relating to certain pasture which the former 
claimed to belong to his manor of Cold Kirby (Kereby) and the 
latter to his manor of Ravensthorpe, the former recognized 
Adam’s right who granted him common pasture for his beasts of 
Cold Kirby for the service of 10s. yearly.6 In 1218-19 it was 
found by a jury that Adam de Boltby had disseised Adam son of 
Jordan de Boltby’ of a tenement in Borrowby, having given him 
a rent of 20s. and taken his homage. On 14 Dec. 1226 Agnes 
daughter of William quitclaimed to Adam de Boltby 2 bovates of 
land in Thirlby.2 He was succeeded by his son Nicholas, who 
described as son of Adam de Boltby made over to the archbishop 
of York all his land at Boltby and Ravensthorpe from 8 Sept. 
1230 for the receipt of seven crops in return for the archbishop’s 
bond dated 24 Oct. of that year to pay 142. 9s. 3d., being the 
debts of Adam de Boltby to Christians and Jews;!° a demise which 
was confirmed by Sir Nicholas de Stuteville." In 1244 Nicholas 
de Boltby brought a case against the abbot of Byland relating 
to a wood in Boltby which he had recovered in an assize of novel 
disseisin against the abbot ;! and on 25 June 1246 he made an 
agreement with the abbot for pasture rights.¥ 


Nicholas de Boltby married Philippa elder daughter and 
coheir of Adam de Tindale, who held the barony of Langley,

Northumberland, in chief by the service of a knight’s fee On 
2 July 1233, after he had made a fine of 20 marks with the king 
for his relief and had done homage, orders were issued to give 
him seisin of Philippa’s share of the lands of Adam de Tindale 
her father, saving the dower of Joan, Adam’s widow ; the share 
of her sister Isabel, wife of Walter son of William de Tunstall, 
being retained in the king’s hand until further orders. In 1236 
Nicholas de Boltby and Walter de Tunstall held the lands com- 
posing the barony of Langley for a knight’s fee ;? and in 1242-43 
Nicholas de Boltby held the knight’s fee entire.4 This suggests 
that Isabel had died in the interval without issue, and that 
Nicholas then became possessed of the whole barony in right of 
his wife. On 16 Oct. 1271 Nicholas de Boltby and his heirs were 
granted free warren in his demesne lands of Langley and other 
places in Northumberland, and also in Boltby, Ravensthorpe and 
Thirlby, co. York.®- He died shortly. Deldre 31. Oct. 1272 ;.and at 
was found that he held in Yorkshire a knight’s fee of 10 carucates 
of Joan de Stuteville which included land, a capital messuage 
and a mill in Ravensthorpe, 22 bovates in Thirlby and 32 bovates 
in Boltby, and 10s. yearly for herbage in Cold Kirby (Kereby);§ 
and also, in Northumberland, Langley and other places, held of 
the king in chief of the inheritance of Philippa his wife, daughter 
and heir of Adam de Tindale, by the service of a knight’s fee ; 
Adam their son being their heir, of full age.? In addition to his 
son he had a daughter. In 1244-45, when possessed of the marriage 
of Walter de Karrow, he was to restore it if he did not marry his 
daughter, described as the archbishop’s kinswoman, to Walter.® 
On 13 Dec. 1272 orders were issued for the delivery to Adam de 
Boltby of the lands of Philippa his mother, deceased, which his 
father had held by curtesy ;® and in 1277 he was holding a knight’s 
fee in chief.° He had two daughters and coheirs, Isabel and Eve. 
Isabel married Thomas de Lucy, son of Alan de Multon by his 
wife Alice de Lucy ;4 and Eve married Alan son of John de Walking- 
ham. In 1279 Alan brought a case against Adam de Boltby to 
keep an agreement made between them relating to the manor of 
Boltby and 10 librates and 5 marks’ worth of land in Langley

and Allerwash (Halweley).1 On 4 Feb. 1281-2, after Adam’s death, 
orders were issued to deliver to Thomas de Lucy and Isabel his 
wife the manor of Langley whereof Adam had enfeoffed them, 
taken into the king’s hand on account of a contention between 
them and Alan de Walkingham and Eve his wife, saving to the 
latter their right when they wished to speak thereof.2 It appears 
that the barony of Langley, or the greater part of it, descended 
in the Lucy family ;3 and that the Yorkshire property, held of the 
Stuteville fee, was inherited by Eve. At the death of Baldwin 
Wake, shortly before 10 Feb. 1281-2 Alan de Walkingham held 
of him a knight’s fee in Boltby, Ravensthorpe and Thirlby.* At 
his own death shortly before 24 Dec. 1283 he held in Yorkshire, 
in addition to several lands (which included Cowthorpe) in his 
own right, the manor of Ravensthorpe with Eve his wife of [the 
heirs of] Sir Baldwin Wake; and in Northumberland land in 
Allerwash® in. chief which they had of the gift of Sir Adam de 
Boltby, her father ; his heir being Adam his son aged [?] 34 years.® 

On 6 May 1284 Eve widow of Alan de Walkingham, tenant 
in chief, was granted a licence to marry whomsoever she would 
for a fine of 8042.7 Her second husband was Richard Knoute, who 
with her held the immediate tenancy of the heirs of Baldwin Wake 
in Boltby, Ravensthorpe, Borrowby and Thirlby in 1284-85, 
Thirlby being held of them by Anora de Boltby in dower.® She 
married thirdly as his second wife Wiliam de Cantilupe, who 
was summoned to parliament from 1299 until his death in 1308.9 
As Eve de Cantilupe she was holding Boltby and Thirlby in 1316.1° 
It is evident that her son Adam de Walkingham died without 
surviving issue, for in 1346 her representative in Thirlby, Ravens- 
_ thorpe, and Boltby was Nicholas de Cantilupe, younger son and 
eventual heir of her third husband.” The tenancy of the Boltby 
fee descended in the Cantilupe family.¥

This is evidently the gift of the donor which is described in the con-

more fully in that of Richard I as ‘terram et wastinam de subtus Hestescaith 
et communem pasturam de Boltebi et de Ravenestorp et Trillebi’.6 As ina 
list of benefactions Odo’s gift of Hestelscait with its appurtenances is said 
to have been made to abbot William,’ who died in 1145,8 that can be taken 
as the latest date for the present charter. As Roger abbot of Byland wit- 
nessed the earliest date is 1142.9 Hesketh grange is marked on the 1” map

(ii) BORROWBY 


93. Gift by Ralph son of Uctred de Borrowby, with the assent of 
his lord William de Stuteville and of his own sons Roger, Richer 
and Nicholas, to Rievaulx abbey of 11 acres of meadow in the 
territory of Borrowby intermixed with the meadows of the 
bishop of Durham, and extending to Cod beck; and liberty to 
carry a spring of water through his land to the abbey grange of 
Crosby. [1183-1203] 

MS. Dodsworth vii, f. 139, from the original formerly in St. Mary’s 
Tower, York. Pd. (incompletely) in Rievaulx Chartulary, 
no. 320, from Dodsworth. 

Omnibus sancte matris ecclesie filiis Radulfus filius Huctredi de 
Bergebi salutem. Sciatis me dedisse et hac presenti carta mea con- 
firmasse Deo et ecclesie sancte Marie de Rieualle et monachis ibidem 
Deo servientibus pro salute anime mee et omnium parentum et heredum

mei et Rogeri et Richerii et Nicolai filiorum meorum in liberam et 
perpetuam elemosinam undecim acras prati in territorio de Berghebi 
que jacent in septem locis inter prata domini episcopi Dunelmensis et 
tendunt in longum usque ad Cotesbec; tenendas in perpetuum liberas 
et quietas ab omni terreno servitio et exactione seculari. Preterea dedi 
et concessi eis fontem et aquam quam duxerunt per terram meam per 
conductum ad grangiam eorum de Crossebi; tenendum cum ipso 
conductu in perpetuum et emendandum libere quotiens opus fuerit. 
Et ego et heredes mei adquietabimus et warentizabimus hec omnia 
predictis monachis contra omnes homines in perpetuum. Hiis testibus, 
Ernaldo de Upsale, Gaufrido Fossart, Gaufrido de Boscowill[elm]i, 
Johanne clerico de Hamesthuait, Andrea de Magnebi, Roberto filio 
Guum, Henrico clerico, Britone, Roberto Borart, Henrico Foliot, 
Roberto Norrais. 


Cod beck flows about a mile on the west side of Borrowby, and Crosby 
grange lies just beyond it. The limits of date are those of William de Stute- 
ville’s tenure of the fee in. chief. 

A carucate in Borrowby, where the greater portion of the vill formed 
a member of the bishop of Durham’s fee,* was held by the family of Borrowby 
as an under-tenancy of the Boltby fee. In 1284-85 Michael de Borrowby

$8. THE ‘CORDANVILLED PEE 


The knight’s fee held by Peter de Cordanvill of Robert de 
Stuteville III in 1166 lay in Sherburn, wap. Buckrose in the 
East Riding. It can be identified as the knight’s fee pertaining 
to the manor of Buttercrambe which in 1282 was held in Sherburn 
by John de Vescy of Baldwin Wake.* 

At the Domesday survey, apart from the 3 carucates in 
Sherburn which formed a berewick of the archbishop’s manor of 
Weaverthorpe, 9 carucates there, forming a berewick of the 
manor of Langton, were held by Hugh son of Baldric, and 6 
carucates by the count of Mortain.® It can be supposed that 
Hugh son of Baldric’s interest in Sherburn, which presumably 
passed to Robert de Stuteville I, was among the lands restored to 
Robert de Stuteville III as a tenancy-in-chief. 

Apart from his tenure in 1166 and his gifts to Guisborough 
priory of the church of Sherburn with half a carucate of land 
and tithe (nos. 94-5), practically nothing seems to be known of 
Peter de Cordanville; nor is there any clue to his relationship to 
Adam de Cordanville of Sherburn who occurs at a later date 
(no. 96). Peter’s charter to Guisborough (no. 94) shows that he 
held a carucate of land in Ugthorpe, par. Lythe in the North 
Riding, of Wiliam de Hamby. Apparently in 1161 William de 
Hamby demised 2 carucates in Ugthorpe to Guisborough, his 
charter being witnessed by Alan de Munceus ‘dominus et testis’.® 
In Ugthorpe, where at the Survey 4 carucates were terra regis,” 
the feudal chain in the twelfth and thirteenth centuries was 
extensive and complex. Peter de Cordanville’s interest was low 
down in the hierarchy of tenures there ; and there is evidence 
that the family of Monceaux, of whom Wiliam de Hamby held 
his land, held their fee in Ugthorpe of the family of Argentom.® 
After Peter’s death William de Argentum confirmed to Guis- 
borough priory his gift of a carucate in Ugthorpe.® 

It is significant that both in Ugthorpe and Sherburn an 
interest was held by the family of Everingham, and it seems 
likely that it was to that family that the Cordanville interest 
passed. In 1202 Robert de Everingham was vouched to warranty 
by the prior of Guisborough in respect of 3 carucates and 3 bovates

in Ugthorpe ;! and with the assent of Hawise his wife he demised

The gift ‘ex dono Petri de Cordavilla ecclesiam de Schireburna cum 
dimidia carucata terre et decimam molendinorum suorum in Schireburna 
et unam carucatam in Uggethorp’ was included in king Henry II’s con-

The charter by which Peter’s gift in Ugthorpe was confirmed by 
William de Argentum after his death (see above), is dated by Farrer as. 
1175-85. The latest date for the present charter is 1183 as Robert de Stute- 
ville III was alive. The later clauses suggest that it was issued as a 
comprehensive instrument subsequently to no. 95. 


95. Notification by Robert the dean and the chapter of St. Peter’s, 
York, that Peter de Cordanville had confirmed in their presence 
his gift to Guisborough priory of the church of Sherburn with 
half a carucate of land, the priory granting that he and his heirs 
should nominate a clerk of eighteen years of age in good condition 
to be received as a canon on each vacancy in the priorate; and 
that Robert the clerk had withdrawn the claim which he had 
in the church. [c. 1150-70]

CHARTERS RELATING TO JHE KNIGHTS’ FEES 173 


It is probable that the first five witnesses were members of the chapter ; 
and it may be that canonicis rather than canonico is intended for the word 
after Serlo. The dates when these men occur as canons show a long period 
of tenure for some of them.! Ralph de St. Columba occurs as early as 
c. 1121-1128 ;? and he and Thomas Sotwame occur together in 1138-42 
and 1153-543 Thomas Sotwame and master Laurence occur together in 
1149-53.4 The dates for William son of Tosti, however, suggest some such 
period as 1150-80. It seems likely, therefore, that the charter was 
issued in the period assigned above, and early rather than late within those 
limits. The dean may be either Robert de Gant or Robert Butevilain, and 
more probably the former. 


96. Gift by Adam de Cordanville of Sherburn to Malton priory of 
a bovate of land and a toft in Sherburn. [ante 1246] 


Malton Chartulary, Cotton MS. Claudius D. xi, f. 146V. 


Omnibus Christi fidelibus visuris vel audituris has literas Adam 
de Cordauile de Skireburn in Hauerfordlid salutem. Noveritis me 
divine pietatis intuitu et fraterne dilectionis respectu concessisse 
dedisse et hac presenti carta mea confirmasse Deo et beate [Marie]° 
et conventui confratrum meorum de Veteri Malton’ unam bovatam 
terre in territorio de Skireburn’ in Hauerfordlid cum uno tofto in

infra villam et extra cum pratis et pascuis et pasturis et in ouenham® 
ubicunque acciderit, unam scilicet de duabus bovatis terre quas Adam 
clericus aliquando tenuit de me, illam scilicet que jacet propinquior 
orienti, illum itaque toftum qui est propinquior tofto Ricardi de 
Riuario versus aquilonem; tenendos et habendos de me et heredibus 
meis in puram et perpetuam elemosinam pacifice libere et quiete ab 
omni seculari servicio et exaccione ad me et ad heredes meos pertinente. 
Et ego Adam de Cordauile et heredes mei warantizabimus prenominatam 
bovatam cum tofto predicto conventui in perpetuum contra omnes 
homines ab omnibus secularibus serviciis et consuetudinibus et ex- 
accionibus. Huis t[estibus], etc. 


Robert son of Robert de Eueringham, for the health of himself and 
Isabel his wife, confirmed this bovate with the toft of the gift of Adam de 
Cordauile (f. 147). This Robert de Everingham the younger died in 1246.7 

Another reference to the grantor is given in a charter of Ingenald son 
of Henry de Willerby to Bridlington priory, giving an acre in Willerby, 
wap. Dickering, adjacent to land formerly belonging to Adam de Cordavilla 
of Sherburn.? Willerby (a member of the Gant fee) is only separated from 
Sherburn by the parish of Ganton.

so, THE CAPTOPRT PEE 


Roger de Cnapetot held 11 bovates for an eighth part of 
a knight’s fee of the new feoffment of Robert de Stuteville III in 
1166. These lay in Barnby, par. Bossall, where land formed part 
of the 7 carucates there and in Bossall and Buttercrambe as a 
berewick belonging to the manor of Scrayingham, held by Hugh 
son of Baldric at the Domesday survey.t In 1282 Reginald de 
Captoft held of Baldwin Wake an eighth of a fee in Barnby.? 
Several references are available to members of the family, but 
they are insufficient to establish the descent.® 

Thus a Reiner or Reginald de Capetot or Capetoft witnessed 
a charter of Robert de Stuteville III to Rievaulx abbey, c. 1160- 
1183 (no. 10); another charter dated 1166 ;4 a charter of William 
de Stuteville, 1175-85,° and others of his in the period 1190-1203 
(nos. 26, 27, 29). He or another Reginald de Capetoft witnessed 
a Cawton charter (no. 77), and others printed m the present 
volume. A Reginald de Capetot’ or Capetoft occurs as a knight 
in Yorkshire in 1203, and witnessed a charter dated 1205.7 Roger 
de Kap[toft} occurs as a witness c. 1190-1199, and as Roger de 
Capetot as a knight in Yorkshire in 1200.9 In 1246 Reginald de 
Capetoft and Helewise his wife gave Geoffrey de Upsall lands in 
South Kilvington and Thornbrough in that parish ;!° and in 1302-03 
the ‘heir of Captoft’ held half a carucate of the Vescy fee in Swan- 
land and North Ferriby.™ 


S10. TH BOSSALL FEE 


In 1282 it was recorded that among the knights’ fees per- 
taining to the manor of Buttercrambe, held by Baldwin Wake, 
was a holding by John de Bossall of three-quarters of a knight’s 
fee in Bossall and Claxton in that parish. This holding evidently 
included the 24 carucates which at the Domesday survey formed 
a berewick of the manor of Scrayingham, held by Hugh son of 
Baldric, and in addition part of the 3 carucates in Barnby, par. 
Bossall, also a berewick of the same manor; and the interest in 
Claxton perhaps consisted of the carucate held there by the
