count of Mortain.! It can be deduced that the three-quarters of a 
knight’s fee was created subsequently to 1166 by a feoffment 
made for the most part from the demesne lands pertaining to 
Buttercrambe. 

The earliest recorded member of the Bossall family appears 
to be Norman de Bossall who witnessed a feoffment to Robert 
de Stuteville III of land in Ingleby Greenhow in a charter dated 
1166 ;2 and also a charter of Robert de Stuteville III, c. 1170- 
1183 (no. 16), and one by which William de Stuteville confirmed 
Cayton to Fountains abbey, 1175-85.2 He was probably the 
predecessor, and perhaps father,4 of Richard de Bossall who 
witnessed two Beningbrough charters c. 1180-90,° and who 
acquired an interest in Ganton and Staxton by marriage. Richard’s 
wife was Maud daughter of Henry son of Richard de Ganton. 
Henry de Ganton confirmed a gift in Staxton which Richard his 
father made to Bridlington priory, 1180-1200.6 Maud de Ganton, 
described as daughter of Henry de Ganton and wife of Richard 
de Bossall gave an undertaking to her lord Richard de Percy 
with regard to her inheritance.? In 1206 Richard de Bossall and 
Maud his wife were parties to a fine of land in Ganton, the land 
being held in her right. The charter printed below shows that 
in 1234 she gave to Maud her granddaughter land in Staxton, which 
was confirmed by her heir Richard de Bossall (the younger), the 
donee’s father. In 1232 Maud de Ganton in her widowhood gave 
a carucate in Fordon® to Whitby abbey, which was confirmed on 
the same occasion by Richard de Bossall ;° the latter evidently 
being her son.- With Richard de Bossall her son and heir she also 
gave to St. Peter's hospital, York, a toit and creft in Ganton; 
which was confirmed by her (second) husband William Francigena 
of Ganton?

Richard de Bossall II occurs as the patron of the church of 
Bossall in 1225 and 1233, making a presentation of William de 
Stuteville, clerk, in 1231.1 He married Mabel daughter of a 
Robert de Stuteville (no. 97); and died before 5 Feb. 1237-8, when 
the patronage of the church was in the hands of dame Dervorguilla 
de Stuteville by reason of her custody of his land and heir.2 His 
successor was William de Bossall, still a minor in 1244.3 William 
de Bossall made gifts of land and privileges in Staxton to Brid- 
lington priory in 1256, naming his predecessor Henry de Ganton.4 
In 1257 he was granted free warren in his demesne lands in 
Bossall, Ganton and Staxton ;°> and as a knight witnessed an 
agreement made by dame Joan de Stuteville in 1269.6 He made 
a presentation to Bossall church on 10 Feb. 1269-70 ;? and in 1277 
he received a discharge of financial obligations in respect of his 
office of sheriff of Yorkshire in 1264 and 1265 which he had been 
hindered almost completely from exercising by reason of the 
disturbances. He must have died before 13 Jan. 1279-80 when 
an institution to Bossall church was made on the presentation of 
Sir John de Bossall, knt.,2 who was presumably his son and heir. 

John de Bossall’s tenure of the three-quarters of a knight’s 
fee in Bossall and Claxton in 1282 has been noted above. He 
died on or Detore 7 July of that year ; on 4 Sépty the escheator 
was ordered to take into the king’s hand the lands which he had 
held as a tenant-in-chief ;! and on 28 Oct. 1283 a grant was made 
of the wardship of his lands during the minority of his heirs. On 
10 April 1290 it was found by inquisition that Nicholas son and 
heir of John de Bossall had been born at Ganton on 29 Sept. 1268.12 

The family continued in the male line until the fifteenth 
century, when the interest in Bossall passed to Richard Redman, 
who has been identified as a younger son of Sir Richard Redman 
of Harewood and Elizabeth Aldburgh his wife.

Although Staxton did not form part of the Bossall tenancy of the 
Stuteville fee, the charter is included here to show the evidence for the 
Bossall-Stuteville marriage. On chronological grounds it can be supposed 
that Mabel daughter of Robert de Stuteville, who married Richard de 
Bossall II, was born c. 1190; but in the absence of other evidence it is 
unsafe to make any definite suggestion for the identification of her father. 
The only likely possibility seems to be the first Robert de Stuteville of 
Great Ayton ; but if so Mabel would have been a daughter by a first wife, 
for he did not marry Emma Malebisse until 1207.7 


Sit. THE LANGTON FEE 


In 1282 it was recorded that John de Vescy, in addition to 
the knight’s fee which he held in Sherburn (see §8), held a knight’s 
fee in Langton [E.R.], and that it was also appurtenant to the 
manor of Buttercrambe, held by Baldwin Wake.® 


1 An abbreviated text from another source, apparently the original, 
is pd. in Whitby Chartulary, i, 140n. 

2 See Chapter I, §5. 

3 Cal. Ing. p.m., ii, no. 439. 


178 STUTEVILLE FEE 


At the Domesday survey Hugh son of Baldric held 39 caru- 
cates in the manor of Langton and its berewicks in five places ; 
and according to the Summary 18 carucates lay in Langton, and 
in the berewicks 9 carucates lay in Sherburn, 6 in Burdale, 3 in 
East Heslerton, 28 in Kennythorpe, and one in Raisthorpe.! 
here1s- clear ee that Robert de Stuteville I held an interest 
in Langton (see the note to charter no. 1); and Robert de Stute- 
ville III, whose daughter Burga had Langton as her maritagiwm 
when she married William de Vescy,? also possessed an interest. 

The interest of Burga de Stuteville in Langton, held by the 
service of a knight’s fee, thus passed to her Vescy descendants, of 
whom John de Vescy, the tenant in 1282, was her great-grandson. 
In 1208 a claim was made by Thurstan de Montfort, by reason of 
his descent from Juliana daughter of Geoffrey Murdac, against 
Eustace de Vescy (Burga’s son and heir) for a moiety of the vill 
of Langton’ In 1302-03 12 carucates in Langton, making a 
knight’s fee, were returned as of the Wake fee, of which Isabel 
de Vescy (widow of William de Vescy) held 10 carucates.* After 
the death at Bannockburn in 1314 of William de Vescy of Kildare, 
the bastard son of William de Vescy, who had been enfeoffed of 
considerable estates by his father, Thomas Wake petitioned the 
king for the reversion of the manor of Langton ;> and at the 
inquisition held in 1315, after the death of Isabel widow of William 
de Vescy the elder, it was found that she had held the manor of 
Langton in dower of the heir of John Wake by knight-service.® 

There is nothing in these references to suggest that the 
Wake tenure was otherwise than a tenure-in-chief ; and in the 
inquisition held after the death of Joan de Stuteville, mother of 
Baldwin Wake, in 1276 it was recorded that she held Cottingham, 
Buttercrambe, Scrayingham and Langton of the king in chief by 
the service of 3 knights, of which John de Vescy held the manor 
of Langton.? But in an inquisition held in 1301 after the death 
of Roger de Mowbray it is stated that among his Yorkshire lands 
were Langton and Wintringham held by William de Vescy of

Langton was included then in error with other Vescy tenures of

taken place. As her son Eustace was born in the period 1169-71 
(note to no. 98) it can be suggested that the date of the marriage 
and the gift of Langton took place in the period 1167-70.

This is preceded by a charter of William de Vescy (Wesci) making the 
same gift, for his own welfare and that of his wife and heirs. 

William de Vescy died before Michaelmas 1183, when his lands had 
fallen into the king’s hand.? In 1185, when his widow was living, his heir 
was aged fourteen and in the king’s wardship.* This heir was his son Eustace, 
who during the year ending at Michaelmas 1189, when the lands were 
still in the king’s hand, received a payment of 3s. per day amounting to 
54l1. 15s. for the year.» He had come of age by Michaelmas 1190, when 
he owed sums for fines for his lands in Northumberland and Yorkshire and 
for marrying at will, and when he rendered account for his scutage.® The 
dates show that his birth can be placed within the limits of 1169 and 1171. 
The ‘pro salute’ clause suggests that Burga’s father, Robert de Stuteville III, 
was alive when she issued the charter.

$12. THE UPSALD FEE 


In 1282 it was recorded that Geoffrey de Upsall held a 
knight’s fee in Upsall, [South] Kilvington and Thornbrough of 
Baldwin Wake, and that it was appurtenant to the manor of 
Buttercrambe.! 

At the Domesday survey 13 carucates in these places were 
held by the count of Mortain, of whom Richard [de Surdeval] 
held a tenancy in Upsall.2. The charter by which William de 
Stuteville confirmed the three vills to Arnold de Upsall (no. 29) 
states that they had been held by Ralph de Upsall, Arnold’s an- 
cestor, of Ralph Paynel by the service of a knight’s fee. It is 
probable that in Upsall as elsewhere, Richard de Surdeval’s holding 
passed to Ralph Paynel by marriage ;3 and this may explain the 
origin of the latter’s tenure of the three vills. It is clear, however, 
that an interest passed subsequently to the Mowbrays. It was 
recorded in 1284-85 that Roger de Mowbray held a knight’s fee in 
[South] Kilvington, Thornbrough and Upsall ; of the 5 carucates 
in South Kilvington the church was endowed with 5 bovates, the 
prior of Newburgh held 6 bovates and the remainder was held of 
the heirs of Baldwin Wake, who held of Roger de Mowbray, who 
held in chief; and the 4 carucates in Upsall and Thornbrough 
were held by Hugh de Upsall of the same heirs similarly. In 
1316 the tenant of the three places was Geoffrey de Upsall (the 
younger).® 

There is no reason to suppose that any interest in the three 
places had been held by Robert de Stuteville I, or formed part 
of the holdings of Robert de Stuteville III, either in chief or as a 
sub-tenure of Roger de Mowbray in 1166, or, indeed, was acquired 
by the Stuteville family before the time of William de Stuteville. 
The date when William de Stuteville acquired the interest is not 
known ; but his charter shows that it was acquired earlier than 
1191-94. It cannot, therefore, have formed part of the 9 knights’ 
fees which he received from William de Mowbray in augmentation, 
when the dispute was settled in 1201.6 

In view of William de Stuteville’s charter Ralph de Upsall 
must have been living early in the twelfth century, and his des- 
cendant Arnold de Upsall late in that century. Arnold de Upsall 
witnessed a charter of William Ingram to Byland abbey, 1180- 
c. 1200,’ and one of William de Stuteville making a grant in his 
fee of Ripley to Fountains abbey, c. 1190-1203. In 1195 he was 
amerced in Yorkshire, paying the balance of his fine in the follow- 
ing year ;* and in the period c. 1183-1203, as lord of Upsall, he 
made agreements with Adam Fossard, his neighbour at Marderby,

a term of the settlement being that he should marry Juliana, 
Adam's sister (nos. 81-2). In 1244 Geoffrey de Upsall (the elder) 
and his heirs had a grant of free warren in his demesne lands in 
Upsall.t 

The difficulty of deciding in whose hand the tenancy-in-chief 
resided at any particular period is shown by a plea in 1210 when 
Geoffrey Luterel complained that his service from a knight’s fee 
with appurtenances in Upsall held of his fee by Arnold de Upsall 
was in arrear ; Arnold agreed that the knight’s fee was held of 
Geoffrey's fee, but said that he held it of Nicholas de Stuteville 
whom he vouched to warranty.? There is no mention here of the 
Mowbrays ; and it is clear that Geoffrey Luterel’s interest was 
derived from his inheritance as heir of William Paynel of Hooton 
Pagnell, grandson of Ralph Paynel and his second wife Maud 
presumed daughter of Richard de Surdeval.? There is also a 
record of the manor of Upsall being held of Sir Andrew Luterel 
at the end of the fourteenth century.4 

There was another holding where a member of the Upsall 
family appears in a subordinate position to the Stutevilles. This 
was at Kirby Knowle, about a mile to the east of Upsall, which at 
the Domesday survey was a berewick of 3 carucates of the manor 
of Bagby, held in chief by Hugh son of Baldric.5 In 1284-85 it was 
recorded that the whole vill of 5 carucates was held by Roger de 
Lascelles of Hugh de Upsall, who held of the heirs of Baldwin 
Wake, who held of Roger de Mowbray.® If this is correct, it can 
be suggested that the berewick of Kirby Knowle formed part of 
the feoffment made to Robert de Stuteville III by Roger de 
Mowbray I.

It is probable that Ralph de Amundeville, the first witness to the 
present charter, whose superior interest is shown by the next, issued on the 
same occasion, can be identified as Ralph, a younger son of Jocelin de 
Amundeville, who held Long Preston of the Percy fee, or with his son a 
younger Ralph. In the period 1196-1200 the younger Ralph claimed a 
knight’s fee in Moor Monkton and Bilbrough, places which had been held 
by Ralph Paynel in 1086 ; and the Amundeville interest in South Kilvington 
may well have been due to Ralph Paynel’s tenancy-in-chief, noted above, 
especially as there is evidence to suppose that Beatrice, the elder Ralph’s 
mother, was a daughter of Ralph Paynel.!. The younger Ralph de Amunde- 
ville was dead in 1201, which fixes the latest date for these charters. It 
cannot be decided whether the Ralph therein mentioned was the father 
or son, though the former is more likely. ; 

The grantee, as Ralph de Beauver, Belveir or Belver, witnessed a 
South Cave charter in 1154 ;4 and occurs in 1150-c. 1170 and 1170-85.3 He 
and Constance his wife, and also Ralph de Amundeville (who gave a rent 
from the mill of Long Preston), were benefactors of St. Clement’s priory, 
York, not later than 1175.4 Gamaliel de Kilvington, one of.the witnesses 
to the present charters, occurs in 1170-86.° In his MS. transcript of the 
charters Farrer suggested c. 1180-c. 1190 as their date—a period which is 
supported by the occurrence of the fourth, fifth and sixth witnesses.

o13.. FHE SEINGSBY FE 


In 1282 it was recorded that Robert Chambard held a 
knight’s fee in Slingsby of Baldwin Wake, and that it was appur- 
tenant to the manor of Buttercrambe.t 

At the Domesday survey 14 carucates in Slingsby were 
held by the count of Mortain, and other land there (probably a 
carucate) formed a berewick of the manor of Hovingham, held by 
Hugh son of Baldric.? There is evidence that in the twelfth 
century a knight’s fee in Slingsby was held of the Paynels of 
Hooton Pagnell, who descended from Richard de Surdeval, an 
important tenant of the count of Mortain in several places ; and 
in 1212 William Bastard, husband of Isabel daughter and coheir 
of William Paynel, was plaintiff against Nicholas de Stuteville 
for services due to him from Nicholas from the tenement held of 
him in Slingsby, namely a knight’s fee.2 The. Paynel interest 
passed to the Mowbrays, possibly in a similar manner as the 
knight’s fee in Upsall (see §12), being united to any interest that 
the Mowbrays held in Slingsby as an appurtenance of Hovingham. 
In 1284-85 it was recorded that 15 carucates in Slingsby (the 
total of the Domesday assessment) were held of the Mowbray 
fee ; of these 74 carucates were held directly of Roger de Mowbray 
by William de Wyville, and of the other moiety 5 carucates were 
held by John Chaumbard of the heir of [Baldwin] Wake, who 
held them of Roger de Mowbray, one carucate formed the en- 
dowment of the church, the Templars and Kirkham priory each 
held 2-bovates, and Malton priory held the remaining carucate ; 
while in another return half a knight’s fee was held directly of 
Roger de Mowbray by William de Wyville and half a knight’s 
fee by John Chamberd, who held it of the Wake heirs who held 
it of Roger de Mowbray.! 

Some notes on the early generations of the family of Cham- 
bord or Chambard have been given with the charters of Robert 
Chambord un the reign of Henry II, giving or confirming’ to 
Marrick priory and Rievaulx abbey land in East Cowton, where 
the family held an under-tenancy of the honour of Richmond ;5

$14. THE FLAWIITH AND THOLTHORPE FEES 


In 1282 among the fees pertaining to the manor of Butter- 
crambe were half a knight’s fee held by William [?] Boneface and 
Luke de Flawith (Flathewath) in Flawith and Tholthorpe, both in 
par. Alne, and in Tholthorpe a quarter of a knight’s fee held by 
William de Mortimer and another quarter held by Sibyl widow of 
Geoffrey de Mortimer.? 

At the Domesday survey 7 carucates in Tholthorpe and 
Wide Open (in Skelton) were soke of the vill of Helperby, held 
by the archbishop of York ;3 and there is no mention of Flawith. 
There appears to be no clue to the origin of the interest of Baldwin 
Wake in these places or to its association with the manor of 
Buttercrambe. 

With regard to the first half knight’s fee it can be noted 
that Eustace son of Alan Boneface quitclaimed land in the neigh- 
bourhood of Flawith to St. Mary’s, York, c. 1180-1194 ;4 and 
that in 1252 Luke de Flawith and Agnes his wife gave to St. 
Leonard’s hospital 2 bovates in Flawith which were evidently 
held in her right.® 

With regard to the Mortimer interest it is significant that 
in 1265 it was recorded that Geoffrey de Mortimer had been of the 
household of Baldwin Wake and held land in Eakley in Stoke 
Goldington, co. Buckingham.* He died on 29 Sept. 1272, holding 
land in that county of the king in chief through the heir of Ralph 
de Mortimer, who was under age and in the wardship of dame 
Joan Wake, by the service of a sixth of a knight’s fee ; he left a 
widow Sibyl and William his son was his heir.’ There can be no 
doubt that they were the tenants, each of a quarter of a knight’s 
fee, in Tholthorpe in 1282. 

In 1284-85 4 carucates 2 bovates are given as the amount 
of land in Tholthorpe ; of these William de Mortimer held 2 caru- 
cates 3 bovates and the earl Marshal® 3 bovates of the fee of 
Baldwin Wake, who [had] held in chief ; the church had an en- 
dowment of 2 bovates ; and the abbot of Byland held 2 bovates 
and a toft.® 7

CHARTERS RELATING TO THE KNIGHTS’ FEES 185 


Sis. THE STILLINGPLEET FEE 


In 1282 Robert de Grey held half a knight’s fee in Stilling- 
fleet among the fees pertaining to the manor of Buttercrambe.! 

At the Domesday survey the assessment of Stillingfleet was 
4 carucates, of which a moiety was held by Erneis de Burun, and 
of the remainder 1$ carucates formed a manor held by Hugh son 
of Baldric, who also held a manor of one carucate in Moreby in 
the same parish.? Robert de Stuteville I held an interest in Moreby 
(charter no. 1); and it can be assumed that Hugh son of Baldric’s 
holding in Stillingfleet also passed to him. It is probable that 
these holdings were restored to Robert de Stuteville III with the 
manor of Cottingham and other portions of his inheritance. 

In 1230 archbishop Walter Gray purchased land in Moreby 
from Agnes de Manfield,* evidently land held of the honour of 
Richmond ; and in 1234 he also purchased 12 bovates in Stilling- 
fleet.» These purchases were presumably made with other lands, 
including Rotherfield, for the benefit of the archbishop’s brother 
Robert ;§ and in 1240 Robert de Grey purchased a carucate in 
Stillingfleet.’ His son Walter de Grey was the father of Robert 
de Grey of Rotherfield,8 who can therefore be identified as Baldwin 
Wake’s tenant in 1282. 

In 1284-85 details are recorded of 5 carucates in Stillingfleet ; 
of these Robert de Gray held 34 carucates, being 2 carucates of 
the heirs of Baldwin Wake, who held in chief, and 14 carucates of 
the heirs of Robert Trussebut who held of Robert de Ros in 
chief; and another carucate was held by Gilbert de Luthe of 
Jordan Foliot, who held of [the heirs of] Baldwin Wake in chief.® 
' Both carucates in Moreby are given as held of the honour of 
Richmond, of which one was held by Robert de Gray”?

186 STUTEVLILLE FEE 


At the Domesday survey Nigel [Fossard} held 6 carucates in 
Clifford, par. Bramham, of the count of Mortain.! Clifford became 
a member of the Fossard tenancy-in-chief, later held by the family 
of Mauley. An under-tenancy was held both in Clifford and 
Bramham by the family of Birdsall,? also known by the name of 
Mowthorpe, Of this family some notes have been given in a 
previous volume in respect of its tenancy of the Paynel fee in 
Terrington and Mowthorpe.* Robert son of William de Birdsall 
and Adam his son were donors to Kirkstall abbey of land and 
pasture in Clifford. Adam son of Robert was dead in 1211, and 
Sara daughter of William de Mowthorpe (apparently Adam’s son 
and heir) was married to Anketin Malore by 1230.5 At the in- 
quisition held in 1275 after the death of Nicholas son of Anketin 
Malore it was found that he held 3 carucates in Clifford of the 
fee of Sir Peter de Mauley, his heirs being his four sisters ;§ and in 
1284-85 the 24 carucates there were held of the heirs of Baldwin 
Wake, who held of Peter de Mauley.? At one of the inquisitions 
held in 1279 after the death of Peter de Mauley II it was found 
that Baldwin Wake held of him 2 knights’ fees in Bramham 
and Clifford.’ 

It is clear that the Stuteville interest in Clifford was due 
to the gift made to William de Stuteville of the service of Adam 
son of Robert de Birdsall for all his tenement which he held there 
of William Fossard (no. 36 above). It can be supposed that this 
mesne tenancy became associated with the manor of Buttercrambe 
for administrative convenience. Certainly no interest in Clifford 
had been an appurtenance of that manor during its tenure by 
Robert de Stuteville I or when the manor was restored to Robert 
de Stuteville ITT as a tenancy-in-chief.

Ralph de Mortemer, Gilbert Tison held a manor of 7 carucates in 
Hessle, a church and a priest being recorded.1 Gilbert also held, 
as his sole tenure in Lincolnshire, a manor of 2 carucates 73 bovates 
im South Ferriby ;* and in 1115-18, Gilbert Tison’s name being 
mentioned in the entry, Nigel d’Aubigny held 2 carucates 63 
bovates in the same place.? The subsequent association of in- 
terests held by the family of Hessle in Hessle and South Ferriby, 
situated on either side of the river Humber, as Stuteville under- 
tenants, and the fact that the service of Gilbert Tison for his 
lands in the East Riding was included in the feoffment made by 
Henry I to Nigel d’Aubigny,* and so passed to the honour of 
Mowbray, suggest that Gilbert’s interests in both Hessle and 
South Ferriby formed part of the 10 knights’ fees which Roger 
de Mowbray granted as an under-tenancy to Robert de Stuteville 
III. There is, however, the discrepancy in the various records, of 
which several examples have already been given, as to whether 
the tenancy-in-chief was held by the Stutevilles and their Wake 
descendants or by the Mowbrays. 

The main line of the family of Hessle descended from Ivo 
de Karkeni,® who with the consent of John his son gave the church 
of Hessle to Guisborough priory (no. 101). In the early years of 
Henry II, as Ivo de Karkenii, he restored to Herbert son of Ade- 
lard the land which Adelard had held of him and his ancestors in 
South Ferriby, saving the moiety of the church which at Adelard’s 
request he had given to Bullington priory.6 The charters printed 
below show that Ivo was succeeded by his son John de Hessle and 
his grandson Robert. John de Hessle and his son Robert made 
gifts to the priory of North Ferriby of land in Hessle and South 
Ferriby, one of John’s charters being confirmed by Robert de 
Stuteville III c. 1164-1183, and another issued at the siege of 
Acre (no. 18 and note). Robert de Hessle gave 2 bovates in South 
Ferriby to the church of Lincoln, which were confirmed by 
Nicholas de Stuteville I (no. 53 and note). In the period 1206-1208 
he gave to Thornholme priory a knight’s fee in Tetford, co. Lincoln, 
for a yearly rent of 100s., which the priory shortly afterwards 
quitclaimed for land in South Ferriby ; and he granted Robert 
Angevin a rent from 14 bovates in Hessle in exchange for land in 
Tetford and [South] Ormesby.’ In 1212 he held a knight’s fee in

The gift was confirmed by Nicholas de Stuteville, son of Nicholas de 
Stuteville (no. 1065); and Agnes, the donor’s widow, sold to the priory her 
right of dower in the half-carucate (no. 1066). Richard Duket occurs as 
rector of Hessle in the period c. 1190-1203 (no. 21 above). The third witness 
occurs as Geoffrey ‘filius Saxelini’ in Chartulary, nos. 1078, 1080 etc. 

Tranby is not mentioned by name in the Domesday survey, but land 
there may have formed part of the 3 carucates in ‘Chrachetorp’, a manor 
held by Gilbert Tison.! It lies in par. Hessle ; and this charter shows that 
the Stutevilles held an interest, though that may have been separate from 
the holding of the Hessle family in Hessle itself. Some of the interests in 
Tranby appear to have passed to one of the Hessle heirs, however, by the 
marriage of Roese de Hessle with Henry de Tranby. In 1284-85 an under- 
tenancy was held by Watton priory through a gift made by Adam de Tranby, 
and other tenancies are recorded, all being members of the Mowbray 
tenancy-in-chief.? 


SiS. “THE ETTON PRE 


In 1282, among the fees pertaining to the manor of Cotting- 
ham, half a knight’s fee was held in Etton by Henry de Eton? 

At the Domesday survey, in addition to manors which 
formed portions of the tenancies-in-chief of the archbishop of 
York and the count of Mortain, Hugh son of Baldric held two 
manors of 9 carucates 2 bovates in Etton and a further manor of a 
earucate in lorp’ jim Etton).* 

It appears that with this half knight’s fee there had been 
associated another half knight’s fee which consisted of 8 carucates 
in “Skerne. and 2Z carucates in Hutton Cranswick, held by 
~ Thomas son of Thomas de Etton of Nicholas de Stuteville I, 
and the lordship of which the latter gave to Meaux abbey.’ For 
this reason, presumably, it was omitted from the lst of holdings 
by knight-service in the inquisition of 1282. 

The family of Etton is the subject of a detailed account by 
Mr. John Bilson in his paper on Gilling Castle.6 He shows that 
they appear in Gilling in Ryedale in the second half of the twelfth 
century, and early in the thirteenth century divide into two 
distinct lines, the Ettons of Etton and the Ettons of Gilling. The 
former held a tenancy under the Stutevilles and Wakes, and 
their eventual heiress married Patrick de Langdale in the four- 
teenth century. According to the account of the family in the 
Meaux Chronicle,’ used by Mr. Bilson, Hugelin de Etton acquired

mentioning the dower of Maud his mother, quitclaimed to the 
abbot of Meaux lands in Skerne and Hutton Cranswick.® Robert 
de Etton, who was living in 1261 (see footnote below), may have 
been the father of Henry de. Etton, the tenant im 12625 but-or 
this no proof has been found.”

a charter in the period 1154-58.

so) ) DHE HONOURS OF LIDDEL-AND ROSEDALE 


In 1212 it was recorded that Nicholas de Stuteville [1] 
held his land in Cumberland of the king by cornage, rendering 
yearly a cornage rent of 56s., and that Ranulf earl of Chester, 
sometime lord of Cumberland, gave the said land to Turgisius 
Brundos, ‘antecessor’ of the said Nicholas by the said service.® 
This land can be identified as the basis of the honour of Liddel,* 
the members of which are given in detail in the inquisition held 
after the death of his granddaughter Joan de Stuteville (mother 
of Baldwin Wake) in 1276; the barony and forest of Liddel, with 
lands in Arthuret, Brackenhill and elsewhere, being held of the 
king in chief by the service of 56s. cornage at the king’s ex- 
chequer at Carlisle and suit at the county court of Cumberland.é 

The reference to the gift made by Ranulf earl of Chester 
shows that the date was not later than 1121, for he appears to 
have surrendered the lordship of Cumberland on becoming earl 
of Chester.6 It is likely that Turgisius Brundos’ was dead at 
Michaelmas 1130, when a certain William Brundos, probably his 
son, paid 2 marks of silver in Yorkshire imposed by the justices 
at Blyth.8 This William can probably be identified with William 
de Rosedale, who gave the land of Kershope, Cumberland, to St. 
Peter’s hospital, York (no. 108), and was a benefactor of Rosedale 
priory (no. 111). His gift to the hospital with Godeheuld his wife 
was made not later than 1157 (no. 109). He was the father of 
Turgis de Rosedale who confirmed his gift to Rosedale priory

(no. 111), and was a consenting party to his gift to the hospital

This younger Turgis, as Turg’ de Russedal’, was among the 
witnesses to a charter of king Henry II at Newcastle upon Tyne 
in 1158 ;} and, as Turgisius de Russendala, to king Malcolm IV’s 
confirmation charter to St. Peter’s hospital, York (no. 109). At 
Michaelmas 1165 the sheriff of Yorkshire accounted for a mark 
paid by Turgis de Russedale.? Described as Turgis de Russedale, 
sometime lord of the manor of Liddel, he is recorded as the donor 
of the church of Arthuret to Jedburgh abbey, to which it was 
afterwards appropriated by the consent of Bernard bishop of 
Carlisle? To the same house he gave the house of religion of 
Liddel [Canobie priory] with the land adjacent and the church 
of Kirkandrews-on-Esk (no. 110). 


Guy de Rossedale, who with the consent of Ralph his son 
gave land near Liddel to Jedburgh abbey not later than 1174 
(no. 110), was evidently of the same family. He can perhaps be 
identified with Guy de Rusedala who witnessed a charter of 
William de Roumare, earl of Lincoln, relating to Little Steeping 
and Halton Holegate, co. Lincoln, 1142-51 ;4 with Guy de Russe- 
dale who with Fulk de Alneto held a knight’s fee of the new 
feoffment of Walter de Aincourt, as recorded in the latter’s carta 
returned from Lincolnshire in 1166 ;°? and with Guy de Russendale 
who owed 5 marks for his wife’s maritagium in Lincolnshire in 
1158.6 A Robert de Russedale witnessed a charter of Robert de 
Stuteville III, c. 1170-1183 (no. 16). 


After the acquisition of the honour of Liddel by Nicholas 
de Stuteville I its history is reasonably clear.? In 1214 dower was 
claimed therein by Sibyl de Valognes, widow of Robert de Stute- 
ville, elder son of Nicholas; it was assigned to Robert’s son 
Eustace in 1219 until his full age by his uncle Nicholas II ; it was 
apparently held by Eustace until his death in 1241, when it 
passed to his first cousin Joan de Stuteville, wife of Hugh Wake ; 
and it was subsequently held by her descendants the Wakes, 
lords of Liddel. 


From an early date in the twelfth century there appears to 
have been a close connexion between the honour of Liddel and 
the lands in North Yorkshire in the wapentake of Pickering Lythe 
which became known as the honour of Rosedale. This consisted 
of land in Middleton, a mile to the north-west of Pickering, in- 
cluding Cropton, 3 miles further north-west, and the vale of the 
river Seven which further north is known as Rosedale where the

priory was founded! Robert son of Nicholas de Stuteville I 
held a knight’s fee in Middleton during his father’s lifetime ; his 
son Eustace was in possession in 1230 ; and in 1214 Robert’s widow 
had claimed dower in the honour of Rosedale and in the vills of 
Middleton and Cropton.? 

The statement that the house of Cistercian nuns at Rosedale 
was founded by Robert son of Nicholas de Stuteville in the reign 
of Richard I® requires reconsideration. At first sight the con- 
firmation charter of king John, confirmed by Edward III (no. 55), 
suggests that Robert was the founder. But another and earlier 
confirmation by king John (no. 111) refers to a gift made to the 
nuns by William de Rosedale and confirmed by Turgis his son, 
and also to a charter of Henry II. The notes given above relating 
to William and his son Turgis, and their gift of land in the honour 
of Liddel to St. Peter’s hospital (nos. 108-9), show that Rosedale 
priory must have been founded at least as early as the opening 
years of the reign of Henry II. It seems likely that William de 
Rosedale was the actual founder.4 Several benefactions from the 
time of Robert de Stuteville and his father Nicholas are given in 
the note to charter no. 55: and the text of a charter of Eustace 
son of Robert de Stuteville at no. 56.

CHAPTER V. 


CHARTERS RELATING TO THE SUB-TENURE OF 
THE HONOUR OF MOWBRAY 


The charters in the present chapter relate for the most part 
to certain lands in Yorkshire held by Hugh son of Baldric at the 
Domesday survey, which passed to Robert de Stuteville I and 
then after his forfeiture in 1106 to Nigel d’Aubigny, and of which 
his son Roger de Mowbray enfeoffed Robert de Stuteville III in 
part satisfaction of the latter’s claims, thus forming a portion of 
his holding of Roger de Mowbray in 1166.1 The lands to which 
these charters relate are mainly the manors of Kirkby Moorside 
and Kilburn and their dependencies. They do not include such 
lands as Thirsk, Coxwold and Hovingham, which had become 
members of the honour of Mowbray similarly, but in which Robert 
de Stuteville III never recovered an interest. Charters relating 
thereto would be included more appropriately in a volume dealing 
with the honour of Mowbray and the tenants of that honour 
other than the Stutevilles.. The charters printed here, however, 
include several issued by Roger de Mowbray both before and 
after the enfeoffment of Robert de Stuteville III took place, 
the date of which can be fixed in the period 1154-66 (see 
p. 5 above). 

Nigel d’Aubigny was a younger son of Roger d’Aubigny 
and a younger brother of William d’Aubigny the butler.2,~ He was 
granted Montbrai and other lands in Normandy forfeited by 
Robert de Mowbray, earl of Northumberland. In June 1118 he 
married as his second wife Gundreda daughter of Gerard de 
Gournay by Edith daughter of William de Warenne, first earl of 
Surrey, and his wife Gundreda, from whom she evidently derived 
her name? Nigel d’Aubigny died in November 1129. - The interest 
taken by Gundreda his widow and Roger his son in abbot Gerold 
and his monks from Calder led to the foundation of Byland abbey.4 
Gundreda is said to have been still living in 1155.5 

Roger de Mowbray, Nigel’s son and heir by his wife Gundreda, 
was still young (adhuc puerulus) when he fought at the battle of 
the Standard in 1138. In 1147 he took part in the second crusade. 
On 17 April: 1153 he-execured a. charter at, York’. -In digathe 
supported the younger Henry against the king, but his castles, of 
which Thirsk was one, were surrendered in 1174, and he made

his peace with the king. In 1186 he was serving in the third 
crusade and in 1188 he died either in Palestine or on the return 
journey. He married Alice daughter of Walter de Gant and 
widow of Ilbert de Lascy II, who had died in or about 1141.! 

Roger de Mowbray had issue two sons, Nigel his heir, and 
Robert. Nigel de Mowbray was at the Council of Clarendon in 
January 1163-4. He accompanied king Richard on crusade, and 
died at Acre in 1191. William de Mowbray, his son and heir, made 
the agreement with William de Stuteville in 1201, by which the 
long-standing claims of the Stuteville family were settled. He 
was one of the Magna Carta barons, and died in or before March 
1223-4. His issue continued in the male line until 1476.

1 That is the date given in Complete Peerage, new ed., vii, 677 ; Farrer

202 STUTEVILLE FEE 


If it can be rightly deduced from this charter that all the churches 
specified were given to Newburgh priory in the time of William de Sainte- 
Barbe, dean of York, who became bishop of Durham in 1143, or in the 
time of archbishop William, then the latest date for the gift of any one of 
them was 1154. The evidence of the history of the foundation of Byland 
abbey shows that the patronage of the churches of Thirsk, Hovingham and 
Kirkby Moorside, offered by Roger de Mowbray to abbot Roger, was refused, 
and that it was given to the canons apparently before their move from Hood 
to Newburgh.1 The evidence of charter no. 163 below shows that Roger 
de Mowbray gave the church:of Welburn {7.e. Kirkdale| to Newburgh 
priory while Augustine was still prior. 

The first seven witnesses and the eleventh (John Lumbard) to the 
present confirmation witnessed a charter to the chapter of York, almost 
certainly issued in 1201 ;? while the first seven with the ninth and tenth 
and the last but one witnessed a charter to the canons, evidently of the 
same date.’ 

There are two general charters of Roger de Mowbray to Newburgh 
priory which are available in print; and they both give rise to certain 
difficulties. The first was printed in the Monasticon, and said to be from 
the original penes Thomas, Lord Fauconberg in 1638.4 It includes all the 
churches mentioned in the present confirmation of the dean and chapter, 
together with several chapels given by name and lands in many places. 
The difficulty lies in the witness clause which cannot possibly belong to it ; 
and indeed the witnesses precisely correspond with those in a general con- 
firmation issued by William de Mowbray,’ who in view of the specified 
relationships of members of the Mowbray family among the witnesses, 
can be identified as the grandson of Roger de Mowbray. This tends to 
throw doubt on the statement that it was taken from a genuine original,® 
though there is no reason to doubt that Roger de Mowbray duly made or 
confirmed the gifts recorded. 

The second was the subject of an inspeximus and confirmation in 
1389.7 A clause suggests that it was issued as a foundation charter, but 
actually it was addressed to prior Richard, the second prior. Farrer’s note 
to his transcript of it states that 1t bears some evidence of having passed 
through the hands of an expert editor in the scriptorium at Newburgh ; 
and he marks it as of doubtful authenticity.

This charter and the next were doubtless issued at much the same time 
as no. 115, and certainly before 5 Feb. 1139-40 when archbishop Thurstan 
died. The second witness can probably be identified as Ralph Nowel, 
bishop of Orkney. The third was William de Sainte-Barbe, dean of York ; 
the fourth was Geoffrey Turcople, archdeacon of Nottingham at this 
period ;* and the fifth was Bertram Haget who witnessed a charter to Roger 
de Mowbray not later than 1145,° and one of Roger de Mowbray’s wife in 
1154.°

206 STUTEVILLE FEE 


The latest date for this charter is 20 June 1143, when William de 
Sainte-Barbe, dean of York, was consecrated bishop of Durham. It was 
issued after the departure of the monks under abbot Roger, who had suc- 
ceeded abbot Gerold, from Hood, where they had remained for four years, 
to Old Byland ; and the earliest date is 1142.! The charter therefore shows 
that the canons from Bridlington were established at Hood within those 
limits of date.2. They subsequently migrated to Newburgh, retaining Hood 
as a cell until the Dissolution. They retained no permanent tenure of the 
church of St. Andrew, Fishergate ; for before the end of the century it was 
in the possession of Hugh Murdac, who founded there a priory of the order 
of Sempringham.? It seems likely that this was the church of St. Andrew 
mentioned in the Domesday survey as in the tenure of Hugh son of Baldric ;4 
for it is reasonable to suppose that its possession by Roger de Mowbray was 
due to the grant made to his father of a large portion of the forfeited holdings 
of Robert de Stuteville [, held by Hugh son of Baldric in 1086. 

It is clear that Gundreda, Roger de Mowbray’s mother, was alive when 
this charter was issued (see no. 150); and it therefore gives an example of 
the danger of assuming that those mentioned in a ‘pro anima’ clause, as 
contrasted with ‘pro salute’, were necessarily dead. 


119. Gift by Roger de Mowbray to Byland abbey of 20s. in his mill 
of Coxwold in exchange for Hood, which at his petition the 
monks had demised to the canons who had come from Bridlington 
to build their cell. [1142-c. 1143] 

MS. Dodsworth xciv, f. 77V. 


Ebor[acensi] archiepiscopo etc. Rogerus de Mowbray salutem. 
Sciatis me dedisse Deo et monachis sancte Marie de Bellalanda viginti 
solidos in molendino meo de Cokewald in escambio pro Hod quod 
peticione mea dimiserunt canonicis qui venerunt de Brellingtona ad 
construendum cenobium suum, etc. 

It will be seen from no. 118 that the canons had arrived at Hood not 
later than 1143 ; and it can be supposed that this charter and the next were 
issued shortly after the provision of Old Byland for abbot Roger and his 
monks. The date 1145, given as the foundation of Newburgh priory,° is 
probably the date when the new site near Coxwold was provided by Roger 
de Mowbray or when the canons moved there from Hood. Augustine, the 
first prior, ruled the house both before and after the migration. The next 
charter shows that the 20s. rent was given by Sampson d’Aubigny ; and it 
must be suggested that the present charter was actually a confirmation 
of this.

master of St. Michael’s hospital, Whitby, by Gundreda wife of

Welburn] with a toft; which were [afterwards] held of the

illius loci in elemosinam perpetuam pro anima viri sui et pro Rogero 
de Moubray filio eorum et pro seipsa duas bovatas terre in Houeton’ 
cum uno tofto quas monachi Rievall[enses] tenent de predicto hospitali 
reddentes ipsi hospitali annuatim sex solidos ad Pentecosten. Con- 
cessit vero et promisit bone memorie Ailredus abbas Rieuallis et 
conventus ejusdem loci quando! receperunt predictas duas bovatas 
terre cum uno tofto, tenendas eas de predicto hospitali, adjuvare et 
succurrere predicto hospitali annuatim et fratribus eyusdem loci in 
necessitatibus eorum, scilicet de veteribus vestimentis et aliis addita- 
mentis que dari voluerint ad festum sancti Martini in elemosinam ... . 


This record is taken from an account of the foundation and early history 
of St. Michael’s hospital for lepers at Spital Bridge, which was founded in the 
time of William {de Percy], abbot of Whitby ; and it shows the close associa- 
tion of the hospital with Whitby abbey. In the other Whitby Chartulary? 
there is another record giving additional details. In this it is stated that 
the foundation was made by abbot William de Percy in 1109, and that Robert 
de Alneto, master of the hospital, sought from the said abbot 2 bovates of 
land in Hoveton with a toft which Gundreda gave to Whitby abbey—a 
slight variation from the text printed above ; and it contains the clause 
‘Et quia abbas et conventus monasterii Riewallis erant domini et possessores 
prefate ville de Houeton ideo optabant tenere de abbate et conventu de 
Whitby prefatas illas duas bovatas terre cum tofto pro sex solidis annuatim 
solvendis monasterio de Whitby’. 

It can be deduced from these records that Robert d’Aunay, a monk 
of Whitby, held the mastership of the hospital before he became a hermit 
at Hood, where he was established in 1138 (no. 115). Consequently the 
date of Gundreda’s gift was made in the period between 1130 (after her hus- 
band’s death in 1129) and 1138. It can also be deduced that Rievaulx 
abbey acquired the tenancy of the 2 bovates in the time of Ailred, who 
became abbot in 1147. In pope Alexander III’s confirmation to Rievaulx 
dated in 1160 they are described as ‘ex dono fratrum hospitalis de Witebi 
duas bovatas in Houetuna’.*

No other reference to the grantor appears to be known ; but, although 
the amount of land and rent does not correspond, the evidence of no. 123 
suggests that Rievaulx abbey did not become possessed of the Whitby 
bovates before 1147 ; and it is possible that the acquisition was due to the 
eift to Rievaulx of what is described as the whole of Hoveton made by 
Ralph Beler in 1154-57 (no. 128). The limits of 1147-57 are therefore 
suggested tentatively. The assent of Robert d’Aunay was probably due to 
his having been the original donee, as master of the hospital, of two of the 
bovates. Nothing definite is known of him after he joined abbot Gerold’s 
monks at Hood in 1138 (see no. 115).

Hoveton is a lost vill which lay between Welburn and Kirkby Moorside. 
The first element of the name is probably the same as that in Hovingham.! 
This may perhaps be preserved in Howkeld Head which is marked on the 
6 in. map on the main road between Kirkby Moorside and Tilehouse bridge, 
and in Howlands lying to the east of Howkeld Head. The eastern and 
southern bounds of Hoveton are given in detail in the present charter, and 
in even greater detail and with greater clarity in the confirmation charter 
of Robert de Stuteville III (no. 10), where it is seen that, although in the 
main Hoveton lay between the river Redover (now Hodge Beck) and Kirkby 
Moorside, a portion lay on the other side of the river near Kirkdale church. 
In his note to this charter Farrer considered that the eastern boundary 
extended about as far as the road from Welburn north to Fadmoor. 

In the list of benefactions to Rievaulx abbey Hoveton, as of the gift 
of Ralph Beler, is entered as the second entry after the gift of Welburn 
made by Roger de Mowbray in 1154, and next before the entry of a gift made 
by king Henry II in 1158.2, Roger de Mowbray’s gift of all Hoveton is 
included in Henry II’s confirmation,’ which, as it was witnessed by Eustace 
son of John who was slain in 1157, was issued not later than that year. 
It is clear that Ralph Beler was the original donor (see no. 127), although in. 
Roger de Mowbray’s charter the rent of 2 marks was to be paid to Robert 
Beler, who had presumably succeeded him. 

Nigel de Mowbray issued a charter confirming his father’s gift, specifying 
the boundary on the east in the same words‘ ‘et similiter per omnes divisas 
quas melius et plenius habuit tempore Henrici regis’; and it contains the 
clauses ‘Ipse etiam Robertus Beler me presente et annuente hanc pre- 
dictam terram similiter ut ego liberam et quietam in perpetuam elemosinam 
voluntate spontanea predictis monachis dedit et concessit excepto quod 
duas marcas argenti Robertus et heredes sui a predictis monachis annuatim 
recipient. Ego quidem et heredes mei totum servicium quod Robertus et 
heredes sui pro eadem terra facere debebant Deo et ecclesie Rieuallfis] 
quietum in perpetuum clamamus’. His testibus, Roberto capellano, Rogero 
de Flameuilla, Roberto de Daiuilla, Hugone Malebisse, Roberto de Beluair, 

Roberto de Busci, Roberto Beler, Willelmo de Tikehil, Baldewino clerico.5

The evidence given in the note to no. 129 will show that this charter 
was issued in Ranulf de Glanville’s first term of office as sheriff, that is to say 
in the period from Michaelmas 1163 to Easter 1170. The latest date is 1169; 
for Roger de Flammavville, one of the witnesses, who was the second husband 
of Juetta de Arches, widow since 1143 of Adam de Brus I, died in 1168 or in 
the following year.1 There can be little doubt that Ralph de Belueer, Roger 
de Mowbray’s steward, took his name from Belvoir.22 Hamund or Hamo 
Beler was a brother of Ralph Beler, the original donor of Hoveton ;? and 
Robert Beler was the latter’s successor (no. 125 note). Seven of the witnesses 
occur in the list of tenants in Roger de Mowbray’s carta of 1166.4 

Nigel de Mowbray issued a charter confirming the 4 bovates in much 
the same terms, adding in his confirmation of the remainder of the vill 
‘nisi quod Roberto Beler tantum duas marcas argenti pro omni servicio 
terreno persolvent’. His testibus, Rogero abbate de Bellelanda, Waltero 
priore ejusdem loci, Landrico cellarario, Roberto capellano, Roberto de 
Daiuilla, Rogero de Flamauill’, Hugone Malebissa, Radulfo [de] Belueer, 
Roberto fratre suo, Roberto de Busci, Balduino clerico.> As at least seven 
of these witnesses occur in his father’s charter the two charters were evidently 
issued on the same occasion ; cf. also the notes to nos. 125, 151.

There is nothing in Roger de Mowbray’s charter (no. 125) to suggest 
that it was issued in the archbishop’s presence; and it may be that he 
issued another shortly afterwards. It is, however, unlikely that any great 
interval elapsed before the archbishop issued this confirmation. In any 
case 1159 can be assigned as the latest limit as John son of Letold, a canon 
of York, had probably become an archdeacon by then.! The latest possible 
date is 1164 in the absence of a legatine style.

The agreement between Ralph Beler and the abbey was made not 
later than 1157, for in Roger de Mowbray’s charter (no. 125) the rent of 
2 marks was to be paid to Robert Beler ; and this notification was probably 
issued before Robert had succeeded. The dean was therefore Robert de 
Gant and not Robert Butevilain.3

The affidation acknowledged by Samson and his wife in the chapter 
of St. Peter’s is mentioned in Roger de Mowbray’s charter (no. 126) as 
having been made subsequently to their affidation in the hand of Ranulf 
[de Glanville] the sheriff. The earliest date is therefore 1163. The latest 
possible date is 1177, when Robert master of the schools was drowned ;% 
but as it is probable that William [d’Eu] was succeeded by Hamo as precentor 
of York in the period c. 1170-1174,* it can be deduced that this notification 
was issued before Ranulf de Glanville began his second term of office as 
sheriff which was not earlier than Michaelmas 1175, and that therefore the 
latest date is the end of his first term of office at Easter 1170. This latest 
date can be put back to 1169, as the notification was clearly issued earlier 
than Roger de Mowbray’s charter (no. 126).

In his note on this charter Farrer deduced, as he had suggested else- 
where, that John son of Letold and Ralph d’Aunay were joint-archdeacons 
of York, i.e. of the West Riding. It is difficult to accept this view in the 
absence of more precise evidence ; and it is likely that each of them held a 
separate archdeaconry. John son of Letold was certainly archdeacon of

Cleveland in the period 1162-67 ; and it can be suggested that this charter 
was issued when he had become archdeacon of Nottingham, being succeeded

archdeacon of York not later than 1174.? 

Robert son of William the sub-archdeacon can presumably be identified

occurs in a document relating to the church of Kirklevington (in Cleveland) 
in the period 1174-80.* 

The grantor of this charter occurs as Engelram the dean as the first 
witness to a charter of Adam de Boltby to Rievaulx abbey, 1154-60 (no. 90). 
It is likely, although no proof is available, that he can be identified with 
Hingelram the priest of Welburn who witnessed no. 124 of date ?1147-57.

(vi) HUTTON LE HOLE 


133. Gift by Hugh son of Baldric to abbot Stephen and the abbey 
of St. Mary, York, of 4 carucates of land in Kirby Misperton, 

3 carucates in Normanby, 8 carucates in Hutton [le Hole], with 

appurtenances from the river Dove to the boundary between 

Spaunton and Hutton and on the moor to the boundary between 

that land and Cleveland. [c. 1085-1093] 

MS. Dodsworth vii, f. 157V, from the original formerly in St. Mary’s 
Tower, York. Pd. in Mon. Ang., iii, 550, from the same. 

Ego Hugo filius Baldrici omnibus fidelibus tam presentibus quam 
futuris salutem. Sciatis me dedisse et concessisse Deo et abbacie 
sancte Marie Eboraci et Stephano abbati et monachis ibidem Deo 
servientibus in puram et perpetuam elemosinam 1i1i).°7 carrucatas terre 
in Kirchebi Mispertun et iij. carrucatas in Normanebi in Hotun viij.t¢ 
carrucatas terre in bosco et in plano in moris et in pascuis in stagnis 
et in aquis et in omnibus pertinentiis suis ab aqua Duue? usque ad 
divisam que est inter Spantun® et Hotun et in mora usque ad divisam 
qua dividitur eadem terra a Cliflandia. Hanc elemosinam dedi ut sit 
libera et quieta ab omni terreno servitio pro anima domini mei regis 
Willelmi et pro memetipso et pro animabus omnium parentum meorum. 
Testibus hiis, Berengario de Thoneia, Willelmo filio Tirri, Rodberto 
capellano, Ricardo milite, et aliis multis. 


Land in the three places named formed berewicks of Hugh son of 
Baldric’s manor of Kirkby Moorside at the Domesday survey. In Normanby 
3 carucates were also terra regis ; and in the Summary, where Hugh son of 
Baldric’s land there is not recorded, it is stated that the king held 3 carucates 
and in the same place the abbot [of St. Mary’s] held 3 carucates of the king. 
William I’s gift of 3 carucates in Normanby was included in William II’s 
confirmation charter not later than 1093, where the gifts of Hugh son of 
Baldric, also included, correspond with those which he made by the present 
charter, except that the amount of land in Kirby Misperton is given as 
3 instead of 4 carucates.* It is uncertain whether his charter was issued 
before or after the death of the Conqueror. 

Of the witnesses the first was Berenger de Toeni, the Domesday tenant- 
in-chief ; and the second, who as William the knight held Wetherby of

134. Gift by Geoffrey abbot of St. Mary’s, York, to Gospatric son 
of Waltheof and his heirs of a carucate of land in Hutton [le 
Hole], rendering 2s. yearly and for tallage as much as would be 
rendered by a third of a carucate in Kirby Misperton; the 
grantees to keep no woodland mares nor a sheepfold for more 
than 60 sheep. [c. 1121-c. 1137] 


Chartulary of St. Mary’s, York (Dean and Chapter, larger volume), 
f. 159¥ (old f. 66V), no. 19. 


Notum sit omnibus posteris nostris et omnibus videntibus vel 
audientibus literas has quod ego Gosfridus abbas ecclesie sancte Marie 
Ebor[acensis] cum communi assensu capituli nostri dedi Gospatricio 
filio Walthef’ et heredibus suis in feodo unam carrucatam terre in 
Hoton, et ipsi reddent inde unoquoque anno duos solidos ad Pentecosten 
et festum sancti Martini; et quando commune taillagium mittemus 
super terram nostram reddet illa carrucata quant[um] tertia pars 
unius carucate in Chirchaby Mispeton’. Et sciendum quod non 
habebunt ibi equas silvestres nec habebunt ovile nisi ad plus usque 
ad lx. oves. Testibus, Rogero presbitero, Daniele dapifero, Willelmo 
dispensatore, Nicholao de Miton, Roberto filio Haschwi. 


In view of the next charter it is clear that Gospatric son of Waltheot 
and Gospatric de Brawby were the same person. Brawby, par. Salton in Rye- 
dale wapentake, was attached to the prebend of Salton, which became a 
tenure of the prior of Hexham by the gift of archbishop Thurstan.? Gos- 
patric was the ancestor of a family known by the names of Brawby, Ridale 
and Habton ; and by reason of a grant of lands in Roxburghshire by king 
David I to Walter, Gospatric’s eldest son, he was the ancestor of the 
Riddells of Scotland.s Asketil or Ansketil, Gospatric’s second son, who 
succeeded his brother Walter, held a knight’s fee of the Bulmer fee in 
1166.+ He had held of Bertram de Bulmer the carucate in Welburn which 
Bertram gave to Rievaulx abbey.’ This was presumably the carucate which 
Asceria widow of Ansketil de Habton held in dower and confirmed to the 
abbey.® 

The dates when this charter and the next were issued fall in the period 
during which Geoffrey was abbot of St. Mary’s, though the dates of his 
. accession and death are uncertain.’

Seal : yellow wax, round, 24 n.; equestrian, sword, heater-shaped

DE MOLBRAI. 


This charter was presumably issued later than the departure of Roger 
de Mowbray for Palestine, where he arrived about Easter 1186 ; the grantor 
died on crusade in 1191; see the introductory notes to this Chapter. 

As Robert de Daiville had held Kilburn of Roger de Mowbray (no. 17), 
it can be supposed that the land restored to his son included the holding 
there. The evidence of nos. 140-1 shows that Robert also had an interest 
in Nawton, par. Kirkdale. It was, perhaps, an earlier Robert de Daiville 
who occurs as lord of Kilburn in 1147 (note to no. 138). It is recorded that 
Nigel d’Aubigny who died in 1129, had given to his special friend, Robert 

- Davil or de Ayvile by name, the vill of Egmanton, co. Nottingham, which 
he had received by the gift of Henry 1.2, An account of the Daiville family 
is given in The Complete Peerage, new ed., iv, 130. 

No account of the history of Kilburn is included in V.C.H., North 
Riding, the parish and that of Felixkirk both being omitted. The temporary 
interest of Robert de Stuteville III, as an under-tenancy of Mowbray, is 
seen in charter no. 17. A reference in the Monasticon (vol. vi, 1118) states 
that the manor of Kilburn had been given by Henry I to the archbishop and

chapter of Rouen, the authority cited being Close Roll 7 John, m. 1d. This 
invites suspicion, and it is clear that the manor was not Kilburn, but Kilham.!

William de Cornbrough, Gikel de Smeaton and Geoffrey Fossard 
(who witnessed the next charter) occur together in 1207 and in the period 
1204-09.3 The first of these occurs in the period 1175-89.4 The date for 
this charter and the next is probably towards the end of the period suggested. 

Several years earlier there had been disputes with the monks. In 
abbot Philip’s history of the house it is recorded that Robert de Davidvilla, 
lord of Kilburn, obstructed the work of the monks, alleging that they were 
enclosing a great part of the ground belonging to his vill of Kilburn ; in 
1147 abbot Roger crossed to Normandy to complain to Roger de Mowbray 
of this and other wrongs, taking letters from dame Gundreda with the 
news of the plea moved in the king’s court by Robert de Stuteville and 
William his brother against her son. Roger de Mowbray wrote to Robert de 
Davidvilla, Gwido de Boltby and Hugh Malbisse, lord of Scawton, ordering 
them to desist from their oppressions. Robert de Davidvilla paid small 
attention at first, but afterwards made peace ; and on his return Roger de 
Mowbray made a great ditch on the west side of the abbey (then at Stocking) 
between the ground given to the monks and the ground of Robert de 
Davidvilla.> It was evidently this ditch, below Middlebergh, which is 
mentioned in the present charter.

1 Sic, query for apponet. 
2 Marginal number lix. 


CHARTERS RELATING TO THE MOWBRAY TENANCY wat 


perpetuam elemosinam scilicet in prato de Rochesberc xviij. perticatas 
in latitudine et lx. in longitudine, et duas acras prati de feudo Edestun 
juxta divisas de Edestun et de Rochesberc. His testibus, Roberto

In the dated register of gifts recorded in the Chartulary! the gifts by 
Gervase and Benedict his son? and Hugh del Tuit of the meadow of Rokes- 
bergh is entered between 1145 and 1152. These gifts are included in pope 
Alexander III’s confirmation of 1160.3 

The grantor’s gift in Rook Barugh was confirmed by Robert de Stute- 
ville III (no. 11). In 1166 he held a knight’s fee of Hugh Bigod, earl of 
Norfolk, part of which lay in Great Edston ; some details of his family have 
been given by Farrer in a note to the record of his gift to Keldholme priory 
of the mill of Edston.+ The theory, however, that the family took its name 
from Thwaite by Aylsham, Norfolk, cannot be accepted ; and the late Mr. 
L. C. Loyd has given evidence that the name was probably, if not certainly, 
derived from Le Thuit, dept. Eure, arr. and cant. Les Andelys.5 The form 
‘del Tuhit’ will be noticed in the present charter.

As recorded in the note to no. 143 the grantor and his father made the 
gift of the meadow of Rook Barugh between 1145 and 1152. It is likely 
that the grantor’s father had died before the additional gifts. were made by 
the present charter, which, however, in view of the occurrence of Walter 
Espec as the first witness cannot be later than 1152. The grantor may be 
Benedict de Wimbeltun who witnessed no. 149. 

At Skiplam, in the northern portion of Welburn, Gundreda mother of 
Roger de Mowbray made a gift of cultivated land to the abbey (no. 150). 
The abbey’s grange there is mentioned in another charter (no. 148). Womble- 
ton lies to the s.w. of Welburn village, and Muscoates further south ; a grange 
is marked on the 1 in. map close to both places. Rook Barugh lies some 
distance to the east, beyond the river Dove in the parish of Normanby. 
Wiresdale occurs as Wiredale in a charter relating to Nawton (no. 141), which 
is to the n.w. of Wombleton.

Charters relating to the prolonged disputes between Alan de Ryedale, 
whose wife is here seen to be a sister and presumably heir of Benedict son

Welburn (nos. 157-60). Engelram occurs as rural dean of Ryedale in the 

' periods 1154-60 and c. 1160-74 (nos. 90, 131); and, if it can be supposed 
that Theoderic was his successor, the period of the present charter can be 
suggested. If the second witness was Robert de Ros I of Helmsley the 
latest date is 1163 when he was dead.

This gift formed the nucleus of the abbey’s possessions in Welburn, which 
was extended by the next charter and then on a more comprehensive scale 
by Roger de Mowbray’s charter (no. 151). The mention of Roger’s wife and 
sons in the next charter, and their omission here, suggests that the present 
charter was issued before his marriage, which took place between 1141 and 
1143.1 It was probably not issued very long before the next; and the 
witnesses sugg@st a date not earlier than c. 1138.7

Robert [de Gant] the dean of York and Robert { Butevilain] the archdeacon.! 
In the confirmation charter of king Henry LI, issued not later than 1157, 
Roger de Mowbray’s gift ‘quicquid est de feudo suo in Wellebruna’ was 
included ;2 and the date 1154, which is precisely given in the list of bene- 
factions, can be accepted. As Gundreda, the grantor’s mother, appears to 
have been alive in 11554 she was therefore living when the charter was issued. 
The charter thus affords another example of the doubtful deduction to be 
made from a ‘pro anima’ clause, to which allusion has been made in the 
note to no. 118, where the ‘pro anima’ clause is the same. 

The bounds described in the charter are precisely defined, and the 
land extended considerably north of the road from Kirkby Moorside to 
Helmsley as far as Middlehead (see no. 150) and the boundary of Cleveland. 
Wombleton is a mile s.w. of Welburn village; Muscoates c, 24 miles s. ; 
Bowforth (Buleford) ? mile s.e. ; North Holme 2 miles s.s.e. ; and Cowldyke 
(Coldic) is marked on the | in. map. to the east of the junction of the rivers 
Redover (now Hodge Beck) and Dove, 2 miles s.e. of Welburn village and a 
mile e. of Welburn Grange. Of the other places mentioned, Lund is repre- 
sented, perhaps, by Lund Court, a mile n.w. of Welburn village, and Lund 
Head, a little further n.w. ; Sleightholme Dale (Slectes) is marked on the 
6 in. map along the middle portion of Hodge Beck, with Sleights common 
to the north ; and further north, above Bransdale, Bloworth Slack (Blawat) 
runs north to Bloworth Crossing, just to the west of Middlehead. The bounds 
can be compared with those defined in the charter of Robert de Stuteville 
LIL (20. 10),

Ralph Beler, the third witness, who was the original donor of Hoveton, 
had been succeeded by 1157 (no. 125 note). The precedence of the first 
witness suggests that he was the grantor’s steward when the charter was 
issued, especially as it is known from the next charter that he held that 
office about this time.

The witness clause raises an important point. Mr. Baildon, on the 
authority of this charter, puts ‘Swein’ in his list of the early priors of New- 
burgh.t But Farrer, in his annotation to one of the charters in the present 
volume, was of the opinion that master Swain was certainly not prior of 
Newburgh, and that what appears to be one witness represented two different 
people.* He probably had in mind master Swain of St. Peter’s hospital, York, 
who frequently occurs in the middle of the twelfth century. 

The witnesses and the subject-matter of the present charter suggest a 
date of the same period as nos. 157-9. For the confirmation of the bounds - 
made by Robert de Daiville see no. 140.

238 STUTEVILLE FEE 


The charter is deleted in red ink in the Chartulary, and has no marginal 
number ; but there is no reason to doubt that it is a copy of a genuine original. 
Richard became bishop of Chester in 1161, and the king did not return to 
England until Jan. 1163. Robert bishop of Lincoln died 27 Dec. 1166, and 
Geoffrey earl of Essex on 21 Oct. of that year, while the king returned to 
Normandy in the preceding March. Within these limits the king was absent 
in Normandy from Feb. to May 1165, and the most likely time for this 
visit to Nottingham seems to be towards the end of 1164.! But the presence 
of Richard de Lucy at St. Omer and the mission of archbishop Roger to 
the pope in November? suggest a date earlier than the meeting of the Council 
of Northampton in October ; and a date in the summer of 1163 after the 
Welsh expedition must not be overlooked.’ 

It has been noted that in the king’s confirmation charter to the abbey 
in 1154-57 there had been included whatever was of Roger de Mowbray’s 
fee in Welburn and all Hoveton (see the notes to nos. 125, 151); and it may 
be that in view of the disputes which had arisen a more precise measure of 
royal protection was deemed to be desirable.

younger generation than William de Stonegrave,' suggests a date not earlier 
than c. 1160. In view of the following charter (no. 158) the latest date can 
be taken as 1169. 

This charter, with a date assigned as c. 1170, is mentioned by Professor 
Galbraith in his paper ‘The Death of a Champion’ as giving an example 
of a civil duel waged in a private court.? 

The location of Cowldyke (Coldic), mentioned here, is given in the 
note to no. 151 ; it hes 1} miles s.e. from Bowforth. The evidence of no. 132 
shows that Alan de Ryedale was also known as Alan de Bowforth. As Alan 
de Buleford he occurs in the wapentake of Ryedale in 1166.° 


158. Notification by Roger de Mowbray of his perambulation of the 
moor before Bowforth and of the testimony that the moor was 
common pasture for Welburn, Hoveton and Bowforth, which 
common of pasture he had given to Rievaulx abbey, forbidding

Rogerus de Molbrai omnibus sancte matris ecclesie filiis et 
omnibus hominibus suis salutem. Sciatis quod ego cum baronibus et 
probis hominibus meis et vicinis perambulavi moram que jacet ante

est et testificatum quod illa predicta mora communis pastura est inter

Wellebr[una], Willelmo clerico de Kirkebi, Rogero de Flamuill’,

The perambulation here mentioned is clearly that which is described 
in the preceding charter ; and the two charters are evidently of much the 
same date. The occurrence of Roger de Flammaville as a witness fixes the 
latest date as 1169 (note to no. 126).

The occurrence of the steward of Everard de Ros gives 1162 as the 
earliest limit of date (note to no. 132); and although the other witnesses 
give no sure guide it is unhkely that this quitclaim was much later than 
the preceding one. The grantor was a tenant in 1166 both of Roger de 
Mowbray and Robert de Gant.’ 

Another quitclaim to Rievaulx abbey within the bounds of Welburn as 
defined in the Mowbray charters was made by Thomas son of Paulinus, 
canon of York, who held land in Nawton and Wombleton ; land in these 
places had been held by the archbishop of York at the Survey, and had 
apparently been attached to the prebend of Stillington ; the charter was 
printed in the section relating to the Archbishop’s Fee by Farrer, who 
assigned the date as 1160-65.

Ernald was abbot of Rievaulx from 1189 to 1199 ;3 and Bernard became 
prior of Newburgh between 1181 and 1186 and had probably been succeeded 
by June 1199.4 Although it is uncertain whether they still held office when 
this agreement was made its date was probably not much later.

At the Survey 3 carucates in Wildon were soke of the manor of Kilburn. 
This charter was issued after the arrival of the canons at their new site at 
Newburgh, probably in 1145 (no. 119 note); and the latest possible date for 
Augustine as prior is 1157.5. Some of the witnesses occur in no. 151 of date 
1154 ; and the third witness suggests a date not much later (cf. nos. 120, 
136). Hugh Camin occurs in 1136-45, and his daughter Maud held a 
knight’s fee of Robert de Stuteville LII in 1166 (see Chapter IV, §3). William 
de Stuteville can probably be identified as a younger brother of Robert 
de Stuteville III (p. 3 above); the date is too early for him to have been 
the latter’s son.

MS. Dodsworth Ixiii, f. 73, from the Byland Chartulary, f. 234. 


Ebor[acensi] archiepiscopo et omnibus hominibus suis Francis 
et Anglis Bartholomeus Gigator salutem. Sciatis quod ego sursum 
relaxo et quietum clamo de me et meis semper monachis de Bell[alanda]

pro hoc facto imperpetuum. 


The earliest date is that of the departure of the monks from Hood to 
Old Byland, and it is unlikely that this quitclaim was made later than the 
preceding charter. In the foundation history of Byland it is recorded that 
Roger de Mowbray gave all the land of Wildon in 1140.4

167. Quitclaim by Roger de Cundy to Byland abbey of all right in 
Great and Little Wildon, made in the presence of Roger de 
Mowbray and his knights in his court of Thirsk. [1142-c. 1154] 


MS. Dodsworth Ixii, f. 72, from the Byland Chartulary, f. 233V. 


Ebor[acensi] archiepiscopo et omnibus hominibus Francis et 
Anglis Rogerus de Cundy salutem. Sciatis quod reddo sursum relaxo 
et quietum clamo de me et meis semper monachis de Bell[alanda] 
totum jus meum quod habui in Magnam Wildonam et Minorem 
Wildonam cum omnibus pertinentiis in puram et perpetuam ele- 
mosinam. Hoc quoque feci coram Rogero de Molbray domino meo et 
militibus ejus in sua curia de Thresc et testis hujus carte tota plena 
curia. Monachi vero receperunt me in fratrem domus sue et heredes 
meos pro hoc facto imperpetuum. 

This quitclaim was evidently made on the same occasion as the preceding 
one. There is a record of a gift made by Roger de Cundi to Byland abbey 
of a carucate of land in Wildon with the consent of Roger de Moubrai and 
Nigel his som’in the presence of the chapter of St. Peter's, Yorle* 

The grantor, as Roger de Cundy or Condi, witnessed charters in 1138 
and in the periods 1145-c. 1152 and 1154-57 (nos. 115, 125, 146, 153, 163); 
and as one of Roger de Mowbray’s clerks in 1153.4, He may be the Roger 
de Cunde, described as a canon of York, 1154-60. A later Roger de Cundi 
witnessed a Byland charter, 1190-1219.4 It can be regarded as certain that 
the elder Roger was a member of the Cundy or Condet family of which 
Canon Foster has given a detailed account in connexion with Thorngate castle 
outside the walls of Lincoln.> He cannot be identified, however, with the 
Roger de Condet, son of Robert de Condet and Alice his wife, who had 

_ Thorngate castle in her own right ; for that Roger was a man of considerable 
importance, holding 8 knights’ fees of the bishop of Lincoln in 1166, and, 
being under age c. 1141, lived to the reign of Richard I.

dum meorum, septemdecem acras terre in cultura que jacet ab oriente 
parte grangie sue de Wildonia. Remisi etiam et quietum clamavi de 
me et de omnibus meis totam reliquam partem predicte culture per 
divisas etiam et terminos. Et ego et heredes mei manutenebimus et 
warantizabimus predicta omnia predictis monachis contra omnes 
homines imperpetuum. Testibus, Ricardo priore de Neuburgh, 
Thoma de Coleuill, Hugone Malbys, etc.

APPENDIX. GC. 
THE EARLY PRIORS OF NEWBURGH 


AUGUSTINE. Charters of Roger de Mowbray printed above 
at nos. 118, 119 show that canons from Bridlington priory came 
to establish a cell at Hood after abbot Roger and his monks had 
left for Old Byland. The date was not earlier than 1142, and not 
later than the consecration of William de Sainte-Barbe, dean of 
York, as bishop of Durham on 20 June 1143. Augustine is named 
as the prior of the canons at Hood in no. 118. Described as prior 
of Hod he witnessed a charter of William the dean and the chapter 
of York to Pontefract priory.2 He continued as prior after the 
canons moved to their new site at Newburgh, which probably 
took place in 1145 (note to no. 119). As prior of Newburgh he 
witnessed a charter of Roger de Mowbray to Rievaulx abbey in 
1154 (no. 151);3 and one of Ernald de Percy II to Guisborough 
priory.* | . 

RICHARD. He is named as prior of Newburgh in a charter 
of Roger de Mowbray, to which the period 1154-57 can be assigned 
(no. 163). He witnessed a charter of Nigel de Mowbray, 1163-69 
(no. 151 note); one of the abbot of Rievaulx relating to Willerby 
dated in 1175 ;> and one of William de Stuteville to Guisborough 
priory, c. 1175-83 (no. 20). He also witnessed several other charters 
issued in the reign of Henry II.6 At Michaelmas 1170 Richard 
prior of Newburgh owed 3 marks ‘pro excessu curie sue’, which 
he paid in the following year ;? and as R. prior of Newburgh he 
was an arbitrator in a dispute settled later than the death of 
archbishop Roger on 26 Nov. 1181.8 The details given here show 
that the extreme limits of his tenure as prior were 1154 and 1186, 
and not less than 1157 and 1181.9 


BERNARD. There can be httle doubt that he was Bernard 
the canon of Newburgh, who sometimes with prior Richard

CHARTERS RELATING TO THE MOWBRAY TENANCY 249 


witnessed several charters,! one of which was dated in 1175.2. He 
had become prior by September 1186, when he was one of the five 
whose names were submitted by the canons of York to the king 
for filling the vacancy in the archbishopric.2 As Blernard] prior 
of Newburgh he was one of the three who wrote to pope Urban 
III about charters issued to the church of Durham, 1185-87 ;:4 
and one of the officials of the archbishopric of York to whom a 
letter from the justices in eyre was directed, 1187-88.5 He was 
appointed by pope Clement III, 1187-91, as one of the arbitrators 
in a dispute relating to the church of Ilkeston, co. Derby ;* and 
by pope Celestine III in 1196 in a dispute relating to the chapel 
of East Harlsey.? In 1199, as Bernard prior of Newburgh, he was 
an arbitrator in a dispute between the priories of Durham and 
Guisborough. As B. the prior he issued a charter to William 
de Stuteville undertaking to provide a resident chaplain in the 
chapel of Gillamoor, 1183-99 (no. 23); and as Bernard the prior 
he is mentioned in an agreement between Rievaulx abbey and 
Newburgh priory, being a contemporary of abbot Ernald (no. 164). 
He witnessed a charter of the chapter of York, 1191-94 ;° and was 
prior when the priory was given land in Hooton Pagnell.!° 

Bernard’s parentage has been elucidated by Dr. Salter in an 
article on William of Newburgh. He cited a charter,” issued in 
1189, which was witnessed by Bernard prior of Newburgh, des- 
cribed as uncle of the grantor, who was William son of William 
son of Elias ; and he showed that William son of Elias can with 
great probability be identified as William of Newburgh, the 
chronicler, having obtained by marriage c. 1160-65 certain manors 
in Oxfordshire, Buckinghamshire and Wiltshire, and having left 
his wife in 1182 or 1183 to enter Newburgh priory ; and further 
that he had two brothers—Bernard prior of Newburgh and 
William de Meisnilhermer. 

M. On 12 June 1199 a papal confirmation of the church of 
Hovingham was issued to M. the prior and the canons of New- 
burgh. 

D. In virtue of a papal appointment of arbitrators, 5 Feb. 
1200-1, in a dispute relating to the chapel of Whitley, par. 
Kellington, D. prior of Newburgh was one of those who issued the 
award on 12 Nov. 1202.14

WALTER. As prior of Newburgh he witnessed an agreement 
between Fountains abbey and Arden priory at Whitsuntide 1212 ;1 
and made a gift of land in Hooton Pagnell not later than 1214.? 
He is probably the Walter, prior, who made an agreement with 
Hugh de Howthorpe by an undated charter, possibly early in the 
thirteenth century.? 


Puitip. In virtue of a papal appointment, 20 Dec. 1224, as 
prior of Newburgh he was an arbitrator in the claim made by St. 
Peter’s hospital, York, against some of the Cistercian houses for 
the yearly render of thraves of corn; the award being issued on 
1 July 1225.4 As prior of Newburgh he was a party to fines on 
7 Jan. 1226-7 and in Jan. 1227-85
